2.
Duality property in type
A^{(1)}_{N-1}

The main result of
[
DFK18
]
is a theorem which states that the
q
-Whittaker limit of the A-type Macdonald operators and their
\tau_{+}
-translates satisfy the quantum Q-system of type
A_{N-1}^{(1)}
. To prove this, we used the explicit form of these difference operators in
x
-space. In this section we will give a different method for proving the same theorem by using Macdonald’s duality relation and the Fourier transform of these operators.

We use the notation for the roots and weights of
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{N}
as follows. Let
\{e_{1},...,e_{N}\}
be the standard basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, then the simple roots are
\{\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1};i=1,...,N-1\}
, the
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{N}
fundamental weights (including
\omega_{N}
) are
\{\omega_{i}=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{i},i=1,...,N\}
, the positive roots are
R_{+}=\{e_{i}-e_{j},1\leq i<j\leq N\}
, and
\rho=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(N-i)e_{i}
. For
u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, we use the notation
u\cdot v
for the standard scalar product. We consider
q\in{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
,
|q|<1
.

2.1.
Macdonald operators and polynomials

Let
x=(x_{1},...,x_{N})
be formal variables, and define the
q
-difference operators

{\mathcal{D}}_{a}(x;q,t)
be the
a
-th Macdonald
q
-difference operator in type
A^{(1)}_{N-1}

[
Mac95
]
:

(2.1)

{\mathcal{D}}_{a}(x;q,t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset\{1,...,N\}\\
|I|=a\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\notin I\\
i\in I\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{tx_{i}-x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\right)\prod_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i},\qquad a\in\{1,...,N\},\quad\Gamma_{i}x_{j}=q^{\delta_{ij}}x_{j}\Gamma_{i}.

These difference operators form a commuting family and preserve the space of symmetric polynomials in
x
.
The common eigenvectors are the monic symmetric Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}(x)
, where
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{N}\geq 0)
is an integer partition:

(2.2)

{\mathcal{D}}_{a}(x;q,t)P_{\lambda}(x)=t^{-{a\choose 2}}e_{a}(s)P_{\lambda}(x)\qquad(a=1,2,...,N),

where,
e_{a}(s)
is the
a
th elementary symmetric function in
s=(s_{1},...,s_{N})
, with
s_{i}=t^{N-i}q^{\lambda_{i}}
. Macdonald polynomials form a basis for the space of symmetric polynomials of
x
.

2.2.
Duality

The Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}(x)
satisfy a remarkable duality property, which is a symmetry under the interchange of the variables
x
and
s
. Under the specialization
x=t^{\rho}q^{\mu}
with
\mu
an integer partition, Macdonald showed that
[
Mac95
]

(2.3)

\frac{P_{\lambda}(t^{\rho}q^{\mu})}{P_{\lambda}(t^{\rho})}=\frac{P_{\mu}(t^{\rho}q^{\lambda})}{P_{\mu}(t^{\rho})}

for all integer partitions
\lambda,\mu
.

Remark 2.1
.

The duality property has a formal generalization to arbitrary values of
\mu,\lambda
, see the discussion on the universal function in Section
2.13
.

The denominators in Equation (
2.3
) can be obtained as specializations of an infinite product expression.
Let

(2.4)

\Delta(x)=\prod_{n\geq 1}\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}}\frac{1-q^{n}x^{-\alpha}}{1-t^{-1}q^{n}x^{-\alpha}}=\prod_{\beta\in\hat{R}_{++}}\frac{1-x^{-\beta}}{1-t^{-1}x^{-\beta}},

where the second product extends over the set
\hat{R}_{++}
of strictly positive affine roots
\beta=n\delta+\sum_{i}\beta_{i}{\alpha}_{i}
,
n\geq 1,\beta_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}
, and we use the shorthand notation
x^{\beta}=q^{-n}\prod_{i}(x_{i}/x_{i+1})^{\beta_{i}}
.
Then
[
Mac95
]
1
1

1

Our definition of the infinite product
\Delta
differs slightly from
that of Macdonald’s
\Delta^{+}
, but is better suited for taking the limit
t\to\infty
below.

(2.5)

P_{\lambda}(t^{\rho})=t^{\lambda\cdot\rho}\,\frac{\Delta(t^{\rho})}{\Delta(t^{\rho}q^{\lambda})}.

Next we use Macdonald’s evaluation homomorphism
u_{\mu}
defined on functions of
x
by
u_{\mu}(f(x))=f(q^{\mu}t^{\rho})
. It maps the generators of quantum torus
\mathbb{T}_{x}
to

u_{\mu}(x_{i})=q^{\mu_{i}}t^{N-i}
and
u_{\mu}(\Gamma_{i})=e^{\partial_{\mu_{i}}}
which acts as the translation
\mu_{i}\mapsto\mu_{i}+1
while leaving
\mu_{j},j\neq i
unchanged.
Applying
u_{\mu}
for
\mu
an integer partition on the eigenvalue equation (
2.2
), and using duality (
2.3
) gives

\displaystyle e_{a}(t^{\rho}q^{\lambda})P_{\mu}(t^{\rho}q^{\lambda})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t^{{a\choose 2}}\,P_{\mu}(t^{\rho})\,u_{\mu}\left({\mathcal{D}}_{a}(x;q,t)\right)\,P_{\mu}(t^{\rho})^{-1}\,P_{\mu}(t^{\rho}q^{\lambda})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{t^{{a\choose 2}}\,t^{\rho\cdot\mu}\,\Delta(t^{\rho}q^{\mu})^{-1}\,u_{\mu}\left({\mathcal{D}}_{a}(x;q,t)\right)\,\Delta(t^{\rho}q^{\mu})\,t^{-\rho\cdot\mu}\right\}\,P_{\mu}(t^{\rho}q^{\lambda}).

Let us finally interchange the partition labels
\lambda\leftrightarrow\mu
in the above equation, and note moreover that since
the polynomial
P_{\lambda}(x)
is entirely determined by its values at the discrete specializations
x=t^{\rho}q^{\mu}
with
\mu
taking values in integer partitions, we can conclude that
P_{\lambda}(x)
satisfies the Pieri equations:

(2.6)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{a}(\Lambda;q,t)\,P_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{a}(x)\,P_{\lambda}(x)\qquad(a=1,2,...,N),

with

(2.7)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{a}(\Lambda;q,t)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle t^{{a\choose 2}}t^{\rho\cdot\lambda}\Delta(s)^{-1}u_{\lambda}\left({\mathcal{D}}_{a}(x;q,t)\right)\Delta(s)t^{-\rho\cdot\lambda}.

The Pieri rules for the Macdonald polynomials express the multiplication of
P_{\lambda}(x)
by an elementary symmetric function
e_{a}(x)
as a linear combination
of Macdonald polynomials with shifted partitions.

Remark 2.2
.

Note that in (
2.7
) the evaluation
u_{\lambda}
maps
x_{i}\to q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{N-i}=s_{i}
and

\Gamma_{i}\to e^{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}}
. The same evaluation map sends the generators of the quantum torus
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
to respectively
{\Lambda}_{i}\to q^{\lambda_{i}}
and
T_{i}\to e^{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}}
.
By a slight abuse of notation, we simply express the operator
{\mathcal{H}}_{a}(\Lambda;q,t)
in terms of the generators
{\Lambda}_{i}
and
T_{i}

and omit the mention of the evaluation map from now on. This amounts to evaluating the left action of the quantum torus
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}

on functions of the variables
({\Lambda}_{1},...,{\Lambda}_{N})
. There is also a right action of the evaluation of
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
on the basis of Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}

in which
{\Lambda}_{i}
acts diagonally
P_{\lambda}\,{\Lambda}_{i}=P_{\lambda}\,q^{\lambda_{i}}
and
T_{i}
by a shift
P_{\lambda}\,T_{i}=P_{\lambda+e_{i}}
. We adopt a single notation using the quantum torus generators, to denote either left or right action.

Explicitly the commuting operators
H_{a}(\Lambda;q,t)

acting on functions of
\Lambda
or
s
are

(2.8)

{\mathcal{H}}_{a}(\Lambda;q,t)=\sum_{I\subset[1,N]\atop|I|=a}\prod_{i\in I,\ j\not\in I\atop j<i}\frac{t^{i-j-1}{\Lambda}_{j}-{\Lambda}_{i}}{t^{i-j}{\Lambda}_{j}-{\Lambda}_{i}}\ \frac{t^{i-j+1}{\Lambda}_{j}-q{\Lambda}_{i}}{t^{i-j}{\Lambda}_{j}-q{\Lambda}_{i}}\,\prod_{i\in I}T_{i},\qquad T_{i}\Lambda_{j}=q^{\delta_{ij}}\Lambda_{j}T_{i}.

They can be thought of as Macdonald Hamiltonians of type
A_{N-1}^{(1)}
: In the
q
-Whittaker limit, they become relativistic
q
-difference
Toda Hamiltonians (see Sect.
2.12
below).

2.3.
The
q
-Whittaker limit

By a slight abuse of languge, we call the limit
t\to\infty
of the difference operators and eigenfunctions above the
q
-Whittaker limit (as opposed to the standard
t\to 0
).
The
q
-Whittaker functions
2
2

2

This is a slight abuse of language, as these are strictly speaking
q^{-1}
-Whittaker functions.
\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
is interpreted as a (class 1)
q
-Whittaker function where
x
is the representation index, and
\lambda
the argument.
are the limits of the Macdonald polynomials
[
Che09
]
:

\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{\lambda}(x).

In this limit we use the
t
-independent variables
\Lambda_{i}=q^{\lambda_{i}}
instead of
s_{i}
. The symmetry between the eigenvalue equation and the Pieri rules under exchange of
x
and
s
is lost in this limit. However, the limit of the difference equations still makes sense.
The
q
-Whittaker functions satisfy the difference equations

(2.9)

\displaystyle D_{a}(x;q)\Pi_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}\Pi_{\lambda}(x),\qquad\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}=\Lambda_{1}\cdots\Lambda_{a};

(2.10)

\displaystyle H_{a}(\Lambda;q)\Pi_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{a}(x)\Pi_{\lambda}(x).

Here, the difference operators are

(2.11)

D_{a}(x;q)=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{a(a-N)}{\mathcal{D}}_{a}(x;q,t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset\{1,...,N\}\\
|I|=a\end{subarray}}\prod_{j\notin I\atop i\in I}\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\prod_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i},

and the Pieri operators are

(2.12)

H_{a}({\Lambda};q)=\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathcal{H}}_{a}({\Lambda};q,t)=\sum_{I\subset[1,N]\atop|I|=a}\prod_{i\in I\atop i-1\notin I}\left(1-\frac{\Lambda_{i}}{\Lambda_{i-1}}\right)\,\prod_{i\in I}T_{i}

with the convention that
\Lambda_{0}:=\infty
. Note the simplified form of these equations:
the Pieri operators are polynomials in
\Lambda^{-\alpha_{i}}
where
\alpha_{i}
are the simple roots, rather than rational functions. Moreover,
the eigenvalues of operators
D_{a}(x;q)
are monomials in
\Lambda
, because
e_{a}(s)
is replaced by its leading term in
t
,
\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}
, where
\omega_{a}
are the fundamental weights.

2.4.
Type
A^{(1)}_{N-1}
quantum Q-system

The main result of
[
DFK18
]
is that the set of all
\tau_{+}
-translations of the operators
D_{a}(x;q)
satisfy the quantum Q-system equations
3
3

3

This was referred to as the type
A_{N-1}
Q-system in e.g.
[
DFK18
]
.
of type
A^{(1)}_{N-1}
. To define this system, let
\{{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;k}:a\in\{1,...,N\},k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
be invertible elements in some non-commutative algebra over
{\mathbb{C}}(q)
, subject to two types of relations: A
q
-commutation relation

(2.13)

{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;k}{\mathcal{Q}}_{b;k+i}=q^{i\min(a,b)}{\mathcal{Q}}_{b;k+i}{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;k},\qquad k\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ a,b\in[1,N],\ |i|\leq 1,

and a cluster exchange-type relation, which can be thought of as a discrete time evolution in the variable
k
:

(2.14)

q^{a}{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;k+1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;k-1}={\mathcal{Q}}_{a;k}^{2}-{\mathcal{Q}}_{a-1;k}{\mathcal{Q}}_{a+1;k},\quad a\in[1,N],\ k\in{\mathbb{Z}},

subject to the boundary conditions
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0;k}=1,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N+1;k}=0
. The algebra generated by the set
\{{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;k}:a\in[1,N],k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
and their inverses is a subalgebra in a certain quantum cluster algebra
[
DFK11
]
.

Define the generalized
q
-Whittaker difference operators, acting on the space of functions in the variables
\{x_{1},...,x_{N}\}
with coefficients in
{\mathbb{C}}(x_{1},...,x_{N},q)
:

(2.15)

D_{a;k}(x;q)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset\{1,...,N\}\\
|I|=a\end{subarray}}\prod_{i\in I}x_{i}^{k}\prod_{j\notin I}\left(\frac{x_{i}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\right)\prod_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i},\qquad a\in[1,N],\ k\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

When
k=0
these are just the
q
-Whittaker difference operators (
2.9
).
The main result of
[
DFK18
]
is that these operators provide a functional representation of the quantum Q-system:

Theorem 2.3

(
[
DFK18
]
)
.

The
q
-difference operators
D_{a;k}(x;q)
of (
2.15
) satisfy the quantum Q-system relations (
2.13
) and (
2.14
).

The original proof of the theorem consisted of working directly with the difference operators
D_{a;k}(x;q)
.
Here, we present a simplification of the proof which uses the duality of Section
2.2
. This method has the advantage that it can be generalized to other root systems.

2.5.
The action of
\tau_{+}
on the difference operators

The difference operators
D_{a}(x;q,t)
are representations of elements of the spherical DAHA acting on the space of functions in
N
variables. There is an action of
SL_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
on DAHA, and the
SL_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
-generator
\tau_{+}
acts on the functional representation by the adjoint action of Cherednik’s Gaussian operator
[
Che05
]

(2.16)

\gamma(x)=e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\log(x_{i})^{2}}{2\log(q)}}.

In the
q
-Whittaker limit, the adjoint action of
\gamma(x)
is well-defined. In particular, the difference operators (
2.11
) are the
\tau_{+}
-translates of
D_{a}(x;q)
:

Lemma 2.4

(
[
DFK19
]
)
.

(2.17)

D_{a;k}(x;q)=q^{-ak/2}\gamma(x)^{-k}D_{a}(x;q)\gamma(x)^{k}.

The proof follows from

\gamma(x)^{-1}\Gamma_{i}\gamma(x)=q^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{i}\Gamma_{i}.

2.6.
Fourier transform

The
q
-Whittaker functions
\{\Pi_{\lambda}(x)\}
, with
\lambda
ranging over integer partitions, form a complete basis of the space of symmetric polynomials. Suppose that a set of difference operators
\{D_{a}(x)\}
in the variables of
\mathbb{T}_{x}
satisfies

D_{a}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=\bar{D}_{a}(\Lambda)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)

for all
\lambda
, where the difference operators
\bar{D}_{a}(\Lambda)
act in the variables of
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda=q^{\lambda}}
. If the operators
\{\bar{D}_{a}(\lambda)\}
satisfy certain relations
\mathcal{R}
, then the set of operators
\{D_{a}(x)\}
satisfy the relations
\mathcal{R}^{\rm op}
with the opposite multiplication. The operators
\bar{D}_{a}(\Lambda)
are the “Fourier transforms” of the operators
D_{a}(x)
.

The strategy is to define the operators
\bar{D}_{a;k}(\Lambda;q)
, starting with initial data
\{\bar{D}_{a;0},\bar{D}_{a;1}:a\in[1,N]\}
, by using the quantum Q-system relations with the opposite multiplication. We will then show that the corresponding Fourier-dual operators
D_{a;k}(x;q)
are the difference operators (
2.15
), which therefore satisfy the quantum Q-system. The simplification of the proof in the Fourier transformed picture is due to the simple form of the initial data in
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
, which allows us to compute the
\tau_{+}
action on the Fourier transformed operators directly.

2.6.1.
Initial data

To find appropriate initial data for the opposite quantum Q-system, we start with the eigenvalue equation (
2.9
), which we write as

(2.18)

D_{a;0}(x;q)\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=\bar{D}_{a;0}(\Lambda;q)\Pi_{\lambda}(x),\qquad\bar{D}_{a;0}(\Lambda;q)=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}},\qquad a\in[1,N].

For the operators
\bar{D}_{a;k}(\Lambda;q)\in{\mathbb{C}}_{q}(\Lambda)[T]
to be well-defined from the opposite quantum Q-system for all
k
, we need another set of initial data,
\{\bar{D}_{a;1}(\Lambda;q),\ a\in[1,N]\}
, such that (we drop the arguments
{\Lambda},q
from now on):

(2.19)

\bar{D}_{a;0}\,\bar{D}_{b;1}=q^{-\min(a,b)}\,\bar{D}_{b;1}\,\bar{D}_{a;0},\qquad[\bar{D}_{a;1},\bar{D}_{b;1}]=0,\qquad a,b\in[1,N].

These are
2N
relations on the quantum torus
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
of dimension
2N
, so they determine

\{\bar{D}_{a;1}\}
up to scalar multiple, which commutes with
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
, i.e. an element in
{\mathbb{C}}(q)
. We choose this constant to be 1:

(2.20)

\bar{D}_{a;1}=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}T^{\omega_{a}}=\Lambda_{1}\cdots\Lambda_{a}T_{1}\cdots T_{a}.

2.6.2.
The set
\{\bar{D}_{a;k}\}

Given the set of
2N
initial data
\{\bar{D}_{a;k},k\in\{0,1\},a\in[1,N]\}
, which form an alternate basis for
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
, all
\bar{D}_{a;k}
are uniquely defined by the requirement that they satisfy the opposite quantum Q-system relation:

(2.21)

q^{a}\,\bar{D}_{a;k-1}\,\bar{D}_{a;k+1}=\bar{D}^{2}_{a;k}-\bar{D}_{a+1;k}\,\bar{D}_{a-1;k},\qquad\bar{D}_{0;k}=1,\ \bar{D}_{N+1;k}=0.

Remark 2.5
.

The quantum Q-system variables are cluster variables in a quantum cluster algebra, and therefore, due to the Laurent property of these algebras, all
\bar{D}_{a;k}
are in fact Laurent polynomials in the initial data (
2.18
) and (
2.20
). As the latter are monomial in the
variables of the quantum torus
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
, so are the
\bar{D}_{a;k}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{q}[\Lambda^{\pm 1},T^{\pm 1}]
.

The main theorem to be proven is

Theorem 2.6
.

\bar{D}_{a;k}(\Lambda;q)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=D_{a;k}(x;q)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)

for all
a\in[1,N]
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
D_{a;k}(x;q)
the difference operators of Equation (
2.15
)

Since
\bar{D}_{a;k}(\Lambda;q)
satisfy the opposite quantum Q-system relations, then the operators
D_{a;k}(x;q)
satisfy the quantum Q-system. Theorem
2.6
therefore implies Theorem
2.3
.

The proof of this theorem performed over the next several subsections as follows. First, in
2.7
we compute a “time translation” operator
g(\Lambda)
, whose adjoint action on
\bar{D}_{a;k}(\Lambda;q)
gives
\bar{D}_{a;k+1}(\Lambda;q)
in accordance with the opposite Q-system recursion evolution. We then show in
2.8
that this time-translation commutes with the Pieri operator or Hamiltonian
H_{1}(\Lambda;q)
. In
2.9
we prove the uniqueness of the solution of the Pieri equation
H_{1}(\Lambda,q)\,f=\hat{e}(x)\,f
. This implies, as shown in
2.10
, that
g(\Lambda)
is the Fourier transform of the Gaussian
\gamma(x)
. The proof of Theorem
2.6
follows (Section
2.11
) from a simple argument using the Fourier transform.

2.7.
The action of
\tau_{+}
on
\bar{D}_{a;k}(\Lambda;q)

The key to proving theorem
2.6
is to find an element
g(\Lambda)
in the completion of the space of rational functions in
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
which realizes the action of
\gamma(x)
in the Fourier transformed picture
4
4

4

The element
g({\Lambda})
is referred to as the “Dehn twist” generator in the geometric formulation of Ref.
[
SS18
]
, which uses a different but related definition of the Fourier transform, under the name of Whittaker transform.
:

Theorem 2.7
.

There is a unique, up to scalar multiple, function
g\equiv g(\Lambda)
acting on the variables of
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
, such that

(2.22)

\bar{D}_{a;k}=q^{-ak/2}\,g^{k}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}\,g^{-k}

is a solution of the opposite quantum Q-system for all
a,k
.

Proof.

The element
g
is determined by the commutation relation (
2.19
) and the subset of Q-system relations
(
2.21
) corresponding to
k=1
. Indeed, assuming such
an element
g
exists, we first note that

g^{m}[{\bar{D}}_{a,0},{\bar{D}}_{b,0}]g^{-m}=q^{(a+b)m/2}[{\bar{D}}_{a,m},{\bar{D}}_{b,m}]=0

for all
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Similarly, conjugating (
2.19
) and (
2.21
) for
k=1
with
g^{m}
gives

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!g^{m}({\bar{D}}_{a,0}\,{\bar{D}}_{b,1}-q^{{\rm Min}(a,b)}{\bar{D}}_{b,1}\,{\bar{D}}_{a,0})g^{-m}=q^{(a+b)m/2}({\bar{D}}_{a,m}\,{\bar{D}}_{a,m+1}-q^{{\rm Min}(a,b)}{\bar{D}}_{a,m+1}\,{\bar{D}}_{a,m})=0

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!g^{m}(q^{a}{\bar{D}}_{a,-1}{\bar{D}}_{a,1}\!\!-\!\!{\bar{D}}_{a,0}^{2}\!\!+\!\!{\bar{D}}_{a-1,0}{\bar{D}}_{a+1,0})g^{-m}=q^{am}(q^{a}{\bar{D}}_{a,m-1}{\bar{D}}_{a,m+1}\!\!-\!\!{\bar{D}}_{a,m}^{2}\!\!+\!\!{\bar{D}}_{a-1,m}{\bar{D}}_{a+1,m})=0

We therefore obtain the opposite of (
2.13
) and (
2.14
) for all
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

We now prove the existence of
g
by construction.
Assume
g=g_{T}g_{\Lambda}
, where
g_{T}
commutes with all
T_{a}
and
g_{\Lambda}
commutes with all
\Lambda_{a}
. Up to a scalar multiple,
g_{T}
is determined by the
N
equations (
2.22
) with
k=1
:

q^{-a/2}\,g\,\bar{D}_{a;0}\,g^{-1}=q^{-a/2}\,g_{T}\,\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}\,g_{T}^{-1}=\bar{D}_{a;1}=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}\,T^{\omega_{a}},\qquad a\in[1,N],

because
g_{\Lambda}
commutes with
\bar{D}_{a;0}=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}
by assumption.
This has the solution

(2.23)

g_{T}=\prod_{i=a}^{N}e^{\frac{(\log T_{a})^{2}}{2\log q}},

where we used the identity

e^{\frac{(\log T_{a})^{2}}{2\log q}}\,\Lambda_{a}\,e^{-\frac{(\log T_{a})^{2}}{2\log q}}=\Lambda_{a}\,e^{\frac{(\log qT_{a})^{2}-(\log T_{a})^{2}}{2\log q}}=q^{1/2}\,\Lambda_{a}\,T_{a}.

To find
g_{\Lambda}
, we use the Q-system (
2.21
) with
k=1
:

(2.24)

\bar{D}_{a;2}=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}\,T^{2\omega_{a}}\,(1-q\,\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}}\,T^{-\alpha_{a}}),\ a\in[1,N-1],\quad\bar{D}_{N;2}=\Lambda^{\omega_{N}}\,T^{2\omega_{N}}.

To simplify the equations below, we set
\Lambda_{N+1}=0
and
\Lambda_{0}=\infty
, i.e. we define
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{N}
by
\Lambda^{-\alpha_{0}}=\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N}}=0
.
The function
g_{\Lambda}
is defined from

(2.25)

\bar{D}_{a;1}=q^{a/2}\,g^{-1}\,\bar{D}_{a;2}\,g=q^{a/2}\,g_{\Lambda}^{-1}\,g_{T}^{-1}\,\bar{D}_{a;2}\,g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda}=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}\,T^{\omega_{a}}.

Acting on (
2.24
) by the adjoint action of
g_{T}^{-1}
, we have

q^{a/2}\,g_{T}^{-1}\,\bar{D}_{a;2}\,g_{T}=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}\,T^{\omega_{a}}\,(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}}).

Using

\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-q^{n}\,\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}})\,T^{\omega_{b}}=T^{\omega_{b}}\,\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-q^{n+\delta_{ab}}\,\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}})=T^{\omega_{b}}\,(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}})^{-1}\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-q^{n}\,\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}})

we can choose

(2.26)

g_{\Lambda}=\prod_{a=1}^{N-1}(\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}};q)_{\infty}^{-1},\qquad(a;b)_{\infty}=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-b^{n}a).

We conclude that the function

(2.27)

g=\left(\prod_{a=1}^{N}e^{\frac{(\log T_{a})^{2}}{2\log q}}\right)\ \prod_{a=1}^{N-1}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{a+1}}{\Lambda_{a}};q\right)_{\infty}^{-1}

satisfies the conditions of Theorem
2.7
.
∎

2.8.
Commutation with the first Pieri operator

In general, the translation operator
g
commutes with each of the Pieri operators
H_{a}(\Lambda;q)
. It is sufficient for our purposes to show that it commutes with the first,
H_{1}(\Lambda;q)
:

Theorem 2.8
.

The function
g(\Lambda)
of Theorem
2.7
commutes with the Pieri operator

H_{1}(\Lambda;q)
of Equation (
2.12
).

Proof.

From (
2.12
),

\displaystyle H_{1}(\Lambda;q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{N}(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a-1}})T_{a}=\sum_{a=1}^{N}T_{a}-\sum_{a=1}^{N-1}\frac{\Lambda_{a+1}}{\Lambda_{a}}T_{a+1}.

For all
a\in[1,N]
,

g(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a-1}})T_{a}g^{-1}=g_{T}(1-q^{-1}\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}})T_{a}g_{T}^{-1}=(T_{a}-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}}T_{a+1}),

again with the convention that
\Lambda^{-\alpha_{0}}=\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N}}=0
.
Summing over all
a=1,2,...,N
results in
gH_{1}(\Lambda;q)g^{-1}=H_{1}(\Lambda;q)
.
∎

2.9.
Uniqueness of the Pieri solution

To prove the result of next section, we resort to a uniqueness argument regarding the solutions to the first Pieri equation
(
2.30
).

Recall that
\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
is eigenfunction
of the Macdonald operator:
D_{1}\,\Pi_{\lambda}={\Lambda}_{1}\,\Pi_{\lambda}
. Writing
\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=x^{\lambda}p_{\lambda}(x)
,
and
D_{1}=\sum_{i}\phi_{i}(x)\,\Gamma_{i}
, the eigenvalue equation turns into

(2.28)

(1-\sum_{i}\phi_{i}(x)\,\frac{{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{1}}\,\Gamma_{i})\,p_{\lambda}(x)=0.

This equation makes it easy to analytically continue

p_{\lambda}(x)
to a function
\hat{p}_{\lambda}(x)
with
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
,
as the dependence on
{\Lambda}
is explicit (in fact polynomial of the variables
{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{i}}
,
{\alpha}_{i}
the simple roots of
A_{N-1}
).
Writing
\hat{p}_{\lambda}(x)

as a series of the variables
x^{-{\alpha}_{i}}
:
\hat{p}_{\lambda}(x)=\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}c_{\beta}({\Lambda})x^{-\beta}
,
Q_{+}
the positive cone of the root lattice of
A_{N-1}
,
(
2.28
) turns into a linear triangular system for the coefficients
c_{\beta}({\Lambda})
,
which are uniquely determined (with
c_{0}({\Lambda})=1
), and rational functions of the
{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{i}}
.
Moreover, specializing
\lambda
to an integer partition in
\hat{p}_{\lambda}(x)
recovers
p_{\lambda}(x)
.
Expanding
c_{\beta}({\Lambda})=\sum_{\delta\in Q_{+}}c_{\beta,\delta}{\Lambda}^{-\delta}
allows to view
\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
as the specialization of a series in
{\Lambda}
:

x^{\lambda}\hat{p}_{\lambda}(x)=x^{\lambda}\sum_{\delta\in Q_{+}}\hat{c}_{\delta}(x)\,{\Lambda}^{-\delta},\ \ \hat{c}_{\delta}(x)=\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}c_{\beta,\delta}x^{-\beta}.

Lemma 2.9
.

Assume we have a (non necessarily polynomial) solution
\Theta_{\lambda}(x)
of the first Pieri rule (
2.30
),
which admits a series expansion of the form
\Theta_{\lambda}(x)=x^{\lambda}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}\tau_{\beta}(x)\,{\Lambda}^{-\beta}
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
.
Then we have, for
\lambda
an integer partition:

\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{\hat{c}_{0}(x)}{\tau_{0}(x)}\Theta_{\lambda}(x),

where
\hat{c}_{0}(x)
is the leading coefficient in the series
\hat{p}_{\lambda}(x)
that specializes to

\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
for integer partitions
\lambda
.

Proof.

Write
\Theta_{\lambda}(x)=x^{\lambda}\theta_{\lambda}(x)
. By use of (
2.12
) for
a=1
,
the Pieri equation turns into

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}\left\{(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{i}})T_{i}-1\right\}\right)\theta_{\lambda}(x)=0.

This is a linear triangular system for the coefficients
\tau_{\beta}(x)
, which are uniquely determined for

\beta\neq 0
, proportional to
\tau_{0}(x)
. The same holds for the coefficients
\hat{c}_{\beta}(x)

of
\hat{p}_{\lambda}(x)
in terms of
\hat{c}_{0}(x)
. We deduce that

x^{\lambda}\,\hat{p}_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{\hat{c}_{0}(x)}{\theta_{0}(x)}\Theta_{\lambda}(x)
, and the Lemma follows
by specialization.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

The argument used in this section prefigures the reformulation in terms of universal solutions performed in Sect.
2.13
below, and extends to all types.

2.10.
Fourier duality of
g
and
\gamma

The final piece of information we need to prove Theorem
2.6
is that
g
acting on the
q
-Whittaker functions is equal to the
Gaussian
\gamma(x)
acting on the same functions:

Theorem 2.11
.

(2.29)

g(\Lambda)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=\gamma(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x).

Proof.

Acting with
g(\Lambda)
on both sides of the first Pieri rule

(2.30)

H_{1}(\Lambda;q)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=e_{1}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)

and noting that
g
and
\gamma
commute with both
H_{1}(\Lambda;q)
and
e_{1}(x)
, we see
that
\gamma^{-1}\,g\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
obeys the
same
first Pieri rule.
We use the uniqueness argument of the previous section for the solutions of (
2.30
).
Applying Lemma
2.9
to
\Theta_{\lambda}=\gamma^{-1}\,g\,x^{\lambda}\,\hat{p}_{\lambda}(x)
,
the latter must be proportional to
\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
when specialized to an integer partition
\lambda
.
To determine the proportionality constant, we compute the leading coefficient of the series expansion
x^{-\lambda}\,\gamma^{-1}\,g\,x^{\lambda}\,\hat{p}_{\lambda}(x)
. Since
g_{\Lambda}
is a power series in
\{\Lambda^{-\alpha_{i}}\}
with leading term 1, the leading term is determined by the action of
g_{T}
on
x^{\lambda}\,\hat{c}_{0}(x)
.

We claim that

g_{T}x^{\lambda}=\gamma\,x^{\lambda}{g}^{\prime}_{T},

where
g^{\prime}_{T}
acts only by shifting
\Lambda
by powers of
q
, leaving the leading term
\hat{c}_{0}(x)
unchanged.

To see this, let
a,b
be elements in an algebra such that
[a,[a,b]]=0
and
[b,[a,b]]=c
commutes with both
a
and
b
.
Then the Campbell-Hausdorff formula
implies:

(2.31)

e^{a}\,e^{b}\,e^{-a}\,e^{-b}=e^{a+b+\frac{1}{2}[a,b]-\frac{1}{12}[b,[a,b]]}e^{-a-b+\frac{1}{2}[a,b]+\frac{1}{12}[b,[a,b]]}=e^{[a,b]+\frac{c}{2}}.

Choosing
e^{a}=g_{T}
and
e^{b}=x^{\lambda}
, i.e.
a=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial_{\lambda_{i}}^{2}}{2\log q}
and
b=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\,\log x_{i}
leads to

[a,b]=\sum_{i}\frac{\log x_{i}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i}}}{\log q}
and
e^{\frac{1}{2}c}=\gamma(x)^{-1}
, which commutes with
e^{[a,b]}
.
We finally write

\displaystyle e^{a}\,e^{b}=g_{T}\,x^{\lambda}=e^{\frac{c}{2}}\,e^{[a,b]}\,e^{b}\,e^{a}=\gamma^{-1}e^{\sum_{i}\frac{\log x_{i}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i}}}{\log q}}e^{\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\log x_{i}}\,g_{T}=\gamma\,x^{\lambda}\,\prod_{i}T_{i}^{\frac{\log x_{i}}{\log q}}g_{T}=\gamma\,x^{\lambda}\,g_{T}^{\prime}

where the last step uses again (
2.31
), with
a=\sum_{i}\frac{\log x_{i}\,\partial_{\lambda_{i}}}{\log q}
,
b=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\log x_{i}

hence
e^{[a,b]}=\gamma^{2}
and
c=0
.

We conclude that
\gamma^{-1}\,g\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
, and the theorem follows.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

The relation
g(\Lambda)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=\gamma(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
is equivalent to the recursion relation for
the quantities denoted
J_{\beta}^{\mu}
in
[
FJMM09
]
(Theorem 3.1), which we denote as
J_{\beta}(x=q^{\mu})
below. These can be identified as the coefficients in the formal expansion of the series

\widetilde{\Pi}_{\lambda}(x):=g_{\Lambda}\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=x^{\lambda}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}J_{\beta}(x)\Lambda^{-\beta}
.
Theorem
2.11
says that
g_{T}g_{\Lambda}\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=\gamma(x)\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
, which implies

(g_{\Lambda}g_{T})\widetilde{\Pi}_{\lambda}(x)=\gamma(x)\,\widetilde{\Pi}_{\lambda}(x)
. As a recursion relation for coefficients, this means

J_{\beta}^{\mu}=\sum_{\delta}\frac{\gamma(q^{\delta})}{(q,q)_{\beta-\delta}}x^{-\delta}J_{\delta}^{\mu}

with the notation
(q,q)_{\alpha}=\prod_{i}(q,q)_{{\alpha}_{i}}
,
where we have used the series expansion
g_{\Lambda}=\sum_{\alpha\in Q_{+}}\frac{\Lambda^{-\alpha}}{(q;q)_{\alpha}}
,
as well as the relation
\gamma(x)^{-1}x^{-\lambda}g_{T}x^{\lambda}\Lambda^{-\delta}=\gamma(q^{\delta})(\Lambda x)^{-\delta}
.
A similar connection holds for certain other root systems, see Remark
4.13
.

Corollary 2.13
.

The (higher)
q
-difference Toda Hamiltonians
H_{a}({\Lambda})
are algebraically independent conserved quantities of the
opposite quantum Q-system (
2.21
).

Proof.

Multiply the Pieri equation
e_{a}(x)\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=H_{a}({\Lambda})\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
on the left by
g\gamma^{-1}
, and use
Theorem
2.11
to rewrite:

g\gamma^{-1}\,e_{a}(x)\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=e_{a}(x)\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=g\,H_{a}({\Lambda})\,g^{-1}\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=H_{a}({\Lambda})\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x),

hence
g
commutes with all
H_{a}({\Lambda})
,
a=1,2,...,N
. The latter are Laurent polynomials of the elements of the quantum torus

\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
, hence of the initial data
\bar{D}_{i;0},\bar{D}_{i;1}
as well, which are invariant under any time translation

(\bar{D}_{i;0},\bar{D}_{i;1})\mapsto(\bar{D}_{i;n},\bar{D}_{i;n+1})
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. These are independent conserved quantities
of the opposite quantum Q-system that governs the
\bar{D}_{a;n}
, as any dependence between
H_{a}({\Lambda})
would imply
a dependence between the
e_{a}(x)
by inverse Fourier transform.
∎

2.11.
Proof of Theorem
2.6

The proof of the main Theorem
2.6
now follows.

Proof.

Acting on
D_{a;0}(x;q)\Pi_{\lambda}(x)
by
\gamma(x)^{-k}\,g^{k}(\Lambda)
, we have

\gamma^{-k}\,g^{k}D_{a;0}(x;q)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=\gamma^{-k}\,D_{a;0}(x;q)\,g^{k}\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=\gamma^{-k}\,D_{a;0}(x;q)\,\gamma^{k}\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=q^{ak/2}\,D_{a;k}(x;q)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x).

Using the eigenvalue equation (
2.9
), this is equal to

\gamma^{-k}\,g^{k}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=g^{k}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}\,\gamma^{-k}\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=g^{k}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}\,g^{-k}\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=q^{ak/2}\,\bar{D}_{a;k}\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x),

which is the statement of Theorem
2.6
.
∎

2.12.
Pieri operators, conserved quantities and Toda Hamiltonians

Let us compare the result of Corollary
2.13
with the conserved quantities of the (opposite) quantum
Q-system of
[
DFK11
,
DFK10
,
DFK18
]
. Those references use a different normalization of the Q-system variables, resulting in the system

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{a;n}\,\bar{Q}_{b;n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v^{-\Lambda_{a,b}}\,\bar{Q}_{b;n+1}\,\bar{Q}_{a;n}

(2.32)

\displaystyle v^{\Lambda_{a,a}}\,\bar{Q}_{a;n-1}\,\bar{Q}_{a;n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\bar{Q}_{a;n})^{2}-\bar{Q}_{a-1;n}\,\bar{Q}_{a+1;n}

with
\Lambda_{a,b}=\min(a,b)\big(N-\max(a,b)\big)
.
The precise relation with the operators
\bar{D}_{a;k}(\Lambda;q)
is

(2.33)

\bar{Q}_{a;n}=q^{-\frac{n{\alpha}^{2}}{2N}}\,(\bar{D}_{N;0})^{-\frac{a}{N}}\,\bar{D}_{a;n}

with
v=q^{1/N}
. The conserved quantities
\bar{C}_{m}
of the quantum system (
2.32
)
are expressed in terms of the initial cluster
\{\bar{Q}_{a;0},\bar{Q}_{a;1}\}

as hard particle partition functions (i.e. generating polynomials of independent sets of vertices) on a graph (Figure 3 of
[
DFK10
]
)
with ordered vertices labeled
1,2,...,2N-1
, with a weight
\bar{y}_{i}
per occupied vertex labeled
i
, where:

\displaystyle\bar{y}_{2a-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{a-1;0}\,(\bar{Q}_{a;0})^{-1}\,(\bar{Q}_{a-1;1})^{-1}\,\bar{Q}_{a;1}\qquad(a=1,2,...,N),

\displaystyle\bar{y}_{2a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{Q}_{a-1;0}\,(\bar{Q}_{a;0})^{-1}\,(\bar{Q}_{a;1})^{-1}\,\bar{Q}_{a+1;1}\qquad(a=1,2,...,N-1).

Using (
2.33
),

\bar{y}_{2a-1}=v^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,T_{a},\qquad\bar{y}_{2a}=-v^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{{\Lambda}_{a+1}}{{\Lambda}_{a}}\,T_{a+1},

hence the resulting conserved quantities are related to the Hamiltonians (
2.12
) by
H_{m}({\Lambda})=v^{m/2}\,\bar{C}_{m}
.

In Ref.
[
DFK18
]
, it was shown that the conserved quantities
\bar{C}_{m}
can be interpreted as type
A
(relativistic)

q
-difference Toda Hamiltonians
[
Eti99
]
. This justifies the identification of the Pieri operators (
2.12
)
as
q
-difference Toda Hamiltonians.

2.13.
Universal solutions and duality

The results of the previous sections may be rephrased in the more uniform context of universal solutions, obtained explicitly in the case of type A in
[
NS12
]
. The following discussion treats the two sets
x
and
s
on equal footing, as formal variables, and implies that the duality property is more general.

2.13.1.
Universal Macdonald and Pieri solutions and duality

We now consider
s=t^{\rho}q^{\lambda}
as a
formal
variable which we may specialize to
integer partitions
\lambda
, and similarly write
x=q^{\mu}t^{\rho}
.
If we consider the difference operators (
2.1
) in the space
{\mathbb{C}}(q,t)[[\{x^{-{\alpha}_{i}}\}]][\Gamma_{1},...,\Gamma_{N}],

i.e. expanded as power series of the variables
x_{i+1}/x_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq N-1
,
then there is a unique series solution
P(x;s)
to the Macdonald eigenvalue equation

(2.34)

\mathcal{D}_{1}(x;q,t)\,P(x;s)=e_{1}(x)\,P(x;s)

of the form

(2.35)

P(x;s)=q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}c_{\beta}(s;q,t)x^{-\beta},\qquad c_{0}(s;q,t)=1.

Note that
q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}=x^{\lambda}\,t^{-\rho\cdot\lambda}=s^{\mu}\,t^{-\rho\cdot\mu}
is symmetric with respect to the interchange of
x
and
s
. The uniqueness of the solution follows from the fact that (
2.34
) is a nonsingular triangular system for the coefficients
c_{\beta}(s;q,t)
.

The series (
2.35
) is called the universal Macdonald solution because, under the specialization of the
variables
s=q^{\lambda}\,t^{\rho}
with
\lambda
an integer partition, the function
t^{\rho\cdot\lambda}\,P(x;s)
specializes to the symmetric Macdonald polynomial
P_{\lambda}(x)
:

(2.36)

P_{\lambda}(x)=t^{\rho\cdot\lambda}P(x;q^{\lambda}\,t^{\rho}),\qquad\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{N}),\quad\lambda_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}.

That is, the infinite series (
2.35
) truncates to a finite number of terms, and the prefactor
t^{\rho\cdot\lambda}q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}=x^{\lambda}
ensures that the function is a symmetric polynomial of
x_{1},...,x_{N}
.
All Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}(x)
are obtained as specializations of
P(x;s)
.
On the other hand, the series
P(x;s)
also specializes to the Baker-Akhiezer quasi-polynomials introduced by Chalykh
[
Cha02
]
. In this case, the relevant specialization consists in taking
t=q^{-k}
,
k
some positive integer. One can check that another truncation occurs leaving us with a finite sum.

Example 2.14
.

Let us illustrate the phenomenon of truncation in the simplest case of
A_{1}^{(1)}
of rank
N=2
. The universal Macdonald series is expressed in terms of the
variables
u=x_{2}/x_{1}
and
v={\Lambda}_{2}/{\Lambda}_{1}
as

P(x;s)=x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}t^{-\lambda_{1}}\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u^{n}\,\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{1-vq^{i}}{1-t^{-1}vq^{i}}\,\frac{1-t^{-1}q^{-i}}{1-q^{-i-1}}.

The first type of truncation occurs for
v=q^{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}}=q^{-k}
for some integer
k\geq 0
. We see that the first factor in the numerator
of the coefficient in the series vanishes as
soon as
n>k
(for
i=k
). The second type of truncation occurs when
t=q^{-k}
for some integer
k\geq 0

and arbitrary
\lambda
. In that case the second factor vanishes as soon as
n>k
.

Similarly, if we consider
\mathcal{H}_{1}(\Lambda;q,t)
as a power series in
\{s_{i+1}/s_{i}\}
,
then the equation

(2.37)

t^{-\rho\cdot\lambda}\mathcal{H}_{1}(\Lambda;q,t)t^{\rho\cdot\lambda}Q(s;x)=e_{1}(x)Q(s;x)

has a unique “universal Pieri” solution of the form

(2.38)

Q(s;x)=q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}\bar{c}_{\beta}(x;q,t)s^{-\beta},\qquad\bar{c}_{0}(x;q,t)=1.

An outcome of the study of
[
NS12
]
is a relation between the universal Macdonald and Pieri solutions, which we re-prove below.

Theorem 2.15
.

The universal Macdonald and Pieri solutions are related via

P(x;s)=\Delta(x)\,Q(s;x),

with
\Delta(x)
as in (
2.4
).

Proof.

First note that
P(x;s)
obey both Macdonald eigenvalue and Pieri equations
[
Che95b
,
Cha02
]
.
Expanding the coefficients of
P(x;s)
as series of
s^{-{\alpha}_{i}}
:
c_{\beta}(s)=\sum_{\delta\in Q_{+}}c_{\beta,\delta}s^{-\delta}
allows to rewrite

P(x;s)=q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}\sum_{\delta\in Q_{+}}\hat{c}_{\delta}(x)\,s^{-\delta}
, where
\hat{c}_{\delta}(x)=\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}c_{\beta,\delta}x^{-\beta}
.
Now both
P(x;s)
and
Q(s;x)
can be viewed as two different solutions of the Pieri equation. By uniqueness, they must be proportional up to an
s
-independent factor. We deduce that
P(x;s)=\hat{c}_{0}(x)Q(s;x)
. To compute
\hat{c}_{0}(x)
, note that it is the limit of

q^{-\lambda\cdot\mu}P(x;s)=\hat{c}_{0}(x)+O(s^{-{\alpha}_{i}})
, when we take
|s_{1}|>>|s_{2}|>>\cdots|s_{N}|>>1
, so that all
s^{-{\alpha}_{i}}\to 0
. Using the
a
-th Macdonald eigenvalue equation (
2.2
) and the explicit formula (
2.1
), and dividing by
{\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{a}
, we have for
a=1,2,...,N
:

\left\{t^{-{a\choose 2}}\frac{e_{a}(s)}{{\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{a}}-\sum_{I\subset[1,N]\atop|I|=a}\prod_{j\not\in I\atop i\in I}\frac{tx_{i}-x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{a}}\prod_{i\in I}{\Lambda}_{i}\Gamma_{i}\right\}\,\big(\hat{c}_{0}(x)+O(x^{{\alpha}_{i}})\big)=0.

In the limit
{\Lambda}_{i+1}/{\Lambda}_{i}\to 0
, all the terms tend to zero except for the leading term in the eigenvalue, and the term corresponding to
I=\{1,2,...,a\}
in the sum:

(2.39)

\left\{1-\prod_{1\leq i\leq a<j\leq N}\frac{1-\frac{x_{j}}{tx_{i}}}{1-\frac{x_{j}}{x_{i}}}\,\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}\cdots\Gamma_{a}\right\}\,\hat{c}_{0}(x)=0.

We verify that
\hat{c}_{0}(x)
and
\Delta(x)
of (
2.4
) both obey (
2.39
) for
a=1,2,...,N
.
Their ratio must therefore be independent of
x
as it is invariant under each
\Gamma_{i}
,
and is easily identified to 1 by taking the limit when all
x^{-{\alpha}_{i}}\to 0
and using
c_{0,0}=1
. The Theorem follows.
∎

Repeating the argument of Section
2.2
starting with (
2.34
), including the interchange of the labels
x
and
s
,
we see that

t^{-\rho\cdot\lambda}\mathcal{H}_{1}(\Lambda;q,t)t^{\rho\cdot\lambda}\,Q(x;s)=e_{1}(x)\,Q(x;s),

where the expansion in
s
of
Q(x;s)=P(s;x)/\Delta(s)
has the form
q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}(1+\mathcal{O}(s^{-\alpha_{i}}))
. By uniqueness of the solution,

(2.40)

Q(x;s)=Q(s;x)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Delta(s)\,P(x;s)=\Delta(x)\,P(s;x).

The duality (
2.40
) states that the series
Q(s;x)
in
s
is equal to the series
Q(x;s)
in
x
. The specialization of
\lambda
and
\mu
to integer partitions reduces to the Macdonald polynomial duality relation (
2.3
). We may therefore interpret (
2.40
) as a universal extension of the duality of Macdonald theory.

2.13.2.
q
-Whittaker limit

The symmetry (
2.40
) is replaced in the
t\to\infty
limit by

(2.41)

\Pi(x;\Lambda)=\overline{\Delta}(x)\mathrm{K}(\Lambda;x),\qquad\overline{\Delta}(x)=\prod_{\alpha\in R_{+}}(qx^{-\alpha};q)_{\infty},

where the universal
q
-Whittaker function
\Pi(x;\Lambda)
and Pieri solution
\mathrm{K}(\Lambda;x)
read respectively

\Pi(x;\Lambda)=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{\rho\lambda}P(x;s),\quad\mathrm{K}(\Lambda;x)=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{\rho\lambda}Q(s;x)

and satisfy the limiting Macdonald eigenvalue equations and Pieri rules:

(2.42)

\displaystyle D_{a}(x;q)\,\Pi(x;\Lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\omega_{a}}\,\Pi(x;\Lambda)

(2.43)

\displaystyle H_{a}(\Lambda;q)\,\mathrm{K}(\Lambda;x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{a}(x)\mathrm{K}(\Lambda;x)

As in the case of the Macdonald function, the universal functions
\Pi(x;\Lambda)
and
\mathrm{K}(\Lambda;x)
are uniquely determined from the difference equations (
2.42
) and (
2.43
) as series of the form

(2.44)

\displaystyle\Pi(x;\Lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{\lambda}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}{c}_{\beta}(\Lambda;q)x^{-\beta},\qquad{c}_{0}(\Lambda;q)=1,

(2.45)

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}(\Lambda;x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{\lambda}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}\bar{c}_{\beta}(x;q)\Lambda^{-\beta},\qquad\bar{c}_{0}(x;q)=1.

Example 2.16
.

Let us compute
\mathrm{K}(\Lambda;x)
explicitly as a solution of the first Pieri equation
H_{1}(\Lambda;q)\,\mathrm{K}(\Lambda;x)=e_{1}(x)\mathrm{K}(\Lambda;x)
with
H_{1}({\Lambda};q)
as in (
2.12
) for
a=1
,
by use of a path model. The equation reduces to the following triangular system for the coefficients
\bar{c}_{\beta}(x;q)
from (
2.45
):

\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}(q^{\beta_{i-1}-\beta_{i}}-1)\right\}\bar{c}_{\beta}(x;q)=\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}x_{i+1}\,q^{\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1}-1}\,\bar{c}_{\beta-e_{i}}(x;q),

with
\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\beta_{i}e_{i}
and
\beta_{0}=\beta_{N}=0
by convention.
The system is nonsingular for
x
generic, and
we may express
\bar{c}_{\beta}(x;q)
as a path model
partition function, namely as the sum over all paths
p
from
0
to
\beta
in the positive quadrant
{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}^{N-1}
of path weights
w(p)
.
The path weight is defined as a product along the path of its vertex weights
w_{v}(a)=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}(q^{a_{i-1}-a_{i}}-1))^{-1}
per vertex
a
visited and edge step weights
w_{e}(s)=x_{i+1}\,q^{b_{i}-b_{i+1}-1}
per edge step
s=b-e_{i}\to b
:

\bar{c}_{\beta}(x;q)=\sum_{{\rm paths}\,p:0\to\beta}\quad\prod_{{\rm vertex}\,a\,{\rm of}\,p}w_{v}(a)\prod_{{\rm edge}\,{\rm step}\,s\,{\rm of}\,p}w_{e}(s)

This construction parallels that of Whittaker vectors performed in
[
DFKT17
]
.

The Fourier transform may be restated in terms of the universal
q
-Whittaker function as follows:

f(x)\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda})={\bar{f}}({\Lambda})\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda})
, and our main theorem
2.6
as
D_{a;k}(x;q)\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda})=\bar{D}_{a;k}({\Lambda};q)\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda})
, also a consequence
of
g({\Lambda})\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda})=\gamma(x)\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda})
.

3.
Duality property for other classical root systems

In this section we present a generalization of the methods of Section
2
for the root systems corresponding to the affine algebras in the classical series, listed in Table
1
. In these cases, the underlying double affine Hecke algebra is of
BC
-type, corresponding to finite-type Weyl groups of types
C_{N}
or
D_{N}
, and the commuting
q
-difference operators and their eigenfunctions are Koornwinder/Macdonald operators and polynomials.
A key ingredient
is the duality property, which implies a relation between the Koornwinder/Macdonald eigenvalue equations and the Pieri rules
[
vD96
,
Sah99
,
Che97
]
.

Our goal is to choose a set of
N
commuting difference operators for each algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
such that, in the
q
-Whittaker limit, these difference operators and their
SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-translates satisfy the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-type quantum Q-system.
There are several known constructions of the difference operators. Koornwinder introduced a first order operator
[
Koo92
]
depending on generic parameters
(q,t,a,b,c,d)
, which was extended by van Diejen to a complete set of
N
commuting difference operators
[
vD95
]
.
Upon specialization of the parameters
(a,b,c,d)
, these correspond to the root systems of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. In some cases, van Diejen’s operators must be supplemented by operators introduced by Macdonald
[
Mac01
]
, who used the structure of specific root systems to produce difference operators for miniscule co-weights.
We combine these constructions and define a set of
N
commuting
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald
operators in Appendix
A
, whose eigenvalues form a basis for the space of fundamental characters of the finite algebra
R^{*}=R({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})
(see Table
1
).

This section is organized as follows. We introduce the Koornwinder operators for generic values of
(a,b,c,d)
in (Section
3.1
). The specialization of the parameters corresponding to
{\mathfrak{g}}
of Table
1
are explained in Section
3.2
.
Finally, the
q
-Whittaker limit of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators is described in Section
3.3
, together with their
\tau_{+}
-translates. We will prove that the
\tau_{+}
translation is the discrete time evolution of the associated quantum Q-systems in Section
4
.

3.1.
Koornwinder Operators, Polynomials and Pieri rules

3.1.1.
The Koornwinder operators

Let
\mathbb{F}
be the field of rational functions in the indeterminates
a,b,c,d,q^{\frac{1}{2}},t^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
W
the Weyl group of type
C_{N}
. It acts on functions in
\mathbb{F}(x_{1},...,x_{N})

by permutations and inversions of the variables. The
W
-invariant space of Laurent polynomials in
x
is generated by
the elementary symmetric functions
\hat{e}(x)
, defined via the generating function

(3.1)

{\hat{E}}(z;x):=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\prod_{\epsilon=\pm 1}(1+zx_{i}^{\epsilon})=\sum_{k=0}^{2N}z^{k}\,{\hat{e}}_{k}(x),

so that
{\hat{e}}_{1}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}+x_{i}^{-1})
. The space
\mathbb{F}[x,x^{-1}]^{W}
is preserved by the action of the Koornwinder operator, defined as follows.

Definition 3.1
.

The Koornwinder operator
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)
is the
q
-difference operator acting on
\mathbb{F}[x,x^{-1}]
:

(3.2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\Phi_{i,\epsilon}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)\,\,(\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}-1),\qquad\Gamma_{i}x_{j}=q^{\delta_{ij}}x_{j}\Gamma_{i},

where

\displaystyle\Phi_{i,\epsilon}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-ax_{i}^{\epsilon})(1-bx_{i}^{\epsilon})(1-cx_{i}^{\epsilon})(1-dx_{i}^{\epsilon})}{(1-x_{i}^{2\epsilon})(1-qx_{i}^{2\epsilon})}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}-1}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}-1}.

Koornwinder polynomials are the unique monic, symmetric, Laurent polynomial eigenfunctions of the eigenvalue equation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma t^{N-1}\left(\hat{e}_{1}(s)-\hat{e}_{1}(\sigma t^{\rho})\right)P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)

where
\lambda
is any integer partition coding the leading term
x^{\lambda}
of
P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)
. We make use of the notations

(3.3)

s_{i}=q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{\rho_{i}}\sigma,\qquad\sigma=\sqrt{{abcd}/{q}},\qquad\rho_{i}=N-i\qquad(i=1,2,...,N).

As an example, the first two Koornwinder polynomials are
P_{\emptyset}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=1
, with eigenvalue 0, and

P_{1,0,0,...,0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)={\hat{e}}_{1}(x)+\frac{1-t^{N}}{1-t}\,\frac{abcd(a^{-1}+b^{-1}+c^{-1}+d^{-1})t^{N-1}-(a+b+c+d)}{1-abcd\,t^{2N-2}}.

3.1.2.
Koornwinder-Macdonald operators

We define the Koornwinder-Macdonald operators to be the set of mutually commuting difference operators, which commute with the Koornwinder operator, chosen so that their eigenvalues are proportional to the elementary symmetric functions
\hat{e}_{m}(s)
.
The first order Koornwinder-Macdonald operator (
A.23
)
is

(3.4)

{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)={\mathcal{K}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)+\frac{1-t^{N}}{1-t}\left(1+\frac{abcd}{q}t^{N-1}\right).

The additive constant in (
3.4
) is
\sigma\,t^{N-1}\,{\hat{e}}_{1}(\sigma t^{\rho})
, so that Koornwinder polynomials satisfy the eigenvalue equation

(3.5)

{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=\sigma\,t^{N-1}\,{\hat{e}}_{1}(s)\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x).

Definition 3.2
.

The Koornwinder-Macdonald operators are a commuting family of difference operators
\{{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t):m\in[1,N]\}
, which are linear
combinations of van Diejen’s commuting difference operators
[
vD96
]
, uniquely defined by their eigenvalues:

(3.6)

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=d_{\lambda;m}^{(a,b,c,d)}\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x),\quad d_{\lambda;m}^{(a,b,c,d)}=\sigma^{m}\,t^{m(N-\frac{m+1}{2})}\,{\hat{e}}_{m}(s).

These operators are explicitly constructed in Appendix
A
, see Definitions
A.2
,
A.8
and Theorem
A.9
.

3.1.3.
Rains operators

A useful alternative “
N
-th order” difference operator was constructed by
Rains
[
Rai05
]
5
5

5

Some of these operators actually appeared in earlier works of van Diejen,
but Rains’ construction is more systematic.
in his study of BC-symmetric polynomials. Rains shows that the operators

(3.7)

{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(u,v)}(x;q,t)=\sum_{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},...,\epsilon_{N}=\pm 1}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{(1-ux_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}})(1-vx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}})}{1-x_{i}^{2\epsilon_{i}}}\,\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}/2}

are maps between
W
-invariant spaces with different values of the parameters
(a,b,c,d)
. They act between bases of Koornwinder polynomials as follows:

(3.8)

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}{a},q^{-\frac{1}{2}}b)}(x;q,t)\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)&=q^{-|\lambda|/2}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1-abq^{\lambda_{i}-1}t^{N-i})\,P_{\lambda}^{(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}a,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}b,q^{\frac{1}{2}}c,q^{\frac{1}{2}}d)}(x),\\
{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}c,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}d)}(x;q,t)\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)&=q^{-|\lambda|/2}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1-cdq^{\lambda_{i}-1}t^{N-i})\,P_{\lambda}^{(q^{\frac{1}{2}}a,q^{\frac{1}{2}}b,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}c,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}d)}(x),\end{array}

where
|\lambda|=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}
.
The product

(3.9)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(a,b)}(x;q,t)\,{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(q^{-\frac{1}{2}}c,q^{-\frac{1}{2}}d)}(x;q,t)

commutes with the Koornwinder operators, since Koornwinder polynomials satisfy the eigenvalue equation

\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=\hat{d}_{\lambda;N}^{(a,b,c,d)}\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x),

with eigenvalues

(3.10)

\hat{d}_{\lambda;N}^{(a,b,c,d)}=q^{-|\lambda|}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1-abq^{\lambda_{i}}t^{N-i})(1-cdq^{\lambda_{i}-1}t^{N-i}).

The operator (
3.9
) is not linearly independent of the set of Koornwinder-Macdonald operators: See Section
A.2.3
, Lemma
A.10
for the explicit expression. However, its factored form will play a crucial role in the proof of our main theorems below.

3.1.4.
Duality

Due to the existence of an anti-involution
*
of the DAHA,
Koornwinder polynomials obey a remarkable duality property
[
vD96
,
Sah99
]
. The involution acts on the parameters
(a,b,c,d)
as

(3.11)

a^{*}=\Big(\frac{abcd}{q}\Big)^{1/2},\quad b^{*}=-\Big(q\frac{ab}{cd}\Big)^{1/2},\quad c^{*}=\Big(q\frac{ac}{bd}\Big)^{1/2},\quad d^{*}=-\Big(q\frac{ad}{bc}\Big)^{1/2},

so that
\sigma^{*}=a
. The duality property for Koornwinder polynomials is

(3.12)

\frac{P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}})}{P^{(a,b,c,d)}_{\lambda}(t^{\rho^{*}})}=\frac{P_{\mu}^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})}{P_{\mu}^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(t^{\rho})}.

where
\mu,\lambda
are both integer partitions.

\rho,\rho^{*}
are defined by

(3.13)

t^{\rho_{i}}=t^{\rho^{(a,b,c,d)}_{i}}=\sigma t^{N-i},\qquad t^{\rho^{*}_{i}}=t^{\rho^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}_{i}}=at^{N-i}.

3.1.5.
Pieri rules

Using the duality relation (
3.12
), one obtains the Pieri rules for Koornwinder polynomials with parameters
(a,b,c,d)
from the eigenvalue equation with parameters
(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})
.
It is useful to define the function

(3.14)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{(\frac{q}{x_{i}^{2}};q)_{\infty}}{(\frac{q}{ax_{i}};q)_{\infty}(\frac{q}{bx_{i}};q)_{\infty}(\frac{q}{cx_{i}};q)_{\infty}(\frac{q}{dx_{i}};q)_{\infty}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\prod_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\frac{(\frac{qx_{j}^{\epsilon}}{x_{i}};q)_{\infty}}{(\frac{qx_{j}^{\epsilon}}{tx_{i}};q)_{\infty}}.

The normalization factor in the duality (
3.12
) is given by (See Theorem 5.1 of
[
vD96
]
):

(3.15)

P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(t^{\rho^{*}})=t^{\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\frac{\Delta^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(t^{\rho})}{\Delta^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})},\ \ P_{\mu}^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(t^{\rho})=t^{\rho\cdot\mu}\frac{\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(t^{\rho^{*}})}{\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}})},

Using the parametrization
x=q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}}
in the eigenvalue equation (
3.6
),

(3.16)

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}(q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}};q,t)P_{\lambda}(q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}})=\theta_{m}\,{\hat{e}}_{m}(q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})\,P_{\lambda}(q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}}),\qquad m\in[1,N],

where
\theta_{m}=\sigma^{m}\,t^{m(N-\frac{m+1}{2})}
and
\Gamma_{i}:\mu_{j}\mapsto\mu_{j}+\delta_{i,j}
in the difference operator
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}={\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}
. Upon specializing
\mu
to integer partitions, we use
the duality relation (
3.12
), where we replace the normalization factor by (
3.15
). Equation (
3.16
) becomes

\displaystyle\theta_{m}{\hat{e}}_{m}(q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})\,P_{\mu}^{*}(q^{\lambda}\,t^{\rho})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{\mu}^{*}(t^{\rho})\,{\mathcal{D}}_{m}(q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}};q,t)\,P_{\mu}^{*}(t^{\rho})^{-1}\,P_{\mu}^{*}(q^{\lambda}\,t^{\rho})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(t^{\rho\cdot\mu}\Delta(q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}})^{-1}\,{\mathcal{D}}_{m}(q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}};q,t)\,\Delta(q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}})\,t^{-\rho\cdot\mu}\right)P_{\mu}^{*}(q^{\lambda}\,t^{\rho}).

Acting with the involution
*
on the parameters and
interchanging the roles of
\lambda
and
\mu
(i.e.
x
and
s
) above, we arrive at the following Pieri rules.

Theorem 3.3
.

The Pieri rules for the Koornwinder polynomials are

(3.17)

{\mathcal{H}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;q,t)\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)={\hat{e}}_{m}(x)\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x),

where the Pieri operators
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;q,t)
are

(3.18)

{\mathcal{H}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;q,t)=\frac{1}{\theta_{m}^{*}}t^{\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\,\Delta^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s)^{-1}\,{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s;q,t)\,\Delta^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s)\,t^{-\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}.

In the difference operators
\mathcal{H}_{m}
,
s
is specialized to
q^{\lambda}\,t^{\rho}
with
\lambda
an integer partition,
\theta_{m}^{*}=a^{m}t^{m(N-\frac{m+1}{2})}
, and
T_{i}:\lambda_{j}\mapsto\lambda_{j}+\delta_{i,j}
.

The explicit first Pieri operator
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;q,t)
is derived in Theorem
B.1
of Appendix
B
.

Specialization of the Koornwinder parameters

, except for

.

3.2.1.
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators and polynomials

The Macdonald operators for type
{\mathfrak{g}}
are a set of
N
commuting operators which commute with the specialization of the Koornwinder operators at values of
(a,b,c,d)
indicated in Table
1
. The list of operators is given in Definition
A.8
. In most cases, these are just the specialized Koornwinder-Macdonald operators, but for a few exceptions, where the operators are chosen so that their eigenvalues be fundamental characters of the finite-dimensional algebra
R^{*}=R({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})
. This occurs when
R^{*}=B_{N}
or
D_{N}
.

Remark 3.4
.

The case of
A_{2N}^{(2)}
is special, as
R=BC_{N}=R^{*}
is non-reduced. In this case, we must use the set of simple roots
{\alpha}_{a}={\alpha}_{a}^{*}
for
B_{N}
,
and the set of fundamental weights
\omega_{a}=\omega_{a}^{*}
for
C_{N}
.

The principle for the choice of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators is that their spectrum generates
the
Grothendieck ring of the algebra
R^{*}
associated to each
{\mathfrak{g}}
. The choice is not unique.
Define

(3.19)

N_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}N,&{\rm for}\ {\mathfrak{g}}=A_{N-1}^{(1)},B_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},A_{2N}^{(2)},\\
N-1,&{\rm for}\ {\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)},\\
N-2,&{\rm for}\ {\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)}.\end{array}\right.

R

\hat{e}_{a}^{(R)}(x)

D_{N}

\hat{e}_{1},...,\hat{e}_{N-2},\hat{e}_{N-1}^{(D_{N})},\hat{e}_{N}^{(D_{N})}

B_{N}

\hat{e}_{1},\hat{e}_{2},...,\hat{e}_{N-1},\hat{e}_{N}^{(B_{N})}

C_{N}
,
BC_{N}

\hat{e}_{1},\hat{e}_{2},...,\hat{e}_{N}

Table 2.
The list of chosen symmetric functions forming a basis for the fundamental characters of the finite Lie algebra
R
. The exceptional symmetric functions in the table are given in (
A.14
).

Definition 3.5
.

For
m\leq N_{\mathfrak{g}}
,
the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}={\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
are

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}:={\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)},\qquad m\in[1,N_{\mathfrak{g}}],

where
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}
are as in Definition
3.2
with

(a,b,c,d)
specialized according Table
1
. For
N_{\mathfrak{g}}<m\leq N
, we use the operators constructed by Macdonald,
described in Section
A.1
:

(3.20)

\displaystyle D_{N}^{(1)}:\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{a}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}:={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{a}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})},\qquad a=N-1,N;

(3.21)

\displaystyle C_{N}^{(1)}:\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(C_{N}^{(1)})}:={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(C_{N}^{(1)})},

(3.22)

\displaystyle D_{N+1}^{(2)}:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(D^{(2)}_{N+1})}:={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(D^{(2)}_{N+1})}.

where
{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{a}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})},{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(C_{N}^{(1)})},{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(D^{(2)}_{N+1})}
are as in (
A.4
-
A.5
),(
A.6
),(
A.7
) respectively.

The choice of higher commuting difference operators (
3.5
) is such that the eigenvalue equation for the
m
th
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operator is (see Theorem
A.15
):

(3.23)

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q,t)P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,{\hat{e}}_{m}^{(R^{*})}(s)\,P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),\qquad

where
\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
are as in (
A.34
-
A.35
) and
{\hat{e}}_{m}^{(R)}(x)
are listed in Table
2
(as these involve the
fundamental weights we choose the same eigenvalues for type
BC_{N}
and
C_{N}
, see Remark
3.4
).

In particular, the first
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators are simply the specialization of the Koornwinder-Macdonald operator (
3.4
).

(3.24)

{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q,t)=t^{\rho_{1}^{\mathfrak{g}}}\hat{e}_{1}(t^{\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q,t)(\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}-1).

The factor
\sigma
under the specialization is
t^{\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}}
(see Table
1
), so that

(3.25)

s_{i}=q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{N+\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}-i}=q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{\rho_{i}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}},\qquad(i=1,2,...,N),

where
\rho^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=\rho(S)
is the half-sum of positive roots of
S
(except when
{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N}^{(2)}
).
The functions
\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q,t)
are

\displaystyle\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q,t)=\prod_{j\neq i}\prod_{\epsilon^{\prime}=\pm 1}\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}-1}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}-1}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}1,&{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)};\\
\displaystyle{\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}-1}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-1}},&{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)};\\
\displaystyle{\frac{tx_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1}{x_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1}\frac{tqx_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1}{qx_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1}},&{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)};\\
\displaystyle{\frac{tx_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1}{x_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1}},&{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N-1}^{(2)};\\
\displaystyle{\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}-1}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-1}\frac{tq^{1/2}x_{i}^{\epsilon}-1}{q^{1/2}x_{i}^{\epsilon}-1}},&{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N+1}^{(2)};\\
\displaystyle{\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}-1}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-1}\frac{tqx_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1}{qx_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1}},&{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N}^{(2)}.\\
\end{array}\right.

The unique monic eigenfunction
P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
of
D_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q,t)
with eigenvalue
t^{\rho^{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}}\hat{e}_{1}(s)
, where
\lambda
is
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition, is the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-type Macdonald (Laurent) polynomial.

Definition 3.6
.

A
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition is a set
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{N})
such that
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}e_{i}
is a dominant integral weight of
R=R({\mathfrak{g}})
.

The
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partitions are simply integer partitions except in the cases
R=B_{N},D_{N}
, where

\displaystyle\lambda\in\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\{({\mathbb{Z}}_{+})^{N-1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}\right\}\cup\left\{({\mathbb{Z}}_{+}\!\!+\!\!{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2}})^{N-1}\times({\mathbb{Z}}+\!\!{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2}})\right\},\ \lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{N-1}\geq|\lambda_{N}|,&R=D_{N};\\
\\
({\mathbb{Z}}_{+})^{N}\cup({\mathbb{Z}}_{+}\!\!+\!\!{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2}})^{N},\ \lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{N}\geq 0,&R=B_{N}.\end{array}\right.

Remark 3.7
.

The
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald polynomial
P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
with
\lambda
an integer partition is the specialization of the Koornwinder polynomial, indexed by the same partition, to the parameters of Table
1
.
For non-integer partitions,
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald polynomials can be obtained from a different specialization of the parameters
[
vD95
]
, see Section
4.3.3
in type
B
. Alternatively, we can use the specialization of the
universal functions of Section
5
, see remark
5.2
.

3.2.2.
Duality

The involution
*
acting on the parameters
(a,b,c,d)
implies an involution
{\mathfrak{g}}\mapsto{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
and
R\mapsto R^{*}
, as listed in Table
1
. In particular,
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
except in the cases
(C_{N}^{(1)})^{*}=B_{N}^{(1)}
and
(B_{N}^{(1)})^{*}=C_{N}^{(1)}
.

The duality relation (
3.12
), specialized to
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald polynomials is

(3.28)

\frac{P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}})}{P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{\lambda}(t^{\rho^{*}})}=\frac{P^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}_{\mu}(q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})}{P^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}_{\mu}(t^{\rho})}.

Note, however, that the range of validity of (
3.28
) is wider, as
\lambda
can be any
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition,
while
\mu
is any
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
-partition, and these are not necessarily integer partitions as in (
3.12
).
First conjectured by Macdonald, the duality (
3.28
) was successively proved for all types in the case of integer partitions:
for type A, it appears in
[
Mac95
]
, for other types the main proof is in
[
Che95b
]
,
supplemented by
[
vD96
]
and
[
Sah99
]
upon specialization of (
3.12
). See Section
5
for a more general duality statement.

G

affine roots in
\widehat{R}_{++}

D_{N}^{(1)}

n\delta+(e_{i}\pm e_{j})\quad(1\leq i<j\leq N;n\geq 1)

B_{N}^{(1)}

n\delta+e_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq N
),
n\delta+(e_{i}\pm e_{j})\ (1\leq i<j\leq N;n\geq 1)

C_{N}^{(1)}

n\delta+2e_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq N
),
n\delta+(e_{i}\pm e_{j})\ (1\leq i<j\leq N;n\geq 1)

A_{2N-1}^{(2)}

2n\delta+2e_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq N
),
n\delta+(e_{i}\pm e_{j})\quad(1\leq i<j\leq N;n\geq 1)

D_{N+1}^{(2)}

\frac{n}{2}\delta+e_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq N
),
n\delta+(e_{i}\pm e_{j})\quad(1\leq i<j\leq N;n\geq 1)

A_{2N}^{(2)}

n\delta+e_{i}
,
(2n-1)\delta+2e_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq N
),
n\delta+(e_{i}\pm e_{j})\quad(1\leq i<j\leq N;n\geq 1)

Table 3.
The subset
\widehat{R}_{++}
of the affine roots for each affine algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

For
\alpha=n\delta+\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{N}\in\widehat{R}
, define
x^{\alpha}=q^{-n}x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{\alpha_{N}}
. The function
\Delta
in Equation (
3.14
) has the following specializations:

(3.29)

\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\prod_{\alpha\in\widehat{R}_{++}}\frac{1-x^{-\alpha}}{1-t^{-1}x^{-\alpha}},

where
\widehat{R}_{++}=\{\alpha+n\delta\in\widehat{R}:\alpha\in R_{+},n>0\}
is a subset of the affine roots of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, see
Table
3
.
In all cases but
{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N}^{(2)}
,
\Delta
is closely related to a simplified version of Macdonald’s function
\Delta^{+}

[
Mac01
]
, suitable for taking the dual
q
-Whittaker limit
t\to\infty
.
The function
\Delta
enters the duality relation (
3.28
) via the evaluation formulas (see (0.6) in
[
Che95b
]
)

(3.30)

P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{\lambda}(t^{\rho^{*}})=t^{\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\frac{\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}(t^{\rho})}{\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}(q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})},\quad P^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}_{\mu}(t^{\rho})=t^{\rho\cdot\mu}\frac{\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(t^{\rho^{*}})}{\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}})}.

3.2.3.
Pieri rules

As in the case of generic
(a,b,c,d)
, the Pieri rules follow from the eigenvalue equations (
3.23
) and the duality (
3.28
). Start with (
3.23
) with
x=q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}}
, and specialize
\mu
to a
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
-partition:

(3.31)

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}};q,t)P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}})=\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,{\hat{e}}_{m}^{(R^{*})}(q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})\,P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(q^{\mu}\,t^{\rho^{*}}),

where
(x,\Gamma)
is specialized to
(q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}},e^{\partial_{\mu}})
.
Following the same steps as in Section
3.1.5
, using the duality relation (
3.28
), one obtains the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Pieri formulas:

Theorem 3.8
.

The Pieri rules for
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald polynomials are

(3.32)

{\mathcal{H}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;q,t)\,P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)={\hat{e}}_{m}^{(R)}(x)\,P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),

where the difference operators
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;q,t)
are given in terms of
{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}_{m}(s;q,t)
:

(3.33)

{\mathcal{H}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;q,t)=\frac{1}{\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}}\,t^{\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\,\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}(s)^{-1}\,{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}(s;q,t)\,\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}(s)\,t^{-\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}.

The explicit Pieri operators
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;q,t)
are listed in Section
B.2
.

3.3.
The
q
-Whittaker limit

The Macdonald polynomials have a symmetry
(t,q)\mapsto(t^{-1},q^{-1})
. For certain root systems, the
q
-Whittaker polynomials are the
t\to 0
limit of the Macdonald polynomials, and therefore the
q^{-1}
-Whittaker polynomials are the
t\to\infty
limit of these polynomials. In this paper, we define the various functions and operators so that they have well-defined limits as
t\to\infty
. We refer to this as the
q
-Whittaker limit
by slight abuse of terminology. We also call
q
-Whittaker polynomials what technically should be called
q^{-1}
-Whittaker polynomials.

3.3.1.
Whittaker difference operators

The eigenvalue equations and Pieri rules of the previous section have well-defined
q
-Whittaker limits. Define

(3.34)

\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

and

(3.35)

D_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q)=\lim_{t\to\infty}(\theta_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{-2}\,{\mathcal{D}}_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q,t),\qquad(a=1,2,...,N).

The first
q
-Whittaker difference operators are obtained from Equation (
3.24
):

(3.36)

D_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q)=1+\sum_{i=1\atop\epsilon=\pm 1}^{N}\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)(\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}-1),

with

\displaystyle\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{x_{i}^{\epsilon}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}\,\frac{x_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}-1}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&(D_{N}^{(1)});\\
\frac{x_{i}^{\epsilon}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-1},&(B_{N}^{(1)}),\\
\frac{x_{i}^{2\epsilon}}{x_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1}\,\frac{qx_{i}^{2\epsilon}}{qx_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1},&(C_{N}^{(1)}),\\
\frac{x_{i}^{2\epsilon}}{x_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1},&(A_{2N-1}^{(2)}),\\
\frac{x_{i}^{\epsilon}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-1}\,\frac{q^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{i}^{\epsilon}}{q^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{i}^{\epsilon}-1},&(D_{N+1}^{(2)}),\\
\frac{x_{i}^{\epsilon}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-1}\,\frac{qx_{i}^{2\epsilon}}{qx_{i}^{2\epsilon}-1},&(A_{2N}^{(2)}).\end{array}\right.

The limiting eigenvalues of
D_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q)
are not symmetric functions because of the dependence of
s
on
t
. Instead, they are given by the dominant term in
t
in the functions
\hat{e}^{(R^{*})}_{a}(s)
,
\Lambda^{\omega_{a}^{*}}
.
The eigenvalue equations are (see Section
A.4
, Theorem
A.18
)

(3.44)

D_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x).

3.3.2.
Pieri rules and Toda Hamiltonians

The Pieri operators (
3.33
) have well-defined limits as
t\to\infty
. Let

(3.45)

H_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};q):=\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathcal{H}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s={\Lambda}t^{\rho^{({\mathfrak{g}})}};q,t).

Using the Pieri operators from Section
B.2
(see also Remark
B.3
), and taking the
t\to\infty
limit, we obtain the following list of first Pieri operators.

Theorem 3.9
.

For all
{\mathfrak{g}}
, the first Pieri operators take the form

(3.46)

H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(\Lambda;q)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{i-1}^{*}})T_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{R*}}(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{i}^{*}})T_{i}^{-1}+M^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(\Lambda;q),

where the roots
{\alpha}_{i}^{*}
are the simple roots of
R^{*}

and
{\alpha}_{0}^{*}=0
by convention.
Here,
N_{R^{*}}=N-1
if
R^{*}=B_{N}
,
N-2
if
R^{*}=D_{N}
, and is equal to
N
otherwise.
The boundary terms
M^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(\Lambda;q)
are as follows:

(3.47)

M^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(\Lambda;q)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N}^{*}})(T_{N}^{-1}+(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N-1}^{*}})T_{N-1}^{-1}),&{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},\\
(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N}^{*}})(1-q\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N}^{*}})T_{N}^{-1}+\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N}^{*}}(q^{-1}\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N-1}^{*}}-(1+q^{-1})),&{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},\\
0,&{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},\\
(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N}^{*}})(1-q^{\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N}^{*}})T_{N}^{-1},&{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N+1}^{(2)},\\
-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{N}^{*}},&{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N}^{(2)}.\end{array}\right.

Explicitly, the first Pieri operators are:

\displaystyle H_{1}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}({\Lambda})=T_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{i-1}}\right)T_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-2}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i+1}}{{\Lambda}_{i}}\right)T_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{N}}{{\Lambda}_{N-1}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N-1}{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)T_{N-1}^{-1}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N-1}{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)T_{N}^{-1},

\displaystyle H_{1}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}({\Lambda})=T_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{i-1}}\right)T_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i+1}}{{\Lambda}_{i}}\right)T_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}}\right)\left(1-\frac{q}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}}\right)T_{N}^{-1}+\frac{q^{-1}}{{\Lambda}_{N-1}{\Lambda}_{N}}-\frac{1+q^{-1}}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}},

\displaystyle H_{1}^{(C_{N}^{(1)})}({\Lambda})=T_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{i-1}}\right)T_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i+1}}{{\Lambda}_{i}}\right)T_{i}^{-1}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)T_{N}^{-1},

\displaystyle H_{1}^{(A_{2N-1}^{(2)})}({\Lambda})=T_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{i-1}}\right)T_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i+1}}{{\Lambda}_{i}}\right)T_{i}^{-1}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}}\right)T_{N}^{-1},

\displaystyle H_{1}^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}({\Lambda})=T_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{i-1}}\right)T_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i+1}}{{\Lambda}_{i}}\right)T_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\left(1-\frac{q^{\frac{1}{2}}}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)T_{N}^{-1}-\frac{1+q^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{{\Lambda}_{N}},

\displaystyle H_{1}^{(A_{2N}^{(2)})}({\Lambda})=T_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{i-1}}\right)T_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i+1}}{{\Lambda}_{i}}\right)T_{i}^{-1}+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)T_{N}^{-1}-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}.

The first
q
-Whittaker Pieri rule is

(3.48)

H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)={\hat{e}}_{1}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x).

Alternatively this equation can be interpreted as the eigenvector equation for
q
-Whittaker functions
in which the roles of variables
{\Lambda}
and
x
are interchanged.

Remark 3.10
.

The Pieri operators
H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(\Lambda;q)
match the relativistic
U_{q}(R)
-Toda Hamiltonians acting on functions of
\Lambda

[
vDE15
,
GT21
]
upon the following correspondence:

D_{N}^{(1)}\to D_{N},\ \ D_{N+1}^{(2)}\to B_{N},\ \ A_{2N-1}^{(2)}\to C_{N}.

The equivalence uses the Etingof automorphism
[
Eti99
]

T_{i}\mapsto T_{2\varpi_{i}}
,
{\Lambda}_{i}\mapsto{\Lambda}_{i}T_{-\varpi_{i}}
and
v=q^{\frac{1}{2}}
in the notations of
[
GT21
]
.
To our knowledge, the cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
do not appear in the literature in relation to
standard constructions of
q
-Whittaker functions for quantum groups. By a slight abuse of terminology, we still call the corresponding limits of Macdonald polynomials
q
-Whittaker functions, and the limiting Pieri operators Toda Hamiltonians, and keep our labeling
with (twisted) affine algebras to avoid confusion. The same correspondence in the
q=1
limit occurs in relation to factorization dynamics
[
Wil15
]
, where the
B_{N},C_{N}

cases match classical Q-system evolutions for the twisted algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N+1}^{(2)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)}
respectively.

3.3.3.
Generalized
q
-Whittaker difference operators

In this section, we consider the
\tau_{+}\in SL(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-action on the
q
-Whittaker difference operators of Section
3.3.1
.
Let
\gamma(x)
be as in Equation (
2.16
). We will define “translated” operators
D^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{a;n}
for all
a=1,2,...,N
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
by suitable use of iterated conjugations with
\gamma^{-1}
. There is a subtlety arising from a distinction according to whether
a
is a long or short label.
These do not necessarily correspond to long and short roots of
R
, but are determined instead by the Q-system evolutions described in the next section.

Definition 3.11
.

All labels
a\in[1,N]
are long except for the following cases:

a=N
for
B_{N}^{(1)}
,
a\in[1,N-1]
for
C_{N}^{(1)}
, and
a\in[1,N]
for
A_{2N}^{(2)}
.

In general, we think of the integer
n
as a discrete time, and as
{\rm Ad}_{\gamma^{-1}}

as a “time translation” operator.

Definition of
D_{1;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
and properties.

We start with the definition of the operators
D_{1;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, which depends on whether

a=1
is a long or short label.

Definition 3.12
.

When the label
a=1
is long (
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},B_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}
),
define, for any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

(3.49)

D_{1;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x):=q^{-\frac{n}{2}}\,\gamma^{-n}\,D_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q)\,\gamma^{n}=q^{-\frac{n}{2}}+\sum_{i,\epsilon}\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,(x_{i}^{n\epsilon}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}-q^{-\frac{n}{2}}),

with
\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
as in (
3.3.1
).

Definition 3.13
.

When the label
a=1
is short (
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
) define

(3.50)

D_{1;-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\sum_{i,\epsilon}\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,x_{i}^{-\epsilon}(\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}-1),

with
\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
as in (
3.3.1
).
Then for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and for
i=0,-1
, define

(3.51)

D_{1;2n+i}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=q^{-n}\,\gamma^{-2n}\,D_{1,i}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q)\,\gamma^{2n},

where
D^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{1,0}(x;q)=D^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{1}(x;q)
.

Conjugating (
3.36
) and (
3.50
) by
\gamma^{-2n}
, we have, when
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
,

(3.52)

\displaystyle D_{1;2n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-n}+\sum_{i,\epsilon}\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,(x_{i}^{2n\epsilon}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}-q^{-n}),

(3.53)

\displaystyle D_{1;2n-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\epsilon}\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,x_{i}^{-\epsilon}(x_{i}^{2n\epsilon}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}-q^{-n}).

The main difference between the cases of Definitions
3.12
and
3.13
is that in the latter case the translation splits
into distinct even and odd time evolution equations (
3.52
-
3.53
).

Lemma 3.14
.

We have the commutation relation

(3.54)

[D_{1;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),{\hat{e}}_{1}(x)]=(q-1)\,D_{1;n+1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)+(q^{-1}-1)\,D_{1;n-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x).

Proof.

Using

[\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon},\hat{e}_{1}(x)]=(q^{\epsilon}-1)x_{i}^{\epsilon}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}+(q^{-\epsilon}-1)x_{i}^{-\epsilon}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}=\sum_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(q^{\epsilon^{\prime}}-1)x^{\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}}\Gamma^{\epsilon}.

we have

\displaystyle[D_{1,n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})},e_{1}^{(x)}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\phi_{i,\epsilon}x^{n\epsilon}[\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon},\hat{e}_{1}(x)]=\sum_{i,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}\phi_{i,\epsilon}x^{(n+\epsilon^{\prime})\epsilon}(q^{\epsilon^{\prime}}-1)\Gamma^{\epsilon}.

∎

Definition of higher generalized
q
-Whittaker operators.

The higher, generalized
q
-Whittaker operators are denoted by
D_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, with
a\in[1,N]
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
They are defined as follows.

Definition 3.15
.

For all
a\in[1,N]
and for all
n
,

D_{a,t_{a}n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=q^{-nt_{a}\omega^{*}_{a}\cdot\omega_{a}/2}\gamma^{-t_{1}n}D_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\gamma^{t_{1}n},

where
D_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
are defined in (
3.35
) and
\omega_{a}

(\omega_{a}^{*})
are fundamental weights of
R
(
R^{*}
),
t_{a}=2
for short labels and
t_{a}=1
for long labels in Definition
3.11
.

Definition 3.16
.

For the short labels
a\geq 2
of
C_{N}^{(1)}
and
A_{2N}^{(2)}
, define

(3.55)

D_{a;-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}:=\frac{(-1)^{a}}{q-1}[D_{1;-a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})},D_{a-1;0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}]_{q^{a}},\qquad(n\in{\mathbb{Z}}),

where
[A,B]_{p}=[A,B]_{p}=AB-pBA
. Then

D_{a;2n-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=q^{-na}\,\gamma^{-2n}\,D_{a;-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,\gamma^{2n}=\frac{(-1)^{a}}{q-1}[D_{1;2n-a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})},D_{a-1;2n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}]_{q^{a}}.

Definition
3.16
anticipates on the desired connection to the quantum Q-systems of the next section and uses a recursive reformulation of the quantum Q-system solution obtained in
[
DFK17b
]
.

Finally, to define the generalized difference operators with odd
n
corresponding to the label
a=N
,
{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)}
, we use
the Rains operators
{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(u,v)}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(a,b,c,d)}
of (
3.7
) and (
3.9
), with the specialization
(a,b,c,d)=(t,-1,q^{1/2},-q^{1/2})
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(t,-1,q^{1/2},-q^{1/2})}={\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(1)}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(0)}

with

{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(1)}={\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(t,-1)},\qquad{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(0)}={\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(1,-1)}.

In the
q
-Whittaker limit
t\to\infty
, these become

(3.56)

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{N}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-N^{2}}\,\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}=R_{N}^{(1)}\,R_{N}^{(0)},

with

(3.57)

\displaystyle\qquad R_{N}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-N(N-1)/2}{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(1,-1)}=\sum_{\epsilon_{1},...,\epsilon_{N}=\pm 1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}-1}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}/2}

(3.58)

\displaystyle R_{N}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-N(N+1)/2}{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(t,-1)}=\sum_{\epsilon_{1},...,\epsilon_{N}=\pm 1}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}-1}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}/2}.

The factorized form of
\widehat{D}_{N}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}
can be used to define the discrete time evolution
as follows:

Definition 3.17
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Define

\displaystyle R_{N;n}^{(i)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{Nn}{8}}\gamma^{-n}\,R_{N}^{(i)}\,\gamma^{n},\qquad(i=0,1),

(3.59)

\displaystyle D^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}_{N;2n-1}

\displaystyle=
