\displaystyle q^{-\frac{Nn}{4}}\,R_{N;n-1}^{(1)}\,R_{N;n}^{(0)}.

Note in particular that
D^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}_{N;2n-1}=q^{-\frac{nN}{2}}\,\gamma^{-n}\,D_{N;-1}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}\gamma^{n}
, similarly to the even
n
case
where
D^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}_{N;2n}=q^{-\frac{nN}{2}}\,\gamma^{-n}\,D_{N;0}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}\gamma^{n}
.

4.
Quantum Q-systems and Macdonald operator conjecture

For any affine algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, there is a corresponding Q-system
[
HKO+99
,
HKO+02
]
, which is a recursion relation satisfied by the characters of the KR-modules
[
CM06
]
of the Yangian
Y({\mathfrak{g}})
. Their deformation into recursion relations for non-commuting variables, called quantum Q-systems, were first defined
[
DFK14
]
using the identification of the Q-system recursion relations as mutations in a cluster algebra. In that case, one may use the canonical quantization of the cluster algebra
[
BZ05
]
, and in
[
DFK14
,
Lin21
,
Lin
]
it is shown that this quantization is related to the graded fusion characters for
{\mathfrak{g}}
. In special cases, these are known to be related to
q
-Whittaker functions.

One of the main conjectures presented in
[
DFK21
]
is that for untwisted
{\mathfrak{g}}
in Table
1
,
some
q
-difference operators

D_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
satisfy
the quantum Q-system relations of type
{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
that the operators

D_{a;1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
and
D_{a;-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
act as raising and lowering operators when acting
on the eigenfunctions
\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\mathbf{x}})
of
D_{a;0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
. The purpose of this section is to prove these statements for all
{\mathfrak{g}}
of Table
1
.

4.1.
Statement of the main theorems

4.1.1.
Quantum Q-systems

For each
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we consider an algebra generated by invertible, non-commuting elements
\{Q_{a;n}^{\pm 1}:a\in[1,N],n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
. The quantum Q-system of type
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a set of relations among these generators, which depend on the root data of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Definition 4.1
.

For each
{\mathfrak{g}}
, let
\Lambda^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
be the
N\times N
matrix defined by

(4.1)

\Lambda^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{a,b}=\omega_{a}^{*}\cdot\omega_{b},\qquad a,b\in[1,N],

where
\omega_{a}
and
\omega_{a}^{*}
are the fundamental weights of type
R
and
R^{*}
respectively,
and
\cdot
is the standard inner product.

In the case of untwisted
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\Lambda^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
is the inverse of the Cartan matrix
C
of
R
,
since
\omega_{a}^{*}=\omega_{a}^{\vee}
.
For the case
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N+1}^{(2)}
,
\Lambda^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
is the inverse of the
symmetrized
Cartan matrix of type
B_{N}
, and for
{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{M}^{(2)}
, it is the inverse of the symmetrized Cartan matrix of type
C_{\lfloor\frac{M+1}{2}\rfloor}
:

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}_{a,b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(D_{a,a})^{-1}\,\Lambda^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}_{a,b}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\min(a,b),&(a,b<N)\\
\frac{1}{2}\min(a,b),&(a=N,\,{\rm or}\,b=N)\\
\frac{1}{4}N,&(a=b=N),\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(A_{2N-1}^{(2)})}_{a,b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(A_{2N}^{(2)})}_{a,b}=(D^{\prime}_{a,a})^{-1}\,\Lambda^{(C_{N}^{(1)})}_{a,b}=\min(a,b).

Here,
D={\rm diag}(1,1,...,1,2)
and
D^{\prime}=D^{-1}
are such that
C=C^{(B_{N})}D
and
C=C^{(C_{N})}D^{\prime}
are symmetric. These are chosen so that
\Lambda^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{1,1}=1
.
Defining
t_{a}=2
for
\alpha_{a}
a short simple root of
R
,
t_{a}=1
for
\alpha_{a}
long,
t_{1}/t_{a}
is
D_{a,a}
for type
B

and
D^{\prime}_{a,a}
for type
C
.

The quantum Q-system relations are of two types. The first are
q
-commutation relations:

(4.3)

{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;t_{a}k+i}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{b;t_{b}k+j}=q^{\Lambda^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{a,b}\,j-\Lambda^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{b,a}\,i}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{b;t_{b}k+j}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;t_{a}k+i},\qquad(i,j=0,1,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}).

The second type of relations are evolution equations in the discrete time variable
n
, and
have the form
q^{\Lambda_{aa}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n-1}={\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n}^{2}-\mathcal{T}_{a;n}
for some monomials
\mathcal{T}_{a;n}
.
Let
\bar{N}_{\mathfrak{g}}
be the maximal integer such that
({\Lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})})_{a,a}=a
for
a\leq\bar{N}_{\mathfrak{g}}+1
:

\bar{N}_{D_{N}^{(1)}}=N-3,\quad\bar{N}_{\mathfrak{g}}=N-2,\ ({\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},C_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}),\quad\bar{N}_{{\mathfrak{g}}}=N-1,\ ({\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N}^{(2)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)}).

The evolution equations are

(4.4)

q^{a}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n-1}={\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n}^{2}-{\mathcal{Q}}_{a+1;n}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{a-1;n},\qquad a\in[1,\bar{N}_{\mathfrak{g}}],\hbox{ all ${\mathfrak{g}}$}.

(4.5)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}D_{N}^{(1)}:\ \ \ q^{N-2}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-2;n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-2;n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-2;n}^{2}-q^{-\frac{(N-2)n}{4}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-3;n}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n},\\
q^{\frac{N}{4}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n}^{2}-q^{\frac{(N-4)n}{4}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-2;n},\\
q^{\frac{N}{4}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n}^{2}-q^{\frac{(N-4)n}{4}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-2;n},\\
B_{N}^{(1)}:\ \ \ q^{N-1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n}^{2}-{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-2;n}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n},\\
q^{\frac{N}{2}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n}^{2}-q^{-n}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n}^{2},\\
q^{\frac{N}{2}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n+2}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n+1}^{2}-q^{\frac{N}{2}-n-1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n},\\
C_{N}^{(1)}:\ q^{N-1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;2n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;2n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;2n}^{2}-q^{-\frac{Nn}{2}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-2;2n}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n}^{2},\\
q^{N-1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;2n+2}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;2n}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;2n+1}^{2}-q^{-\frac{Nn}{2}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-2;2n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n},\\
q^{\frac{N}{2}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n}^{2}-q^{\frac{(N-2)n}{2}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;2n},\\
D_{N+1}^{(2)}:\ q^{N-1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n}^{2}-q^{-\frac{Nn}{4}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-2;n}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n}^{2},\\
q^{\frac{N}{4}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n}^{2}-q^{\frac{(N-2)n}{4}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n},\\
A_{2N-1}^{(2)}:\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ q^{N}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;n}^{2}-q^{-n}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n}^{2},\\
A_{2N}^{(2)}:\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ q^{N}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n-1}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n}^{2}-q^{-n}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;2n}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n},\\
q^{N}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N,2n+2}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N,2n}&=&{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n+1}^{2}-q^{-n}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;2n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n+1}.\end{array}

Remark 4.2
.

If
{\mathfrak{g}}\neq A_{2N}^{(2)}
, the evolution equations above
are equivalent to the quantization of the Q-system
cluster algebras. These correspond to exchange matrices
[
Ked08
,
DFK09
,
Wil15
]
:

B=\begin{pmatrix}C^{t}-C&-C^{t}\\
C&0\end{pmatrix}
for untwisted
{\mathfrak{g}}
and

B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-C\\
C&0\end{pmatrix}
for twisted
{\mathfrak{g}}
, where
C
is the Cartan matrix of
R
. These correspond to the initial cluster data
(Q_{a;i}:a\in[1,N],i=0,1)
.
We choose a skew-symmetric
q
-commutation matrix to be the inverse of the skew-symmetrized matrix
B
,
of the form
\begin{pmatrix}0&\Lambda\\
-\Lambda^{t}&\Lambda^{t}-\Lambda\end{pmatrix}
with

{\Lambda}={\Lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
as in Definition
4.1
.
The quantized cluster variables obey the commutation relations (
4.3
), and
the relevant quantum mutations
[
DFK09
]
are

(4.6)

q^{\Lambda^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{a,a}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n+1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n-1}=({\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n})^{2}-q^{\frac{1}{2}D_{a,a}^{-1}}:\mathcal{\mathcal{}}T_{a;n}:\qquad(a=1,2,...,N),

with
D_{a,a}=1
except for
a=N
in types
A_{2N-1}^{(2)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}
, where it is equal to
\frac{1}{2},2
, respectively. The monomial
T_{a;k}
is the product of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{b;k^{\prime}}
appearing as the second term in the right hand side of each Q-system relation, not including any factors of
q
,
and the normal ordering
:\cdot:
is defined as

:\prod X_{i}^{\mu_{i}}{:}=q^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i<j}a_{i,j}\mu_{i}\mu_{j}}X_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots X_{k}^{\mu_{k}}
if

X_{i}X_{j}=q^{a_{i,j}}X_{j}X_{i}
.
The quantum Q-system relations (
4.4
-
4.5
) are equivalent to the quantum mutations (
4.6
) after a renormalization of the cluster variables
(see
[
DFK21
]
Lemma 4.4).

In this section, we prove the following Theorem, which is one of the main results of this paper:

Theorem 4.3
.

For each
{\mathfrak{g}}
in Table
1
,
the limit
t\to\infty
of the generalized
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators
D_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x,q)
of Section
3.3.3
satisfy the corresponding quantum Q-system relations (
4.3
-
4.5
).

Therefore the algebra generated by the elements
{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;k}
subject to the quantum Q-system relations has a functional representation given by the difference operators of Section
3.3.3
.

4.1.2.
Raising/lowering operator conditions

We refer to the polynomials
\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{\lambda}(x)
(
3.34
), which are the common eigenfuctions of

D_{a;0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q)=D_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q)
, as
q
-Whittaker polynomials.
For non-twisted
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we conjectured in
[
DFK21
]
that
D_{a;1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
,
D_{a;-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
are raising and lowering operators acting on
\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
. The following are the statement of this result for all
{\mathfrak{g}}
in Table
1
, and will be proven in this Section:

Theorem 4.4
.

For any
{\mathfrak{g}}
in Table
1
,

(4.7)

\displaystyle D_{a;0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),

(4.8)

\displaystyle D_{a;1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,\Pi_{\lambda+\omega_{a}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),

where
\omega_{a}
and
\omega_{a}^{*}
are fundamental weights of
R
or
R^{*}
, respectively.

Combining Theorem
4.4
and Theorem
4.3
with the relevant quantum Q-system relations, it follows that
D_{a;-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
is a lowering operator:

Corollary 4.5
.

For all
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we have the following lowering operator conditions

(4.9)

D_{a;-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)={\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}^{*}})\,\Pi_{\lambda-\omega_{a}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),

where
{\alpha}_{a}^{*}
are the simple roots of
R^{*}
.

Note that the prefactor guarantees that the result is
0
whenever
\lambda-\omega_{a}
is not a dominant integral
R
-weight, i.e. not a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition.

The proof follows the steps of Section
2
, using the Fourier transformed operators
\bar{D}_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
.

4.2.
Opposite quantum Q-systems and integrability

For each
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we consider the solutions
\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n}
which satisfy the quantum Q-system with the opposite multiplication, referred to as the quantum
\bar{\rm Q}
-system.
We construct a representation
\bar{D}_{a;n}(\Lambda)
of the solutions
\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n}
of the quantum
\bar{\rm Q}
-system, subject to appropriate initial data, in terms of
q
-difference operators written in terms of the quantum torus
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Lambda}=\langle{\Lambda}_{i},T_{i}\rangle_{i=1}^{N}
. Elements in this algebra act on functions of
\Lambda
as
q
-difference operators in
\Lambda
.
The
initial data
\bar{D}_{a;0}({\Lambda})
are deduced from the eigenvalue equation (
4.7
).
These are supplemented with a choice
of
\bar{D}_{a;1}({\Lambda})

ensuring that the quantum commutation relations opposite to those of Eq. (
4.3
) are satisfied:

(4.10)

\bar{D}_{a;0}\,\bar{D}_{b;1}=q^{-{\Lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{a,b}}\,\bar{D}_{b;1}\,\bar{D}_{a;0},

with
{\Lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
as in (
4.1
).

Definition 4.6
.

For all
{\mathfrak{g}}
, define

\bar{D}_{a;0}:=\bar{D}_{a;0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}^{*}},\qquad\bar{D}_{a;1}:=\bar{D}_{a;1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,T^{\omega_{a}},

where
\omega_{a},\omega_{a}^{*}
are fundamental weights of
R,R^{*}
.

These obey the relations (
4.10
), since

{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}T^{\omega_{b}}=q^{-\omega_{a}^{*}\cdot\omega_{b}}T^{\omega_{b}}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}
.
Since all Q-system evolutions are two-step recursion relations, the following are uniquely defined:

Definition 4.7
.

Define
\bar{D}_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})=\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n}
for all
n\neq 0,1
to be the solutions of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-type quantum
\bar{\rm Q}
-system relations subject to the initial data in Definition
4.6
.

Remark 4.8
.

Due to the Laurent property of quantum cluster algebras
[
BZ05
]
, the solutions
\bar{D}_{a;n}
are Laurent in the initial data
\{\bar{D}_{a;i}:i=0,1,a\in[1,N]\}
. Since these are monomials in
\{T_{a},\Lambda_{a},a\in[1,N]\}
, all quantum cluster variables are Laurent in the quantum torus generators, therefore they
are
q
-difference operators.
Although this argument doesn’t apply to
{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N}^{(2)}
, we will show that all solutions of the
\bar{\rm Q}
-system are Laurent in this case also.

4.2.1.
Time translation operator
g

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
\bar{D}_{a,n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
be as in Definition
4.7
.
For each
{\mathfrak{g}}
there exists an element
g=g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
in a completion of the quantum torus
{\mathbb{T}}_{\Lambda}
, such that for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
:

(4.11)

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a,n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{n}{2}{\Lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{a,a}}\,g^{n}\,\bar{D}_{a,0}\,g^{-n},\qquad\hbox{$a$ long},

(4.12)

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a,2n+i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-n{\Lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{a,a}}\,g^{n}\,\bar{D}_{a,i}\,g^{-n},\qquad i=0,1,\ \hbox{$a$ short}.

These elements are

(4.13)

g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda}\,\left(\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N-1}{\Lambda}_{N}};q\right)^{-1}_{\infty},&{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},\\
\left(g_{T}^{1/2}\,\left(\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}};q\right)^{-1}_{\infty}\right)^{2}\,g_{\Lambda},&{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},\\
(g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda})^{2}\left(\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}};q\right)^{-1}_{\infty},&{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},\\
g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda}\,\left(\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}};q^{2}\right)^{-1}_{\infty},&{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N-1}^{(2)},\\
g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda}\,\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}};q^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)_{\infty}^{-1},&{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N+1}^{(2)},\\
g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda}\,\left(q^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}};q\right)^{-1}_{\infty}\,g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda}\,\left(\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}};q\right)_{\infty}^{-1},&{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N}^{(2)},\end{array}\right.

where we use the shorthand
g_{T},g_{\Lambda}
of Equations (
2.23
) and (
2.26
).

Note the
\Lambda
-dependence of
g
is only via
\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}^{*}}
where
\alpha_{a}^{*}
are the simple roots of
R^{*}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
n
. The inductive step relies on the fact
that Equations (
4.11
) and (
4.12
), which can be written as

(4.14)

\bar{D}_{a;m+t_{a}}=q^{-\Lambda_{a,a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}t_{a}/2}g\bar{D}_{a;m}g^{-1},\quad t_{a}=\left\{\begin{matrix}1&{\rm if}\ a\ {\rm long}\\
2&{\rm if}\ a\ {\rm short}\end{matrix}\right.,

are compatible with the
\bar{\rm Q}
-system evolution, from which
\bar{D}_{a;n}
are defined.
Define for all labels
a
:
\bar{\mathcal{T}}_{a;n}:=\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n}^{2}-q^{\Lambda_{a,a}}\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n-1}\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{a;n+1}.

If (
4.14
) hold for all
m\leq n
, then they hold for
m=n+1
iff
g\bar{\mathcal{T}}_{a;n}g^{-1}=q^{\Lambda_{a,a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}t_{a}}\bar{\mathcal{T}}_{a;n+t_{a}}
. This is easily checked case-by-case.
To complete the proof, we must check (
4.11
-
4.12
) for some initial values of
n
.
Using the form of
\bar{\mathcal{T}}_{a;n}
, we see that for long roots, it is enough to show that (
4.11
) holds for
n=-1
and
n=0
, so we need the explicit expressions for

\bar{D}_{a,n}
with
n=-1,0,1
to start the induction, and for short roots, we also need the expression for
n=2
:

(4.15)

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a,-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a,0}(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}^{*}})T^{-\omega_{a}},\qquad a\in[1,N],

(4.16)

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a,2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a,1}(1-\Lambda^{-\alpha_{a}^{*}}T^{-\alpha_{a}})T^{\omega_{a}},\qquad\hbox{$a$ short.}

Let

h_{{\alpha}}(u)=({\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}};q^{u})_{\infty}^{-1}
,
h_{\alpha}=h_{\alpha}(1)
and
h_{{\alpha}}^{+}=(q^{\frac{1}{2}}{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}};q)_{\infty}^{-1}
.
Then
g_{\Lambda}=\prod_{a=1}^{N-1}h_{{\alpha}_{a}^{*}}
.
It is useful to rewrite the
g
operators of (
4.13
) as

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}(u),\quad{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},D_{N+1}^{(2)},\ u=1,2,\frac{1}{2}\ {\rm resp.},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{1}g_{2}\ \hbox{ where }\ g_{1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}},\\
g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda},\\
g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{+},\\
\end{array}\right.\ \hbox{ and }\ g_{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g_{1}\,g_{\Lambda},&{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},\\
g_{1}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}},&{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},\\
g_{T}g_{\Lambda}h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}},&{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N}^{(2)},\end{array}\right.

We use the exchange relations, valid for any
x,y\in\mathbb{Q}
,

(4.18)

\displaystyle(g_{T})^{x}\,({\Lambda}_{a})^{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{xy^{2}}{2}}\,({\Lambda}_{a})^{y}\,(T_{a})^{xy}\,(g_{T})^{x},

(4.19)

\displaystyle h_{{\alpha}_{b}^{*}}\,T^{\omega_{a}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{\omega_{a}}\,(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}^{*}})\,h_{{\alpha}_{b}^{*}},\qquad h_{{\alpha}_{b}^{*}}^{-1}\,T^{-\omega_{a}}=(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}^{*}})T^{-\omega_{a}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{b}^{*}}^{-1}.

Long labels.
In this case, we need only verify that (
4.11
) hold for
n=\pm 1
,
Note that
\bar{D}_{a;0}=\Lambda^{\omega_{a}^{*}}
commutes with
g_{\Lambda}
.
In the self-dual cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}
, all labels are long
and Equation (
4.11
) with
n=1
,
g\bar{D}_{a;0}g^{-1}=q^{\Lambda_{a,a}/2}\bar{D}_{a;1}
, follows from

g_{T}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}=q^{\frac{\Lambda_{a,a}}{2}}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}T^{\omega_{a}}g_{T}=q^{\frac{\Lambda_{a,a}}{2}}\bar{D}_{a;1}\,g_{T}.

For
n=-1
it is equivalent to

\displaystyle g^{-1}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}g_{\Lambda}^{-1}g_{T}^{-1}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}=q^{-\frac{{\Lambda}_{a,a}}{2}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,g_{\Lambda}^{-1}T^{-\omega_{a}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}\,g_{\Lambda}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{{\Lambda}_{a,a}}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}\,(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}^{*}})T^{-\omega_{a}}\,g^{-1}=q^{-\frac{{\Lambda}_{a,a}}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{a;-1}\,g^{-1}.

For the long labels
a<N
of
B_{N}^{(1)}
,
\omega_{a}^{*}=\omega_{a}
,
\Lambda_{a,a}=a
and (
4.11
) with
n=\pm 1
follow from

g_{T}^{1/2}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}\,g_{T}^{1/2}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}=q^{\frac{a}{4}}g_{T}^{1/2}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,T^{\omega_{a}/2}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}\,g_{T}^{1/2}\\
=q^{\frac{a}{2}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}T^{\omega_{a}}g_{T}^{1/2}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}\,g_{T}^{1/2}=q^{\frac{a}{2}}\bar{D}_{a;1}g_{T}^{1/2}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}\,g_{T}^{1/2}

and

\displaystyle g^{-1}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{\Lambda}^{-1}(h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}g_{T}^{-1/2})^{2}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}=q^{-\frac{a}{2}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,g_{\Lambda}^{-1}T^{-\omega_{a}^{*}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}g_{T}^{-1/2}h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}g_{T}^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{a}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{a,0}\,(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}^{*}})T^{-\omega_{a}^{*}}\,g^{-1}=q^{-\frac{\Lambda_{a,a}}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{a;-1}\,g^{-1}.

For the long label
a=N
of
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)}
,
2\omega_{N}^{*}=\omega_{N}
, (
4.11
) with
n=\pm 1
follows from

\displaystyle g_{T}g_{\Lambda}g_{T}\,\bar{D}_{N;0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{N}{2}}g_{T}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}g_{\Lambda}T^{\omega_{N}^{*}}\,g_{T}=q^{N}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}T^{2\omega_{N}^{*}}g_{T}g_{\Lambda}g_{T}=q^{N}\bar{D}_{N;1}\,g_{T}g_{\Lambda}g_{T}

and

\displaystyle g^{-1}\,\bar{D}_{N;0}=h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}g_{\Lambda}^{-1}g_{T}^{-1}g_{\Lambda}^{-1}g_{T}^{-1}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}=q^{-\frac{N}{4}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}g_{\Lambda}^{-1}g_{T}^{-1}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}T^{-\omega_{N}^{*}}g_{\Lambda}^{-1}g_{T}^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad=q^{-\frac{N}{2}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}T^{-2\omega_{N}^{*}}\,(g_{\Lambda}^{-1}g_{T}^{-1})^{2}=q^{-\frac{N}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{N;0}\,(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{N}^{*}})T^{-2\omega_{N}^{*}}\,g^{-1}=q^{-\frac{N}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{N;-1}\,g^{-1}.

Therefore (
4.11
) holds when
n=\pm 1
and
a
long.

Short labels.
Next we verify (
4.12
) for short labels
a
. We claim the following half-evolution equations hold
in types
B_{N}^{(1)},C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
:

(4.20)

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{1}{2}{\Lambda}_{a,a}}g_{2}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}\,g_{2}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a;1},

(4.21)

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{1}{2}{\Lambda}_{a,a}}g_{1}\,\bar{D}_{a;1}\,g_{1}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a;2}.

To see this, Equation (
4.20
) for
B_{N}^{(1)}
with
a=N
, where
\omega_{N}^{*}=2\omega_{N}
, follows from

g_{2}\,\bar{D}_{N;0}\,g_{2}^{-1}=g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}g_{T}^{-\frac{1}{2}}=q^{\frac{N}{4}}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}T^{\omega_{N}}=q^{\frac{\Lambda_{N,N}}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{N;1}.

For the short labels of
C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
, where
\omega_{a}=\omega_{a}^{*}
, it follows from

g_{2}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}\,g_{2}^{-1}=g_{T}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}g_{T}^{-1}=q^{\frac{a}{2}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,T^{\omega_{a}^{*}}=q^{\frac{\Lambda_{a,a}}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{a;1}.

Equation (
4.21
)
{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)}
with
a=N
, where
{\alpha}_{N}^{*}=2{\alpha}_{N}
, follows from

\displaystyle g_{1}\,\bar{D}_{N;1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}T^{\omega_{N}}=g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}T^{\omega_{N}}(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{N}^{*}})\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{N}{4}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}T^{2\omega_{N}}(1-q{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}T^{-{\alpha}_{N}^{*}/2})\,g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}=q^{\frac{\Lambda_{N,N}}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{N;2}\,g_{1}.

When
a\leq N-1
for
C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
, for
a\leq N-1
,
{\alpha}_{a}^{*}={\alpha}_{a}
and

\displaystyle g_{1}\,\bar{D}_{a;1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{T}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,g_{\Lambda}T^{\omega_{a}}=g_{T}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,T^{\omega_{a}}\,(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}^{*}})\,g_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{a}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{a;1}\,(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}^{*}}T^{-{\alpha}_{a}^{*}})\,T^{\omega_{a}}\,g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda}=q^{\frac{a}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{a;2}\,g_{1}.

For
A_{2N}^{(2)}
,
a=N
,

\displaystyle g_{1}\,\bar{D}_{N;1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{T}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}\,g_{\Lambda}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{+}T^{\omega_{N}}=g_{T}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}(1-q^{-\frac{1}{2}}{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{N}^{*}})T^{\omega_{N}}\,g_{\Lambda}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{N}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{N;1}\,(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}T^{-{\alpha}_{N}^{*}})\,g_{T}\,g_{\Lambda}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{+}=q^{\frac{N}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{N;2}\,g_{1}.

Equations (
4.20
),(
4.21
) imply (
4.12
) follows for
i=1,n=1
.

We also have the half-evolution equations for short labels:

(4.22)

\displaystyle q^{\frac{1}{2}{\Lambda}_{a,a}}g_{1}^{-1}\,\bar{D}_{a;1}\,g_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a;0},

(4.23)

\displaystyle q^{\frac{1}{2}{\Lambda}_{a,a}}g_{2}^{-1}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}\,g_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a;-1}.

Equation (
4.22
) is equivalent to

\bar{D}_{a;1}=q^{-\frac{1}{2}{\Lambda}_{a,a}}g_{1}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}\,g_{1}^{-1}
, which is unchanged if we replace
g_{1}
by
g_{2}
,
as only the
T
-dependent part
g_{T}
acts on
\bar{D}_{a;0}
, and the equation is therefore equivalent to
(
4.20
).
To show (
4.23
) for
B_{N}^{(1)}
,

\displaystyle g_{2}^{-1}\,\bar{D}_{N;0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{\Lambda}^{-1}h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}\,g_{T}^{-\frac{1}{2}}{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}=q^{\frac{N}{2}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}\,g_{\Lambda}^{-1}h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}\,T^{-\omega_{N}}\,g_{T}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{N}{2}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}\,(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{N}^{*}})\,T^{-\omega_{N}}g_{\Lambda}^{-1}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}\,g_{T}^{-\frac{1}{2}}=q^{\frac{N}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{N;-1}\,g_{2}^{-1},

and for
a
short in
C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
,

g_{2}^{-1}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}=q^{\frac{a}{2}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}g_{\Lambda}^{-1}T^{-\omega_{a}}\,g_{T}^{-1}=q^{\frac{a}{2}}\,{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}^{*}})T^{-\omega_{a}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}g_{\Lambda}^{-1}\,g_{T}^{-1}=q^{\frac{a}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{a;-1}\,g_{2}^{-1}.

These half-evolutions imply Equation (
4.12
) with
i=1,n=-1
.

The Theorem follows by induction.
∎

4.2.2.
Integrability and conserved quantities

We claim that the time-translation operators
g
commute with the Pieri operators for each
{\mathfrak{g}}
, which therefore have an interpretation as the conserved quantities of the quantum
\bar{\rm Q}
-system.

Theorem 4.10
.

For all
{\mathfrak{g}}
, the operator
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
commutes with the first Pieri operator
H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
.

The proof is by explicit calculation for each
{\mathfrak{g}}
. It is given in Appendix
C
.

In what follows, we need to generalize to type
{\mathfrak{g}}
the statement of Section
2.9
about the uniqueness of the solutions to the first Pieri equation. Starting with

D_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)={\Lambda}_{1}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, writing
\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=x^{\lambda}p_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, and
conjugating (
3.36
) with
x^{-\lambda}
results in the equation

\left(\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{1}}+\sum_{i=1\atop\epsilon=\pm 1}^{N}\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\left(\frac{{\Lambda}_{i}^{\epsilon}}{{\Lambda}_{1}}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{1}}\right)-1\right)\,p_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=0.

The difference operator is polynomial in
\{{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{i}^{*}}\}
,
{\alpha}_{i}^{*}
the simple roots of
R^{*}
, so we may analytically continue the solution

p_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
to
\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
,
and then consider
\Lambda
to be a formal parameter. The coefficients
\phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
have series expansions
in the variables
x^{-{\alpha}_{i}}
,
{\alpha}_{i}
the simple roots of
R
, hence we may expand
\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}(R)}c_{\beta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})x^{-\beta}
, as well as
c_{\beta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})=\sum_{\delta\in Q_{+}(R^{*})}c_{\beta,\delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}{\Lambda}^{-\delta}
. When dealing with formal variables, we may exchange the summations and write a new series expansion,

\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\sum_{\delta\in Q_{+}(R^{*})}\hat{c}_{\delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x){\Lambda}^{-\delta},\ \ \hat{c}_{\delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}(R)}c_{\beta,\delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}x^{-\beta}.

The first Pieri equation (
3.48
) is also easily extended to formal
\lambda
, as the
dependence on
{\Lambda}^{-\alpha_{a}^{*}}
is polynomial, while
T_{i}:\Lambda_{j}\mapsto q^{\delta_{ij}}\Lambda_{j}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\Theta_{\lambda}(x)
(
\lambda
arbitrary) be a solution of the first Pieri equation (
3.48
), such that

\Theta_{\lambda}(x)=x^{\lambda}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}(R^{*})}\tau_{\beta}(x){\Lambda}^{-\beta}
.
Then when
\lambda
is evaluated as a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition,

\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=\frac{\hat{c}_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)}{\tau_{0}(x)}\,\Theta_{\lambda}(x).

Proof.

The Pieri equation is a triangular linear system for the coefficients
\tau_{\beta}(x)
, determined uniquely for
\beta\neq 0
up to the factor
\tau_{0}(x)
. The same holds for the coefficients
\hat{c}_{\beta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
in the expansion of
\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, up to
\hat{c}_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
. We deduce that
x^{\lambda}\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\frac{\hat{c}_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)}{\tau_{0}(x)}\,\Theta_{\lambda}(x)
. The Lemma follows by specialization to

{\mathfrak{g}}
-partitions.
∎

Theorem 4.12
.

The action of
g(\Lambda)
on
\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
is equivalent to acting by a Gaussian in
x
:

(4.24)

g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\gamma^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),\ \ \gamma^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\gamma(x)^{t_{1}},

where

t_{1}=2
if
1
is a short label (i.e.
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
), and is equal to 1 otherwise.

In other words,
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(\Lambda)
is the Fourier transform of
\gamma(x)^{t_{1}}
.

Proof.

Like
\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, the quantity
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
can be continued to
arbitrary
\lambda
, and we write it as
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})x^{\lambda}\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
.
Multiplying the first Pieri equation by
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
on the left, we get:

g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,x^{\lambda}\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\Big(g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,x^{\lambda}\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\Big)={\hat{e}}_{1}(x)\,\Big(g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,x^{\lambda}\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\Big),

hence both
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,x^{\lambda}\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
and
x^{\lambda}\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
obey the same first Pieri rule. Apply Lemma
4.11
to the function

\Theta_{\lambda}(x)=g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,x^{\lambda}\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,x^{\lambda}(\hat{c}_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)+O({\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{i}^{*}}))
.
Expanding
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=1+O({\Lambda}^{-\alpha_{a}^{*}})
, we see that the leading order term
in
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,x^{\lambda}\hat{p}_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
has only contributions from the action of the
g_{T}
parts of
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
on the leading term
x^{\lambda}
. The total contribution of the
g_{T}
terms is
g_{T}^{t_{1}}
, as directly read off (
4.13
).
Finally noting that
(g_{T})^{t_{1}}\,x^{\lambda}=\gamma(x)^{t_{1}}\,x^{\lambda}T^{t_{1}\xi}(g_{T})^{t_{1}}
, where
x=q^{\xi}
(using (
4.18
)), we find that the leading term is
\tau_{0}(x)=\gamma(x)^{t_{1}}\hat{c}_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
,
and the Theorem follows from the relation
\tau_{0}(x)/\hat{c}_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\gamma(x)^{t_{1}}
.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

As noted in Remark
2.12
, in the cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N+1}^{(2)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},D_{N}^{(1)}
,
the relation (
4.24
) boils down to the recursion relation of
[
FJMM09
]

(Theorem 3.1) for the coefficients
J_{\beta}^{\mu}=J_{\beta}(x=q^{\mu})
for the root systems
R=B_{N},C_{N},D_{N}
respectively (see Table
1
). These are the coefficients in the series expansion

\tilde{\Pi}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{\lambda}(x)=x^{\lambda}g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{\Lambda}\,\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{\lambda}(x)=x^{\lambda}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}J_{\beta}(x)\Lambda^{-\beta}
, up to a rescaling
q\to q^{2}
in the cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}
.
Here we use the notation

g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{\Lambda}=g_{\Lambda}\times\left\{\begin{matrix}&1/\left(\frac{1}{\Lambda_{N}};q^{1/2}\right)_{\infty}&{\rm for}\ {\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N+1}^{(2)},\,R=B_{N}\\
&1/\left(\frac{1}{\Lambda_{N}^{2}};q^{2}\right)_{\infty}&{\rm for}\ {\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N-1}^{(2)},\,R=C_{N}\\
&1/\left(\frac{1}{\Lambda_{N-1}\Lambda_{N}};q\right)_{\infty}&{\rm for}\ {\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},\,R=D_{N}\end{matrix}\right..

Corollary 4.14
.

The Pieri operators
H_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
,
a=1,2,...,N
of Eq. (
3.45
) are algebraically independent conserved quantities of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-quantum
\bar{\rm Q}
-systems.

Proof.

To see that the time translation operator
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
commutes with all higher Pieri operators
H_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
,
a\in[1,N]
,
act with
(g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda}))^{-1}\gamma(x)^{t_{1}}
on
the Pieri equations, and use Theorem
4.12

g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})^{-1}\gamma(x)^{t_{1}}\,H_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})^{-1}H_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)={\hat{e}}_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),

which implies that
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})^{-1}H_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})=H_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
by the definition of the Pieri operators. The statement follows by noting that any dependence between
H_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
would imply a dependence between
\hat{e}_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, which are independent.
∎

4.3.
Proof of Theorems
4.3
and
4.4

The proof of both Theorems relies on the following.

Theorem 4.15
.

For all
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},B_{N}^{(1)},C_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
we have the relation

(4.25)

D_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\bar{D}_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),\qquad a\in[1,N],n\in{\mathbb{Z}}

valid for any
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition
\lambda
.

Theorem
4.3
follows from Theorem
4.15
, as any relation satisfied by the difference operators
\{\bar{D}_{a;n}\}
implies the opposite relation for the difference operators
\{D_{a;n}\}
. Theorem
4.4
is the
particular case of (
4.25
) with
n=0,1
. We provide a proof of (
4.25
) for long labels, short labels except for
B_{N}^{(1)}
,
and for the short label of
B_{N}^{(1)}
separately.

4.3.1.
Long labels and even time short labels

In this case, the proof is similar to that in type
A_{N-1}^{(1)}
. Acting on both sides of the eigenvalue equation (
4.7
), which we write as (
4.25
) with
n=0
,
with
\gamma(x)^{-t_{1}n}\,(g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda}))^{n}
and using Theorem
4.12
,

\displaystyle\gamma^{-t_{1}n}\,D_{a;0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,(g^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{n}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\gamma^{-t_{1}n}\,D_{a,0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\gamma^{{t_{1}n}}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=q^{\frac{\Lambda_{a,a}t_{a}n}{2}}\,D_{a;nt_{a}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

\displaystyle=(g^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{n}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\gamma^{-t_{1}n}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=(g^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{n}\,\bar{D}_{a;0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,(g^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{-n}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=q^{\frac{\Lambda_{a,a}t_{a}n}{2}}\,\bar{D}_{a;nt_{a}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),

where we have used Definition
3.15
. Therefore, Eq. (
4.25
) holds for
n
a multiple of
t_{a}
.

4.3.2.
Short labels, odd
n
for
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}

In both cases,
a=1
is a short label,
t_{1}=2
.

Lemma 4.16
.

For
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
and for any
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition
\lambda
,

\Big(D_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)-\bar{D}_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\Big)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=q^{-1}\Big(D_{1,-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)-\bar{D}_{1,-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\Big)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x).

Proof.

Applying Equation
3.54
with
n=0
, acting on

\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
,

\displaystyle[D_{1,0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),{\hat{e}}_{1}(x)]\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\left(D_{1,0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})-{\hat{e}}_{1}(x)\bar{D}_{1,0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\right)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

(4.26)

\displaystyle\quad=\left[H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda}),\bar{D}_{1,0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\right]\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=(q-1)\left(D_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)-q^{-1}\,D_{1,-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\right)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),

where we used the Pieri rule (
3.48
), and the eigenvalue equation (
4.25
) with
n=0,a=1
.
The only terms in
H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
of Equations (
3.46
-
3.47
) which fail to commute with
\bar{D}_{1,0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})={\Lambda}_{1}

are
T_{1}+\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{2}}{{\Lambda}_{1}}\right)T_{1}^{-1}
if
N\geq 2
. Therefore,

\displaystyle[H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda}),\bar{D}_{1,0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[T_{1}+\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{2}}{{\Lambda}_{1}}\right)T_{1}^{-1},{\Lambda}_{1}]=(q-1)\,{\Lambda}_{1}\,T_{1}+(q^{-1}-1)({\Lambda}_{1}-{\Lambda}_{2})T_{1}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(q-1)\bar{D}_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})+(q^{-1}-1)\bar{D}_{1,-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda}),

where used
\bar{D}_{1,-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})=({\Lambda}_{1}-{\Lambda}_{2})T_{1}^{-1}
. The lemma follows.
∎

Defining
\Sigma_{n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=(D_{1,n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)-\bar{D}_{1,n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda}))\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, Lemma
4.16
says that

\Sigma_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=q^{-1}\,\Sigma_{-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
.

Lemma 4.17
.

For
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
, we have:

H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\Sigma_{i}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}={\hat{e}}_{1}(x)\,\Sigma_{i}^{({\mathfrak{g}})},\qquad(i=\pm 1).

Proof.

As
\Sigma_{-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=q\,\Sigma_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
, we may restrict ourselves to
i=1
.

(4.27)

\displaystyle H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\Sigma_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(D_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,-H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\bar{D}_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\Big)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{{\hat{e}}_{1}(x)\,D_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)+(q-1)\big(D_{1,2}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)-q^{-1}D_{1,0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\bar{D}_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})-(q-1)\big(\bar{D}_{1,2}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})-q^{-1}\bar{D}_{1,0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\big)\Big\}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{{\hat{e}}_{1}(x)\,D_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)-\bar{D}_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\Big\}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)={\hat{e}}_{1}(x)\,\Sigma_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})},

where we used Lemma
3.14
, as well as the
a=1
,
n=0,2
cases of
(
4.25
) proven above.
∎

Lemma
4.16
, together with a uniqueness argument (Lemma
4.11
), implies that
there exists a function
{\alpha}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

such that
\Sigma_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}={\alpha}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
,
\Sigma_{-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=q\,{\alpha}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
:

\{D_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)-{\bar{D}}_{1,1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})-{\alpha}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\{D_{1,-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)-\bar{D}_{1,-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})-q\,{\alpha}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=0.

Using this equation with
\lambda=0
,

D_{1,-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=D_{1,-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,1=0
, using (
3.50
).
Similarly,

\bar{D}_{1,-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,\Pi_{\emptyset}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=({\Lambda}_{1}-{\Lambda}_{2})T_{1}^{-1}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)|_{\lambda=0}=0
, since the prefactor vanishes.
Therefore,
{\alpha}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=0
and
\Sigma_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=\Sigma_{-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=0
.
Multiplying
\Sigma_{-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
by
(g^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{n}\gamma^{-2n}
, we conclude that for
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
,
a=1
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and any integer partition
\lambda
, Equation (
4.25
) holds.

This result can be extended to all short labels for these algebras, as follows.

Lemma 4.18
.

Equation (
4.25
) holds for all short labels
a
and
n
odd in the case of
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
.

Proof.

Let
N_{\mathfrak{g}}
be as in Equation (
3.19
). Then
if
a\leq N_{\mathfrak{g}}-1
, the relations of the quantum
\bar{\rm Q}
-system are identical to those of type
A_{N_{\mathfrak{g}}-1}^{(1)}
, and hence we can apply
Theorem 2.8 of
[
DFK17b
]
, which states that any solution of the relations for
a=1,2,...,N_{\mathfrak{g}}-1

q^{a}\,\mathcal{Q}_{a;n+1}\,\mathcal{Q}_{a;n-1}=(\mathcal{Q}_{a;n})^{2}-\mathcal{Q}_{a+1;n}\mathcal{Q}_{a-1;n},\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{a;n}\mathcal{Q}_{b;n+1}=q^{{\min}(a,b)}\,\mathcal{Q}_{b;n+1}\,\mathcal{Q}_{a;n},

satisfies

(-1)^{a}(q-1)\mathcal{Q}_{a;n}=[\mathcal{Q}_{1;n-a+1},\mathcal{Q}_{a-1;n+1}]_{q^{a}},\qquad(a=1,2,...,N_{\mathfrak{g}}).

Therefore the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-quantum
\bar{\rm Q}
-system solution
\bar{D}_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
obeys the opposite relations

(-1)^{a}(q-1)\,\bar{D}_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=[\bar{D}_{a-1;n+1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})},\bar{D}_{1;n-a+1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}]_{q^{a}}.

Using this and Definition
3.16
,
we deduce that

\displaystyle\{D_{a;2n-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)-\bar{D}_{a;2n-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\frac{(-1)^{a}}{q-1}\{[D_{1;2n-a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),D_{a-1;2n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)]_{q^{a}}-[\bar{D}_{a-1;2n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda}),\bar{D}_{1;2n-a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})]_{q^{a}}\}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=0,

by use of Eq. (
4.25
) for
D_{a-1;2n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
and
D_{1;2n-a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
with
a
even
and for

D_{1;2n-a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
when
a
is odd.
∎

4.3.3.
The case of
B_{N}^{(1)}
for odd
n

It remains to prove (
4.25
)
for
{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)}
,
a=N
,
n
odd. The methods above are inapplicable, and instead we use expression (
3.59
) for
D_{N;2n-1}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}
in terms of Rains operators.

Recall that
B_{N}^{(1)}
-partitions are both integer and half-integer partitions, due to the spin representation with highest weight
\omega_{N}
and character
s_{\omega_{N}}(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1+x_{i}}{\sqrt{x_{i}}}
.
Denote

(4.28)

P_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}=P_{\lambda}^{(t,-1,q^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})},\qquad\tilde{P}_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda}^{(t,-q,q^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})}.

There is a factorization of Macdonald polynomials
[
vD95
]
,

(4.29)

P_{\lambda+\omega_{N}}(x)=s_{\omega_{N}}(x)\,\tilde{P}_{\lambda}(x),\qquad\hbox{$\lambda$ integer partition}.

The parameter specialization (
4.28
) for
\tilde{P}_{\lambda}
is obtained by conjugating

{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}(x,q,t)
by
s_{\omega_{N}}^{-1}
and identifying the resulting parameters
(a,b,c,d)
.
We must therefore consider the action of the difference operators on both functions

\Pi_{\lambda}=\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{\lambda}
and
\tilde{\Pi}_{\lambda}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{P}_{\lambda}
with
\lambda
integer partitions.

Moreover, the Rains operators map the eigenfunctions of the
B_{N}^{(1)}
-type difference
operators to those of

B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}}
-type, corresponding to different parameters. We denote the corresponding
q
-Whittaker limit of the eigenfunctions functions as
\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}
. To these parameters there corresponds a different quantization of the Q-system, a
time-translation operator
g^{\prime}({\Lambda})
, and Hamiltonians.

Specializing to the
B_{N}^{(1)}
parameters
(a,b,c,d)=(t,-1,q^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})
and taking the
t\to\infty
limit as in (
3.56
),
(
3.8
) become

\displaystyle R_{N}^{(0)}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime},

(4.30)

\displaystyle R_{N}^{(1)}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\Pi_{\lambda},

for integer partitions
\lambda
.
We identify the
B_{N}^{(1)\ ^{\prime}}
specialization to be
(a,b,c,d)=(tq^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}},1,-1)
,

\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\Pi_{\lambda}^{(B_{N}^{(1)\ ^{\prime}})}=\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{\lambda}^{(tq^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}},1,-1)}.

Lemma 4.19
.

Equations (
4.30
) also hold for half-integer partitions.

Proof.

Using the specialization
(a,b,c,d)=(t,-q,q^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})
as in (
4.28
),
in the limit
t\to\infty
(
3.8
) becomes

\tilde{R}^{(0)}_{N}\,\tilde{\Pi}_{\lambda}=q^{\frac{N}{4}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\frac{q^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\,\tilde{\Pi}_{\lambda}^{\prime},\ \ \tilde{R}^{(1)}_{N}\,\tilde{\Pi}_{\lambda}^{\prime}=q^{\frac{N}{4}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\tilde{\Pi}_{\lambda},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{(0)}_{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-N(N-1)/2}\,q^{-\frac{N}{4}}\mathcal{R}^{(1,-q^{\frac{1}{2}})}_{N}=s_{\omega_{N}}^{-1}\,R_{N}^{(0)}\,s_{\omega_{N}},

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{(1)}_{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-N(N+1)/2}\,q^{-\frac{N}{4}}\mathcal{R}^{(t,-q^{\frac{1}{2}})}_{N}=s_{\omega_{N}}^{-1}\,R_{N}^{(1)}\,s_{\omega_{N}},

by use of the limit of (
4.29
). Here,

R_{N}^{(0)}
and
R_{N}^{(1)}
are as in (
3.56
). Restoring the factors
s_{\omega_{N}}
,

\displaystyle R_{N}^{(0)}(x)\,s_{\omega_{N}}\,\tilde{\Pi}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}\tilde{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots\tilde{\Lambda}_{N}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\tilde{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\,s_{\omega_{N}}\,\tilde{\Pi}_{\lambda}^{\prime},

\displaystyle R_{N}^{(1)}(x)\,s_{\omega_{N}}\,\tilde{\Pi}_{\lambda}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}\tilde{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots\tilde{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,s_{\omega_{N}}\,\tilde{\Pi}_{\lambda},

where
\tilde{\Lambda}=q^{\omega_{N}}{\Lambda}=q^{\omega_{N}+\lambda}
. Combining this with the limit of (
4.29
) implies that (
4.30
) is satisfied for half-integer partitions
\tilde{\lambda}=\omega_{N}+\lambda
as well as integer partitions.
∎

Combining equations (
4.30
) leads to the eigenvalue equation for
\hat{D}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}(x)
of (
3.56
)

\hat{D}_{N}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}(x)=R_{N}^{(1)}(x)\,R_{N}^{(0)}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}={\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N}\left(1+\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\,\Pi_{\lambda},

which is consistent with the relation
\hat{D}_{N}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}=R_{N}^{(1)}\,R_{N}^{(0)}=D_{N,0}+D_{N-1,0}
and the eigenvalues
\bar{D}_{a,0}
in Definition (
4.6
).

The
q
-Whittaker limit of the difference operators for the
{B_{N}^{(1)}}^{\prime}
parameters are obtained as in (
A.38
), and are
denoted by
D_{a}^{\prime}(x)=D_{a}^{(B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}})}(x)
,
a=1,2,...,N
. The corresponding limit
of the Rains operator (
3.9
) is

\hat{D}_{N}^{\prime}(x)=R_{N}^{(0)}(x)\,R_{N}^{(1)}(x)=D_{N}^{\prime}(x)+D_{N-1}^{\prime}(x)
.
This identification follows from the
t\to\infty
limit of Lemma
A.10
, and from the eigenvalues,
which are identical to those of the
B_{N}^{(1)}
case, since
\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}=\xi_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}
.
As in the
B_{N}^{(1)}
case,
We define the generalized Macdonald operators

\displaystyle D_{a;n}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-na/2}\,\gamma^{-n}\,D_{a}^{\prime}(x)\,\gamma^{n},\qquad(a=1,2,...,N-1),

\displaystyle D_{N;2n}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-nN/2}\,\gamma^{-n}\,D_{N}^{\prime}(x)\,\gamma^{n},

(4.31)

\displaystyle D_{N;2n+1}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-nN/4}\,R_{N;n+1}^{(0)}(x)\,R_{N;n}^{(1)}(x)=q^{-nN/2}\,\gamma^{-n}\,D_{N;1}^{\prime}(x)\,\gamma^{n}.

Note the reversal of the order in the last product and the different time index compared to (
3.59
).
The operators
D_{a;n}^{\prime}
will be shown to satisfy the set of recursion relations below.

Definition 4.20
.

The type
{B_{N}^{(1)}}^{\prime}
-quantum Q-system relations
6
6

6

This “quantum Q-system” is new, and
we call it a Q-system by analogy with the other
{\mathfrak{g}}
cases.

are the same as the type
B_{N}^{(1)}
relations in (
4.3
)–(
4.5
) except for the two equations with
a=N
:

\displaystyle q^{N/2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n+2}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n+1}^{2}-q^{\frac{N-1}{2}-n}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n},

\displaystyle q^{N/2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n+1}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n}-q^{-\frac{n}{2}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n})({\mathcal{Q}}_{N;2n}+q^{\frac{1-n}{2}}\,{\mathcal{Q}}_{N-1;n}).

In particular, the q-commutation relations are as in type
B_{N}^{(1)}
(
4.3
).

The eigenvalue equation for
D_{a}^{\prime}
corresponding to any
\lambda
is

D_{a;0}^{\prime}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}={\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}
,
with
\omega_{a}^{*}
a fundamental weight of type
C_{N}
. Therefore,
the candidate Fourier transforms
\bar{D}_{a,n}^{\prime}(\Lambda)
are defined so that they satisfy the opposite quantum Q-system to that of Definition
4.20
, subject to the same initial data as in type
B_{N}^{(1)}
,

\bar{D}^{\prime}_{a;0}={\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}
and

\bar{D}_{a;1}^{\prime}={\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}T^{\omega_{a}}
,

\bar{D}_{N;1}^{\prime}={\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}T^{\omega_{N}}
.
Together with the recursions of Def.
4.20
, this determines
\bar{D}_{a;n}^{\prime}
for all
a,n
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a;-1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a;0}^{\prime}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{a+1}}{{\Lambda}_{a}}\right)T^{-\omega_{a}}\qquad(a=1,2,...,N-1),

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{N;-1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{N;0}^{\prime}\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\left(1+\frac{q^{\frac{1}{2}}}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)T^{-\omega_{N}},\ \ \bar{D}_{N;2}^{\prime}=\bar{D}_{N;1}^{\prime}\left(1-q^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}}\frac{1}{T_{N}}\right)T^{\omega_{N}}.

As before, these are sufficient to determine the form of the time-translation operator
g^{\prime}(\Lambda)
.

Lemma 4.21
.

The operator

(4.32)

\displaystyle g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,g_{{\Lambda}_{N}}^{\prime}\,g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,g_{{\Lambda}_{N}}^{\prime\prime}\,g_{\Lambda},

\displaystyle g_{{\Lambda}_{N}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(q^{\frac{1}{2}}{\Lambda}_{N}^{-2};q)_{\infty}},\quad g_{{\Lambda}_{N}}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{1}{({\Lambda}_{N}^{-1};q^{\frac{1}{2}})_{\infty}\,(-q^{\frac{1}{2}}{\Lambda}_{N}^{-1};q^{\frac{1}{2}})_{\infty}},

where
g_{T}
is as in (
2.23
) and
g_{\Lambda}
as in (
2.26
),
is the time translation operator for the
B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}}
opposite quantum Q-system. That is, for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{a;n}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-an/2}\,(g^{\prime})^{n}\,\bar{D}_{a}^{\prime}\,(g^{\prime})^{-n}\qquad(a=1,2,...,N-1),

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{N;2n+i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-Nn/2}(g^{\prime})^{n}\,\bar{D}_{N;i}^{\prime}\,(g^{\prime})^{-n},\quad i=0,1.

The Pieri rules for
B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}}
are obtained by duality. Using Theorem
B.1
with

(a,b,c,d)=(1,-1,tq^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})=(t^{\frac{1}{2}},-t^{-\frac{1}{2}},t^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}},-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}})
, the
q
-Whittaker limit of the first Pieri operator is

\displaystyle H_{1}^{(B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}})}({\Lambda})=T_{1}+\sum_{a=2}^{N}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{a}}{{\Lambda}_{a-1}}\right)T_{a}+\sum_{a=1}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{a+1}}{{\Lambda}_{a}}\right)T_{a}^{-1}

\displaystyle+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\left(1+\frac{q}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\left(1-\frac{q}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}}\right)T_{N}^{-1}+\frac{q^{\frac{1}{2}}-q^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{{\Lambda}_{N}}-\frac{q^{\frac{1}{2}}+q^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}}+\frac{q^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{{\Lambda}_{N-1}{\Lambda}_{N}}.

By direct calculation, this operator commutes with
g^{\prime}(\Lambda)
and as a consequence

(4.33)

g^{\prime}({\Lambda})\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)=\gamma(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)\ .

We want to show that
D_{N;1}(x)\Pi_{\lambda}=\bar{D}_{N;1}(\Lambda)\Pi_{\lambda}
(case
a=N,n=1
of (
4.25
)). Using
4.12
, Definition (
3.59
) and the relations (
4.30
), we find

(4.34)

\displaystyle D_{N;1}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{N}{4}}\,R_{N;0}^{(1)}\,R_{N;1}^{(0)}\,\Pi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{3N}{8}}\,g\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\,(g^{\prime})^{-1}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\Pi_{\lambda}.

Recall the notation

h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}=\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{q^{n}}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}}\right)^{-1}
, then we have the relations

\left(1+\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)(g_{{\Lambda}_{N}}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}=h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}

and
T_{N}^{\frac{1}{4}}(g_{{\Lambda}_{N}}^{\prime})^{-1}=h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}^{-1}T_{N}^{\frac{1}{4}}
. These allow to compute

(4.35)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!g\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\,(g^{\prime})^{-1}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}\,g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}\,g_{\Lambda}({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\!\!\left(1+\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)g_{\Lambda}^{-1}\,(g_{{\Lambda}_{N}}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}g_{T}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,(g_{{\Lambda}_{N}}^{\prime})^{-1}g_{T}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}\,g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{T}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,(g_{{\Lambda}_{N}}^{\prime})^{-1}g_{T}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{N}{16}}g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,h_{{\alpha}_{N}^{*}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{1}\cdots T_{N})^{\frac{1}{4}}\,(g_{{\Lambda}_{N}}^{\prime})^{-1}\,g_{T}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{N}{16}}g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{1}\cdots T_{N})^{\frac{1}{4}}\,g_{T}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{N}{8}}({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,(T_{1}\cdots T_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{3N}{8}}\,{\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N}\,(T_{1}\cdots T_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}=q^{\frac{3N}{8}}\,\bar{D}_{N;1}.

Combining this with (
4.34
) results in the relation

D_{N;1}(x)\Pi_{\lambda}=\bar{D}_{N;1}(\Lambda)\Pi_{\lambda}
. Multiplying by
\gamma^{-n}g^{n}
and using (
4.33
):

(4.36)

D_{N;2n+1}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}(x)=\bar{D}_{N;2n+1}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}({\Lambda})\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}(x),\qquad(n\in{\mathbb{Z}}).

This completes the proof of (
4.25
), and Theorems
4.3
and
4.4
follow.

Corollary 4.22
.

The
D^{\prime}
-operators satisfy the quantum
B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}}
-quantum Q-system of Definition
4.20
. Moreover we have the raising
operator conditions for any
B_{N}^{(1)}
-partition
\lambda
:

D_{a;1}^{\prime}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}={\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,\Pi_{\lambda+\omega_{a}}^{\prime},\qquad(a\in[1,N]),

\omega_{a},\omega_{a}^{*}
the fundamental weights of
B_{N},C_{N}
.

Proof.

Starting from the eigenvalue equations

D^{\prime}_{a}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\bar{D}_{a}^{\prime}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}
for
a=1,2,...,N
and
multiplying with
\gamma^{-n}(g^{\prime})^{n}
, and still denoting
\bar{X}
the prime Fourier transform of
X
,

D^{\prime}_{a;t_{a}n}(x)\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=q^{-na/2}\gamma^{-n}\,D^{\prime}_{a}(x)\,\gamma^{n}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=q^{-na/2}(g^{\prime})^{n}\,\bar{D}^{\prime}_{a}\,(g^{\prime})^{-n}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\bar{D}^{\prime}_{a;t_{a}n}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}.

For the short label
a=N
, using (
4.31
), (
4.30
), (
4.35
) and
\bar{D}_{N;1}=\bar{D}_{N;1}^{\prime}={\Lambda}^{\omega_{N}^{*}}T^{\omega_{N}}
:

\displaystyle D^{\prime}_{N;1}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{N;1}^{(0)}\,R_{N}^{(1)}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=q^{-\frac{N}{8}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}g\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\,(g^{\prime})^{-1}\,\Pi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{N}{8}}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,q^{\frac{3N}{8}}\,\bar{D}_{N;1}\,({\Lambda}_{1}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\Pi_{\lambda}=\bar{D}_{N;1}^{\prime}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}.

The relation is easily extended by multiplication with

\gamma^{-n}(g^{\prime})^{n}
, resulting in
D^{\prime}_{N;2n+1}(x)\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\bar{D}_{N;2n+1}^{\prime}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The Corollary follows.

∎

5.
Universal Solutions

The relations between Macdonald eigenvalue equations and Pieri rules are embodied by the duality property of Macdonald polynomials (
3.28
).
We now discuss a reformulation of this duality in terms of universal solutions along the same lines as the case of type
A
,
treated in Section
2.13
. This allows to re-prove the main results of this paper in terms of universal solutions.

5.1.
Universal solutions

5.1.1.
Universal Koornwinder-Macdonald and
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald eigenvalue solutions

As in the case of type A, it is extremely fruitful to think directly of
x
and
s

as dual variables, whose roles may be interchanged.
To this end, we use changes of variables:

x_{i}=q^{\mu_{i}}\,t^{{\rho_{i}}^{*}}=q^{\mu_{i}}\,at^{N-i},\quad s_{i}=q^{\lambda_{i}}\,t^{\rho_{i}}=q^{\lambda_{i}}\,\sigma t^{N-i},\qquad\lambda,\mu\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N},

where we use the notations of Section
3.1.4
, see Equation (
3.13
).
Note that if we write
P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=x^{\lambda}p_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)
and substitute this into the
Koornwinder-Macdonald eigenvalue equation (
3.5
), we obtain

\left(\frac{(1-t^{N})(1+\sigma t^{N-1})}{1-t}-\sigma t^{N-1}\hat{e}_{1}(s)+\sum_{i=1\atop\epsilon=\pm 1}^{N}\Phi_{i,\epsilon}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)({\Lambda}_{i}^{\epsilon}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}-1)\right)\,p_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)=0,

where we have conjugated (
3.4
) with
x^{-\lambda}
. Noting that
\Phi_{i,\epsilon}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)
can be expanded in series
of the variables
x^{-{\alpha}_{i}}
,
{\alpha}_{i}
the
B_{N}
simple roots, this suggests what we call a universal solution
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;x)
of the
Koornwinder-Macdonald eigenvalue equation (
3.5
)
in the form

(5.1)

P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)=q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}\,\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}c_{\beta}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s)\,x^{-\beta},\ \ c_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s)=1,

where
Q_{+}
denotes the positive cone of the root lattice of
B_{N}
.
The normalizing prefactor for
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)
is such that

q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}=x^{\lambda}\,t^{-\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}=s^{\mu}\,t^{-\rho\cdot\mu}
,
and is invariant under the interchange of
\lambda\leftrightarrow\mu
, therefore under
x\leftrightarrow s
.
Equation (
3.5
) for
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)
and generic
s
,

(5.2)

{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}\,P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)=\sigma t^{N-1}\,\hat{e}_{1}(s)\,P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s),

is equivalent to a linear triangular, generically nonsingular, system for the
coefficients
c_{\beta}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s)
, which uniquely fixes them for all
\beta\in Q_{+}
. The solution
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)
is therefore unique,
and we refer to it as
the universal Koornwinder-Macdonald solution
.
The normalization
{c}_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s)
uniquely fixes the solution, which is otherwise determined up to the overall normalization given by this coefficient.

Specializing the Koornwinder parameters
(a,b,c,d)
according to Table
1
,
we obtain the universal solutions

P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;s)
of the first
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald eigenvalue equations (
3.23
):

(5.3)

{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;s)=t^{\rho_{1}}\,\hat{e}_{1}(s)\,P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;s).

These universal solutions have the expansion

(5.4)

P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;s)=q^{\mu\cdot\lambda}\,\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}(R)}c_{\beta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s)\,x^{-\beta},\ \ c_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s)=1,

with the coefficients
c_{\beta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s)
uniquely determined by (
5.3
), up to the normalization
c_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s)
.

Remark 5.1
.

The apparent discrepancy between
Q_{+}

in (
5.1
) and
Q_{+}(R)
in (
5.4
) is just an artifact of the specializations.
For instance using the
C_{N}^{(1)}
specialization
(a,b,c,d)=t^{1/2}(1,-1,q^{1/2},-q^{1/2})

leads to an operator
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(C_{N})}(x;q,t)
which has an expansion as a series of the variables
\{x_{i+1}/x_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N-1}
and
x_{N}^{-2}
, as opposed to
the generic case
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)
which has an expansion as a series of the variables
\{x_{i+1}/x_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N-1}
and
x_{N}^{-1}
. This simply means that the series solution of the Macdonald eigenvalue equation at the specific
C_{N}^{(1)}
specialization is an even function of
x_{N}^{-1}
, i.e. the coefficients of odd powers of
x_{N}^{-1}
vanish.

Remark 5.2
.

Specializing
s=q^{\lambda}t^{\rho}
to
\lambda
an integer partition, and using the uniqueness of the universal solution, we recover
the Koornwinder polynomial

(5.5)

P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s=q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})=t^{-\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x).

The specialization therefore truncates the series (
5.1
) to finitely many terms.
Specializing the Koornwinder parameters to the values in Table
1
,

(5.6)

P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;s=q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})=t^{-\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\,P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),

for
\lambda
an integer partition,
\rho=\rho^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
and
\rho^{*}=\rho^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}
. Moreover, by uniqueness,
(
5.6
) holds also for
\lambda
any
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition, for example the non-integer partitions in the cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},B_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}
.
This presentation is therefore more economical, as a single universal function
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)

contains the information on all the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald polynomials as well.

Another important specialization of the universal Koornwinder function
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)
is the Koornwinder Baker-Akhiezer quasi-polynomials introduced by Chalykh
[
Cha02
]
. The latter correspond to specializing the paramaters
a,b,c,d,t
to arbitrary negative integer powers of
q
. The effect is again a truncation of the series to finitely many terms.

5.1.2.
Universal Pieri solutions

We also define the universal solution
Q^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;x)
of the first Pieri equation (
3.17
):

(5.7)

Q^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;x)=q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}\,\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}{\bar{c}}_{\beta}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)\,s^{-\beta},\ \ {\bar{c}}_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=1

subject to:

{\hat{e}}_{1}(x)\,Q^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;x)=\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;q,t)\,Q^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;x),

where

(5.8)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;q,t)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle t^{-\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\,{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;q,t)\,t^{\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{at^{N-1}}\,\Delta^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s)^{-1}\,{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s;q,t)\,\Delta^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s).

The series expansion (
5.7
) exists because
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;q,t)

has a series expansion in the variables
s^{-{\alpha}_{i}}
. As above, the normalization

{\bar{c}}_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=1
uniquely fixes the solution.

Specializing the parameters
(a,b,c,d)
as in Table
1
, we have the universal solutions to
the first
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Pieri equations (
3.32
):

(5.9)

{\hat{e}}_{1}(x)\,Q^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;x)=\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;q,t)\,Q^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;x),

where we have used the fact that
{\hat{e}}_{1}^{(R)}(x)={\hat{e}}_{1}(x)
for all
R
, and

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s=\Lambda t^{\rho};q,t):=t^{-\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\,{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(\Lambda;q,t)\,t^{\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}
,
with
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(\Lambda;q,t)
as in (
3.33
). The solution
Q^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;x)
is a series of the form

Q^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;x)=q^{\mu\cdot\lambda}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}^{*}}{\bar{c}}_{\beta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,s^{-\beta},\ \ {\bar{c}}_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=1,

where the sum extends over the positive root cone
Q_{+}^{*}
of
R^{*}
.
Due to triangularity, the coefficients of the series are uniquely determined by (
5.9
).

5.2.
Duality

5.2.1.
Duality of universal functions

The duality of Macdonald polynomials can be extended to the universal functions as follows.

Theorem 5.3
.

The functions
Q^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;x)
and
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)

are related via

(5.10)

Q^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;x)=\frac{P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)}{\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)},

with
\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}
as in (
3.14
).

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Theorem
2.15
.
The universal solution
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)
also obeys
the Pieri equation (
5.8
), as a consequence of the existence of a solution to the bispectral problem
[
Che95b
,
vD96
,
Sah99
,
Cha02
]
,
i.e. of both Koornwinder eigenvalue and Pieri equations, and of the uniqueness of the solution up to the overall normalization determined by the leading coefficient. Expanding the coefficients

c_{\beta}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s)=\sum_{\delta\in Q_{+}}c_{\beta,\delta}^{(a,b,c,d)}s^{-\delta}

allows to write an expansion

P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)=q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}\sum_{\delta\in Q_{+}}\hat{c}_{\delta}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)\,s^{-\delta},\ \ \hat{c}_{\delta}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}}c_{\beta,\delta}^{(a,b,c,d)}x^{-\beta}.

Ehe Pieri equation uniquely fixes the coefficients in this expansion, up to the overall factor
\hat{c}_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)
, hence
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)=\hat{c}_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)\,Q^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;x)
as a series in
s^{-{\alpha}_{i}}
.

To compute
\hat{c}_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)
, we note that it can be extracted as the
successive limits
s_{1}\to\infty
,
s_{2}\to\infty
, …,
s_{N}\to\infty
of
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)
. Let us examine
the
m
eigenvalue equation (
3.6
), for
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)=q^{\lambda.\mu}(\hat{c}_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)+O({\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{i}}))
. Writing

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}=d_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)+\sum_{I\subset[1,N]\atop|I|\leq m}\sum_{\epsilon_{k}=\pm 1\atop k\in I}d_{I,\epsilon,m}(x)\prod_{k\in I}\Gamma_{k}^{\epsilon_{k}},

we have

\left(d_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)-\theta_{m}\,\hat{e}_{m}(x)+\sum_{I\subset[1,N]\atop|I|\leq m}\sum_{\epsilon_{k}=\pm 1\atop k\in I}d_{I,\epsilon,m}(x)\prod_{k\in I}{\Lambda}_{k}^{\epsilon_{k}}\,\Gamma_{k}^{\epsilon_{k}}\right)(\hat{c}_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)+O({\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{i}}))=0,

where
\theta_{m}=\sigma^{m}t^{m(N-\frac{m+1}{2})}
. Dividing by
{\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{m}
, we find
that all the terms have a factor of the form
{\Lambda}^{-\beta}
for some
\beta\in Q_{+}
, and only those with
\beta=0

survive in the limit
|{\Lambda}_{1}|>>|{\Lambda}_{2}|>>\cdots>>|{\Lambda}_{m}|>>1
. This leaves us with

(5.11)

\left(\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}\cdots\Gamma_{m}-\frac{\theta_{m}^{2}}{d_{[1,m],(+)^{m},m}(x)}\right)\hat{c}_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=0.

Let us compute
d_{[1,m],(+)^{m},m}(x)
. From Definition
A.8
, and the explicit expression of the
van Diejen operators (
A.8
-
A.9
), we easily see that the coefficient of
\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}\cdots\Gamma_{m}
in
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}
comes from the highest order
van Diejen operator
{\mathcal{V}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}
, and more precisely from the term
s=1
,
J_{1}=J=\{1,2,...,m\}

in (
A.8
):

\displaystyle d_{[1,m],(+)^{m},m}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{(1-ax_{i})(1-bx_{i})(1-cx_{i})(1-dx_{i})}{(1-x_{i}^{2})(1-qx_{i}^{2})}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\frac{1-tx_{i}x_{j}}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}\frac{1-qtx_{i}x_{j}}{1-qx_{i}x_{j}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\prod_{1\leq i\leq m<j\leq N}\frac{1-tx_{i}x_{j}}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}\frac{tx_{i}-x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}.

Using

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{m}^{2}}{d_{[1,m],(+)^{m},m}(x)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{(1-\frac{1}{x_{i}^{2}})(1-\frac{1}{qx_{i}^{2}})}{(1-\frac{1}{ax_{i}})(1-\frac{1}{bx_{i}})(1-\frac{1}{cx_{i}})(1-\frac{1}{dx_{i}})}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\frac{1-\frac{1}{x_{i}x_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{tx_{i}x_{j}}}\frac{1-\frac{1}{qx_{i}x_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{qtx_{i}x_{j}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\prod_{1\leq i\leq m<j\leq N}\frac{1-\frac{1}{x_{i}x_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{tx_{i}x_{j}}}\frac{1-\frac{x_{j}}{x_{i}}}{1-\frac{x_{j}}{tx_{i}}}

we conclude that
\hat{c}_{0}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)
and
\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)
of (
3.14
) both obey
(
5.11
) for
m=1,2,...,N
, so their ratio must be a constant as it is invariant under
the action of each
\Gamma_{i}
. This constant is
1
, by noting that

q^{-\lambda\cdot\mu}P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)\to 1

and
\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}\to 1
in the limit when all
x^{-{\alpha}_{i}}\to 0
.
The Theorem follows.
∎

Using the specializations of the parameters
(a,b,c,d)
as in Table
1
,

Corollary 5.4
.

The functions
Q^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;x)
and
P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;s)

are related via

(5.12)

Q^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;x)=\frac{P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;s)}{\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)},

with
\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
as in (
3.29
).

It is now a simple exercise to relate the universal
(a,b,c,d)
Koornwinder-Pieri solution to the universal
solution of the
(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})
Koornwinder-Macdonald eigenvalue equation. As a result we have the following duality relation between universal Pieri solutions.

Theorem 5.5
.

We have the following duality formulas:

(5.13)

Q^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(x;s)=Q^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;x),

and their
{\mathfrak{g}}
specializations:

(5.14)

Q^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}(x;s)=Q^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(s;x).

Proof.

For conciseness, we omit the superscripts
(a,b,c,d)
and use the superscript
*
to stand for
(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})
. Starting from the equation
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(s)\,Q(s;x)=\hat{e}_{1}(x)\,Q(s;x)
, using (
5.8
), we have

{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{*}(s)\,\Delta^{*}(s)Q(s;x)=\sigma t^{N-1}\,\hat{e}_{1}(s)\,\Delta^{*}(s)Q(s;x).

Interchanging the variables
x\leftrightarrow s
, we find that
\Delta^{*}(x)Q(x;s)
is a solution to the

(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})
eigenvalue equation. Moreover, using the normalization of

Q(x;s)
with
s
and
x
interchanged, we have for small

\{x^{-{\alpha}_{i}}\}
:
\Delta^{*}(x)Q(x;s)=q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}\big(1+O(\{x^{-{\alpha}_{i}}\})\big)
. We conclude that

\Delta^{*}(x)Q(x;s)=P^{*}(x;s)
by uniqueness of the solution. The Theorem follows from

P^{*}(x;s)=\Delta^{*}(x)Q^{*}(s;x)
by Theorem
5.3
applied to
(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})
.
∎

This can alternatively be rephrased as duality between Koornwinder-Macdonald eigenvalue universal solutions:

(5.15)

\Delta^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s)P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)=\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)\,P^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s;x),

and their specializations:

(5.16)

\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}(s)\,P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;s)=\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,P^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}(s;x).

Some of the above relations appear in different guises in the literature: explicitly in the type A case
[
NS12
]
(see also Section
2.13
), implicitly
for the other types
[
Che95b
,
Che97
]
where universal functions are obtained as
x,s
-symmetric
reproducing kernels. We now detail the explicit link between the universal function duality relation (
5.15
)
and the Koornwinder polynomial duality (
3.12
).

Theorem 5.6
.

The universal function duality relation (
5.15
) reduces to the Koornwinder polynomial duality relation (
3.12
) upon specializing the variables
x=q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}}
and
s=q^{\lambda}t^{\rho}
for
\lambda,\mu
integer partitions.

Proof.

Starting from the universal function
P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;s)
, we use the specialization

s=q^{\lambda}t^{\rho}
, leading to the
Koornwinder polynomial

P^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})=t^{-\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\,P^{(a,b,c,d)}_{\lambda}(x)
. Similarly, using the specialization

x=q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}}
on the function
P^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s;x)
leads to

P^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s,q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}})=t^{-\rho\cdot\mu}\,P^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}_{\mu}(s)
.
The double specialization
s=q^{\lambda}t^{\rho},x=q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}}
of (
5.15
) results in

(5.17)

t^{-\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\,\Delta^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})\,P^{(a,b,c,d)}_{\lambda}(q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}})=t^{-\rho\cdot\mu}\,\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}})\,P_{\mu}^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(q^{\lambda}t^{\rho}).

The relation (
3.12
) follows from the following identity:

(5.18)

\frac{\Delta^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(q^{\lambda}t^{\rho})}{\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}})}=t^{\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda-\rho\cdot\mu}\,\frac{P_{\mu}^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(t^{\rho})}{P^{(a,b,c,d)}_{\lambda}(t^{\rho^{*}})},

itself a consequence of (
3.15
), and of the identity
\Delta^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(t^{\rho})=\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(t^{\rho^{*}})
.
The Theorem follows.
∎

Similarly, under the suitable specialization, the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald duality relation (
5.16
)
reduces to the Macdonald polynomial duality (
3.28
) for
x=q^{\mu}t^{\rho^{*}}
and
s=q^{\lambda}t^{\rho}
,
where
\lambda
is any
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition, and
\mu
any
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
-partition.

5.2.2.
Duality of universal solutions in the
q
-Whittaker limit

Universal solutions of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald eigenvector and
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Pieri equations simplify drastically in the
t\to\infty
limit.
They read:

(5.19)

\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;{\Lambda}):=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\,P^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;{\Lambda}t^{\rho})=x^{\lambda}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}(R)}c_{\beta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,x^{-\beta},

and

(5.20)

{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x):=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}\,Q^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda}t^{\rho};x)=x^{\lambda}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}(R^{*})}{\bar{c}}_{\beta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,{\Lambda}^{-\beta},

with
c_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})={\bar{c}}_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=1
.
Note that the limit
t\to\infty
has broken the previous symmetry
x\leftrightarrow s
,
as the
s
variable itself contained a
t
-dependent factor. However, Corollary
5.4
of the previous section turns into the following.

Theorem 5.7
.

The universal solutions
\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;{\Lambda})
and
{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x)
are related via:

(5.21)

\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;{\Lambda})=\bar{\Delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x),\ \ \bar{\Delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\prod_{\alpha\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{++}({\mathfrak{g}})}(1-x^{-\alpha}).

The eigenvalue and Pieri equations are:

(5.22)

\displaystyle D_{a}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;{\Lambda})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\Lambda}^{\omega_{a}^{*}}\,\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;{\Lambda}),

(5.23)

\displaystyle H_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\hat{e}}_{m}^{(R)}(x)\,{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x).

Both equations for
a,m=1
turn into simple triangular recursion relations for the
coefficients
c_{\beta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
and
{\bar{c}}_{\beta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
respectively.

5.3.
Fourier transform and proof of the Macdonald- Q-system conjecture

Like in the case of type A, we may reformulate the Fourier transform (
1.3
) in the
q
-Whittaker limit in terms of universal solutions via:

f(x)\,\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;{\Lambda})={\bar{f}}({\Lambda})\,\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;{\Lambda})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad f(x)\,{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x)={\bar{f}}({\Lambda})\,{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x).

The main result of Section
4
is the relation (
4.25
) which expresses the fact that
\bar{D}_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
is the Fourier transform of
D_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
.
In terms of the universal Pieri solution, we expect:

D_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x)=\bar{D}_{a;n}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x).

As a consequence any relation satisfied by the
\bar{D}
’s is satisfied by the
D
’s in the opposite direction, thus proving the Macdonald Q-system conjecture.
The proof of these identities is identical to that in Section 4, and relies on the Fourier duality between
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
and
\gamma^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, whose adjoint action respectively generates the discrete time translation in the
{\Lambda}
and
x
pictures:

\gamma^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x)=g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})\,{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x).

The proofs rely on a uniqueness argument which can be rephrased as follows. The universal Pieri solution
{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x)
(
5.20
) is fixed by the Pieri equation (
5.23
) for
m=1
, up to an overall multiplicative function independent of
{\Lambda}
, and fixed by the leading term normalization
{\bar{c}}_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=1
.
Any other series solution of this Pieri equation is therefore proportional to
{\bf K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda};x)
, by a factor independent of
{\Lambda}
.

As we saw in Section
4.3.3
, the case of odd times
a=N
for
{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)}
required the use of Rains operators, and the mapping to
a companion theory
B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}}
with its own
q
-Whittaker polynomials and quantum Q-system. Let us rephrase the action of Rains operators at finite
t
(
3.8
) in the
B_{N}^{(1)}
specialization
(a,b,c,d)=(t,-1,q^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})
of Table
1
in terms of universal Macdonald functions. Let us denote for short
P(x;s)\equiv P^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}(x;s)
and
P^{\prime}(x;s)\equiv P^{(B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}})}(x;s)
the respective universal Macdonald solutions. As both
B_{N}^{(1)},B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}}
share the same
\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}=\xi_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2}
, i.e.
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}=t^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
R({\mathfrak{g}})=R({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})
while
t^{\rho^{*}_{i}}=t^{N-i+1}
and
t^{(\rho^{\prime})^{*}_{i}}=q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{N-i+1}
,
we may rewrite (
3.8
) as:

\mathcal{R}_{N}^{(1,-1)}(x)\,P(x;s)=F(s)\,P^{\prime}(x;s),\qquad\mathcal{R}_{N}^{(t,-1)}(x)\,P^{\prime}(x;s)=F^{\prime}(s)\,P(x;s),

with

F(s)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+t^{-\frac{1}{2}}s_{i}),\quad F^{\prime}(s)=t^{N(N+1)/2}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+t^{-\frac{1}{2}}s_{i}^{-1}),

where we have used the same notations as in the proof of Lemma
A.10
, with

u=ab/\sigma=-t^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and taken into account the product formula
q^{|\lambda|}=t^{-N^{2}/2}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}
.
Taking the limit
t\to\infty
results in:

R_{N}^{(0)}\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda})=({\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\left(1+\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\,\Pi^{\prime}(x;{\Lambda}),\ \ R_{N}^{(1)}\,\Pi^{\prime}(x;{\Lambda})=({\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda}).

The steps of the proof can then be repeated identically, in particular establishing that

\gamma(x)\,\Pi^{\prime}(x;{\Lambda})=g^{\prime}({\Lambda})\,\Pi^{\prime}(x;{\Lambda})
, and then
