D_{a;n}^{\prime}\,\Pi^{\prime}(x;{\Lambda})=\bar{D}_{a;n}^{\prime}\,\Pi^{\prime}(x;{\Lambda})
for all
a\in[1,N]
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
as well as finally
D_{a;n}\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda})=\bar{D}_{a;n}\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda})
, from which the main Theorems follow.

6.
Discussion

6.1.
Companion quantum Q systems

Part of the proof of our main theorem, concerning the short label in type
B_{N}^{(1)}
(Section
4.3.3
), revealed that acting with the Rains operators (
3.8
) and their
\tau_{+}
-translates on the
q
-Whittaker functions gives rise to a companion system
B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}}

4.20
to the
B_{N}^{(1)}
quantum Q-system. This new system has the same classical (
q\to 1
limit) as the
B_{N}^{(1)}
quantum Q-system but corresponds to a different specialization of the Koornwinder parameters
(a,b,c,d)
, and has different Pieri operators and time translation operator (
4.32
).

One can obtain two further companion systems using the Rains operators, illustrated in Figure
1
,
from the theories at specialization parameters corresponding to

{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N-1}^{(2)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
. These also have the same classical limit as the respective quantum Q-systems, and may be considered as alternative quantizations. In type
A_{2N}^{(2)}
, which is not a cluster algebra mutation in the first place, the companion system is, in some sense, simpler and more natural from the quantization point of view, and appears to be the one related to the graded tensor product character formulas of KR-modules
[
Lin
]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{N}^{(1)}\!\!\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(t,-1,q^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2N}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(t,-1,t^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}},-t^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}}\!\!\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(1,-1,tq^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2N}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(q^{\frac{1}{2}}t,-q^{\frac{1}{2}},t^{\frac{1}{2}},-t^{\frac{1}{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2N-1}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(t^{\frac{1}{2}},-t^{\frac{1}{2}},q^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})\quad}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{N}^{(1)}\ \ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(1,-1,q^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2N-1}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(1,-1,t^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}},-t^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}})\quad}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{N+1}^{(2)}\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(t,-1,tq^{\frac{1}{2}},-q^{\frac{1}{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{N}^{(1)}\ \ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(t^{\frac{1}{2}},-t^{\frac{1}{2}},t^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}},-t^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{(0)}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{(2)}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{(1)}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{(1)}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{(0)}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{(0)}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{(2)}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{(1)}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{(2)}_{N}}

Figure 1.
The nine families of Koornwinder-Macdonald operators/polynomials. We have indicated the Rains operators that intertwine the various theories, and the specializations of the Koornwinder parameters
(a,b,c,d)
.

Using the definition of the Rains operators (
3.7
), together with the properties (
3.8
) of Section
3.1.3
,
from with the specializations of Table
1
, one obtains from the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald polynomials

P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
companion polynomials
P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})}(x)
, using
three different specializations of the Rains operators,

{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(0)}={\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(1,-1)}
,
{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(1)}={\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(t,-1)}
and
{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(2)}={\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(t^{\frac{1}{2}},-t^{\frac{1}{2}})}
.
In the cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)},C_{N}^{(1)}
, the Rains operators
leave the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald polynomials invariant up to some scalar, so that
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{g}}
. In those cases, the Rains operator is itself a Koornwinder-Macdonald operator, as shown in Lemma
A.13
.
Only the specializations
{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)}
and
A_{2N}^{(2)}
are mapped to new companion theories
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}\neq{\mathfrak{g}}
.

In the
q
-Whittaker limit, the Rains operators tend to
R_{N}^{(0)},R_{N}^{(1)}

of (
3.57
-
3.58
) and to

R^{(2)}_{N}=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-N(N+1)/2}{\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(t^{\frac{1}{2}},-t^{\frac{1}{2}})}=\sum_{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{N}=\pm 1}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{x_{i}^{2\epsilon_{i}}}{x_{i}^{2\epsilon_{i}}-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}-1}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma_{i}^{{\epsilon_{i}}/2}.

These map the
q
-Whittaker polynomials
\Pi_{\lambda}=\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
to a multiple of the
monic companions
\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})}=\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})}
.
More precisely (c.f. (
4.30
) for
B_{N}^{(1)}
), (
3.8
) implies the following mapping of polynomials:

\begin{array}[]{rll}A_{2N-1}^{(2)}:&R^{(0)}_{N}\,\Pi_{\lambda}=({\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime},&R^{(2)}_{N}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=({\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{\lambda},\\
A_{2N}^{(2)}:&R^{(2)}_{N}\,\Pi_{\lambda}=({\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime},&R^{(1)}_{N}\,\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=({\Lambda}_{1}{\Lambda}_{2}\cdots{\Lambda}_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{\lambda}.\end{array}

For the new companion cases
A_{2N-1}^{(2)\,^{\prime}},A_{2N}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}
, we define
companion Koornwinder-Macdonald operators by specializing Definition
A.8
for
m=1,2,...,N
at the appropriate values of the parameters as in Figure
1
. Their
q
-Whittaker limits are denoted by
D_{m}^{\prime}=D_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})}
, and their
\tau_{+}
-translates are defined for all
a\in[1,N]

and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
as follows:

\displaystyle A_{2N-1}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}:\ D^{\prime}_{a;2n+i}=q^{-na}\,\gamma^{-n}\,D^{\prime}_{a;i}\,\gamma^{n},\quad(i=-1,0)

\displaystyle A_{2N}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}:\ \ \ \ \ \ D^{\prime}_{a;n}=q^{-na/2}\,\gamma^{-n}\,D^{\prime}_{a}\,\gamma^{n},

where
D^{\prime}_{a;-1}
is defined as in (
3.50
) with the suitable function
\phi^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})}_{i,\epsilon}(x)
. Note that all labels of
A_{2N-1}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}

are short and all labels of
A_{2N}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}
are long, which is the reverse of the case for the un-primed theories.
Using the methods of this paper, it can be shown that these new operators obey new quantum Q-systems, with the same
q
-commutation relations and recursion relations as their non-primed companions, except for the label
N
, where

\begin{array}[]{rl}A_{2N-1}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}:&q^{N}\,Q_{N,2n+2}\,Q_{N,2n}=Q_{N,2n+1}^{2}-q^{-2n}\,Q_{N-1,2n+1}^{2},\\
&q^{N}\,Q_{N,2n+1}\,Q_{N,2n-1}=(Q_{N,2n}-q^{-n}Q_{N-1,2n})(Q_{N,2n}+q^{1-n}Q_{N-1,2n}),\\
A_{2N}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}:&q^{N}\,Q_{N,n+1}\,Q_{N,n-1}=Q_{N,n}^{2}-q^{-\frac{n}{2}}Q_{N,n}Q_{N-1,n}.\end{array}

for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. To derive these results, we first define the
candidate Fourier transforms
\bar{D}^{\prime}_{a;n}
via the
opposite quantum Q-systems and same initial
data as the non-primed companions (that is, the same values of
\omega_{a},\omega_{a}^{*}
), and compute the time translation operators:

\displaystyle A_{2N-1}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}:\ \ \ g^{\prime}=g_{T}g_{\Lambda}\frac{1}{({\Lambda}_{N}^{-2};q^{2})_{\infty}}g_{T}g_{\Lambda}\frac{1}{({\Lambda}_{N}^{-1};q)_{\infty}(-q{\Lambda}_{N}^{-1};q)_{\infty}},

\displaystyle A_{2N}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}:\ \ \ \ \ g^{\prime}=g_{T}g_{\Lambda}\,\frac{1}{({\Lambda}_{N}^{-1};q)_{\infty}},

which commute, respectively, with the two Pieri operators or Toda Hamiltonians

\displaystyle A_{2N-1}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}:\ \ \ H_{1}^{\prime}({\Lambda})=T_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}{\scriptstyle\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{i-1}}\right)}T_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}{\scriptstyle\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i+1}}{{\Lambda}_{i}}\right)}T_{i}^{-1}+{\scriptstyle\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)\left(1+\frac{q}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)}T_{N}^{-1},

\displaystyle A_{2N}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}:\ \ \ \ \ H_{1}^{\prime}({\Lambda})=T_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}{\scriptstyle\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{i-1}}\right)}T_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}{\scriptstyle\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{i+1}}{{\Lambda}_{i}}\right)}T_{i}^{-1}+{\scriptstyle\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}\right)}T_{N}^{-1}-{\scriptstyle\frac{q^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{{\Lambda}_{N}}}

obtained from the
q
-Whittaker limit of the specialized operators of Theorem (
B.1
). After proving that

g^{\prime}\Pi_{\lambda}=\gamma^{t_{1}}\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}
(
t_{1}=2
for
A_{2N-1}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}
,
t_{1}=1
for
A_{2N}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}
),
one concludes that
D_{a;n}\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\bar{D}_{a;n}^{\prime}\Pi_{\lambda}^{\prime}
and the quantum Q-system relations for
D_{a;n}
follow.

Finally, matching the specialization of the formula (
3.14
) for
\Delta^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})}
with the form of the product over affine roots (
3.29
), we make the following identification of affine roots corresponding to
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}}:\ \ \ \widehat{R}_{++}=\scriptstyle{\left\{(n-\frac{1}{2})\delta+e_{i}:\ 1\leq i\leq N,\ n\geq 1\right\}\sqcup\left\{n\delta+(e_{i}\pm e_{j}):\ 1\leq i<j\leq N,\ n\geq 1\right\}}

\displaystyle A_{2N-1}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}:\ \widehat{R}_{++}=\scriptstyle{\left\{(2n-1)\delta+2e_{i}:\ 1\leq i\leq N,\ n\geq 1\right\}\sqcup\left\{n\delta+(e_{i}\pm e_{j}):\ 1\leq i<j\leq N,\ n\geq 1\right\}}

\displaystyle A_{2N}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}:\ \ \ \widehat{R}_{++}=\scriptstyle{\left\{(n-\frac{1}{2})\delta+e_{i},\,2n\delta+2e_{i},:\ 1\leq i\leq N,\ n\geq 1\right\}\sqcup\left\{n\delta+(e_{i}\pm e_{j}):\ 1\leq i<j\leq N,\ n\geq 1\right\}}.

6.2.
Universal solutions and Path models

In Section
5
, we introduced universal solutions
P(x;s)
and
Q(s;x)
for the
various eigenvalue equations and Pieri rules, by solving an infinite triangular linear system of equations for the expansion coefficients in each case. Remarkably,
the solutions can all be put in the form of path models,
such as that for the
q
-Whittaker vectors in the
A_{N-1}^{(1)}
case

[
DFKT17
]
, see also
[
GT21
]
. As shown in Example
2.16
, this results in an interpretation of the solution of the triangular system as a partition function for weighted paths on the relevant positive root cone
Q_{+}
or
Q_{+}^{*}
.

Let us start with the case of
Q(s;x)=q^{\lambda\cdot\mu}\sum_{\beta\in Q_{+}^{*}}{\bar{c}}_{\beta}(x)\,s^{-\beta}
,
\bar{c}_{0}(x)=1
, the series solution of
the Pieri equation
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(s)\,Q(s;x)=\hat{e}_{1}(x)\,Q(s;x)
(either the Koornwinder or
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald Pieri rule).
The important fact is that
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(s)
is a
q
-difference operator in the variable
s

of the form
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(s)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=0,\pm 1}h_{i,\epsilon}(s)T_{i}^{\epsilon}
, where the coefficients
h_{i,\epsilon}
are rational functions
of the variables
\{s^{-{\alpha}_{i}^{*}}\}
, with
{\alpha}_{i}^{*}
the simple roots of
R^{*}
.
Factoring out the common denominator in the coefficients,
h_{i,\epsilon}(s)=p_{i,\epsilon}(s)/q(s)
with
p_{i,\epsilon}(s)
and
q(s)

some
polynomials
in
\{s^{-{\alpha}_{i}^{*}}\}
, the Pieri equation is equivalent to

\left(\hat{e}_{1}(x)\,q(s)-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=0,\pm 1}p_{i,\epsilon}(s)x_{i}^{\epsilon}T_{i}^{\epsilon}\right){q^{-\lambda\cdot\mu}Q(s;x)}=0.

The difference operator can be written as

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=0,\pm 1}\sum_{{\alpha}\in U^{*}}h_{i,\epsilon,{\alpha}}(x)s^{-{\alpha}}T_{i}^{\epsilon}

for some
x
-dependent coefficients
h_{i,\epsilon,{\alpha}}(x)
, where
{\alpha}
is summed over a
finite
subset
U^{*}
of
Q_{+}^{*}
.
Noting that
T_{i}^{\epsilon}\,s^{-\beta}=q^{-\epsilon e_{i}\cdot\beta}s^{-\beta}
and that
s^{-{\alpha}}\,s^{-\beta}=s^{-({\alpha}+\beta)}
,
and collecting the coefficient of
s^{-\beta}
in the Pieri equation, we obtain

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=0,\pm 1}\sum_{{\alpha}\in U^{*}}h_{i,\epsilon,{\alpha}}(x)\,q^{-\epsilon e_{i}\cdot(\beta-{\alpha})}\,{\bar{c}}_{\beta-{\alpha}}(x)=0,

or equivalently

\left(\sum_{i,\epsilon}h_{i,\epsilon,0}(x)q^{-\epsilon e_{i}\cdot\beta}\right){\bar{c}}_{\beta}(x)=-\sum_{i,\epsilon}\sum_{{\alpha}\in U^{*}\setminus\{0\}}h_{i,\epsilon,{\alpha}}(x)q^{-\epsilon e_{i}\cdot(\beta-{\alpha})}{\bar{c}}_{\beta-{\alpha}}(x).

The factor on the left hand side is non-vanishing for generic
x
and is therefore invertible.
The path model interpretation goes as follows. Given the initial data
\bar{c}_{0}(x)=1
, the coefficient
{\bar{c}}_{\beta}(x)

is the partition function of paths from
0
to
\beta
in
Q^{*}_{+}
, consisting of steps
in the finite subset
U^{*}\setminus\{0\}
. Each path has a weight equal to the product of vertex and edge weights along the path,
defined respectively as

w_{\varphi}=\left(\sum_{i,\epsilon}h_{i,\epsilon,0}(x)q^{-\epsilon e_{i}\cdot\varphi}\right)^{-1},\quad w_{0}=1,\quad w_{\varphi-{\alpha},\varphi}=-\sum_{i,\epsilon}h_{i,\epsilon,{\alpha}}(x)q^{-\epsilon e_{i}\cdot(\varphi-{\alpha})}

for any
\varphi\in Q_{+}^{*}
,
{\alpha}\in U^{*}\setminus\{0\}
. The weight of a path
p
is a product over its vertex and edge sets
v(p),e(p)
:

w(p)=\prod_{\varphi\in v(p)}w_{\varphi}\times\prod_{(\varphi-{\alpha},\varphi)\in e(p)}w_{\varphi-{\alpha},\varphi},

and the coefficient
\bar{c}_{\beta}(x)
is the partition function of all such paths

{\bar{c}}_{\beta}(x)=\sum_{{\rm paths}\,p\ {\rm in}\ Q_{+}^{*}\atop{\rm from}\ 0\to\beta}w(p).

A similar argument leads to a formulation of the Macdonald eigenvalue equation universal solution
P(x;s)
in terms of a path model on
Q_{+}
.

The path model for the Pieri solution simplifies drastically in the
q
-Whittaker limit
as the Hamiltonians are directly polynomials of the
{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{i}^{*}}
, giving rise to a small set of steps
U^{*}\setminus\{0\}
. For instance, by inspection of (
3.46
-
3.47
), we find that for all
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
w_{\varphi}^{-1}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}x_{i}^{\epsilon}(1-q^{-\epsilon\beta\cdot e_{i}})
.

Example 6.1
.

In the case
{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{4}^{(2)}
, the
q
-Whittaker limit of the Pieri equation is

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(T_{1}+\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{2}}{{\Lambda}_{1}}\right)(T_{2}+T_{1}^{-1})+\left(1-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{2}}\right)T_{2}^{-1}-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{2}}-(x_{1}+x_{1}^{-1}+x_{2}+x_{2}^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\sum_{n,m\geq 0}{\tilde{c}}_{n,m}(x)\left(\frac{{\Lambda}_{2}}{{\Lambda}_{1}}\right)^{n}\left(\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{2}}\right)^{m}

expressed in terms of the variables
{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{1}}=\frac{{\Lambda}_{2}}{{\Lambda}_{1}},{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{2}}=\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{2}}
,

{\alpha}_{i}
the simple roots of
B_{2}
, and with the normalization
{\tilde{c}}_{0,0}(x)=1
.
The is equivalent to the recursion relation

{\tilde{c}}_{n,m}(x)=\frac{q^{n-1}(q^{-m}x_{2}+x_{1}^{-1}){\tilde{c}}_{n-1,m}(x)+(q^{m-n-1}x_{2}^{-1}+1){\tilde{c}}_{n,m-1}(x)}{x_{1}(q^{-n}-1)+x_{1}^{-1}(q^{n}-1)+x_{2}(q^{n-m}-1)+x_{2}^{-1}(q^{m-n}-1)},

which has the form
{\tilde{c}}_{n,m}(x)=a_{n,m}{\tilde{c}}_{n-1,m}(x)+b_{n,m}{\tilde{c}}_{n,m-1}(x)
, the solution is

{\tilde{c}}_{n,m}(x)=\sum_{{\rm paths}\,p:(0,0)\to(n,m)}\prod_{{\rm steps}\,s\in p}w_{s}\ ,

where the is sum over all the paths on
{\mathbb{Z}}_{+}^{2}
with steps
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, from the origin to
(n,m)
, of the product of step weights
w_{s}
where

w_{s}=a_{i,j}
for a horizontal step
(i-1,j)\to(i,j)
and
w_{s}=b_{i,j}
for a vertical step
(i,j-1)\to(i,j)
.

6.3.
Universal solutions and
q
-Whittaker functions

We have used the terminology
q
-Whittaker functions for the
t\to\infty
limits of Koornwinder-Macdonald polynomials. Strictly speaking,

\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
is a class-
1

q
-Whittaker function for the quantum universal enveloping algebra of a simple Lie algebra only in the cases where the Pieri operators are known to be
q
-deformed quantum Toda Hamiltonians.
These are related to the quantum Q-system of types

{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{N-1}^{(1)},D_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)}
, whose conserved quantities are the relativistic Toda Hamiltonians,
of types
A_{N-1},D_{N},B_{N},C_{N}
, respectively (see Remark
3.10
).
By analogy, we call all the eigenfunctions
\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

q
-Whittaker functions for all
{\mathfrak{g}}
in this paper.
These share a number of properties.

In the
q
-Whittaker limit, the universal series solutions of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald eigenvalue equations and the Pieri equations (
5.19
-
5.20
) of Section
5
,
when specialized to
\lambda
a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition, reduce respectively to the analogues of
class-1
q
-Whittaker functions
\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q^{\lambda})
, and the analogues of fundamental

q
-Whittaker functions

\Psi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\mathrm{K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q^{\lambda})
. The former is a Weyl-symmetric polynomial in
x
, whereas the latter is a non-symmetric series solution of the relativistic Toda equation with prescribed leading term. It is associated with the highest weight Verma module
V_{\mu}
, where
\mu
is obtained from the variable
x=q^{\rho+\mu}
.
In the theory of Whittaker functions, the fundamental Whittaker function is convergent only in a particular Weyl chamber. The class-1 function is a linear combination of fundamental Whittaker functions, regular in all the Weyl chambers.
It is obtained via a symmetrization over the Weyl group action on the fundamental solutions with suitable coefficients.

A subtlety arises in the study of convergence of the above series. In the definition of
\bar{\Delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
as an infinite product, it is assumed that
|q|<1
for convergence.
On the other hand, the series
\mathrm{K}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(q^{\lambda};x)
or
\Psi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
is well-behaved for
|q|>1
.
Obviously
\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, being a polynomial, makes sense for both
|q|>1
and
|q|<1
.
Note that the infinite product

\tilde{\Delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=\frac{1}{\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\prod_{{\alpha}\in R_{+}}(1-q^{-n}x^{-{\alpha}})}

is another solution of (
2.39
), convergent for
|q|>1
instead of
|q|<1
. (To see this, note that conjugating

\Gamma_{i}^{\pm 1}
with
\bar{\Delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
or
\tilde{\Delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
yields the same result.). The remarkable fact is that as a convergent series for
|q|>1
,

\tilde{\Delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Psi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
is not equal to the polynomial class-
1

q
-Whittaker function
\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
. We conjecture
7
7

7

Such a symmetrization formula exists relating classical fundamental and class-
1
Whittaker functions.
that for general
{\mathfrak{g}}

it requires a symmetrization over the Weyl group, valid for
|q|>1
:

(6.1)

\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\sum_{w\in W}\tilde{\Delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(wx)\,\Psi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(wx).

We end up with two characterizations of
\mathrm{K}({\Lambda};x)
: (1) as a series with
|q|<1
in the variables
{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{i}}
, equal to
\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;{\Lambda})/\bar{\Delta}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)

(by Theorem
5.7
) and (2) as a series with
|q|>1
equal to
\Psi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
when
\lambda
is a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-partition. The polynomials
\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
are expressed in terms of both, but with very different formulas for
|q|<1
and
|q|>1
.

Example 6.2
.

Let us illustrate the above in the simplest case of
A_{1}^{(1)}
. Denoting by
u=x_{2}/x_{1}
,

v={\Lambda}_{2}/{\Lambda}_{1}
,

\displaystyle p(u;v)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle x_{1}^{-\lambda_{1}}x_{2}^{-\lambda_{2}}\,\Pi(x;{\Lambda})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u^{n}\,\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{1-vq^{i}}{1-q^{-i-1}},

\displaystyle k(v;u)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle x_{1}^{-\lambda_{1}}x_{2}^{-\lambda_{2}}\,\mathrm{K}({\Lambda};x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}v^{n}\,\frac{q^{n^{2}}u^{n}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1-q^{i})(1-uq^{i})}.

With
\bar{\Delta}(u)=(qu;q)_{\infty}
and
\tilde{\Delta}(u)=1/(u;q^{-1})_{\infty}
,

\displaystyle p(u;v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}(u)\,k(v;u)\qquad(|q|<1),

\displaystyle\Pi_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\,\tilde{\Delta}(u)\,k(q^{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}};u)+x_{2}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{1}^{\lambda_{2}}\,\tilde{\Delta}(u^{-1})\,k(q^{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}};u^{-1})\quad(|q|>1),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}x_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\,p(u;q^{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}})\quad(|q|<1),

where the second and third lines hold for any integers
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq 0
.

6.4.
Summary/Perspectives

We have proved the Macdonald operator-quantum Q-system conjectures, which state that suitably defined,

\tau_{+}
-translated
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald difference operators, in the
q
-Whittaker limit, obey the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-type quantum Q-system
relations, and may as such be considered as cluster variables in a suitable quantum cluster algebra in all cases except
A_{2N}^{(2)}
.
The proofs cover the cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{N-1}^{(1)},D_{N}^{(1)},B_{N}^{(1)},C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},A_{2N}^{(2)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}
, and rely strongly on the duality between the Koornwinder/Macdonald eigenvalue equations and the associated Pieri rules.

We have proved that the conserved quantities of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-quantum Q-systems are the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Pieri operators in the
q
-Whittaker limit,
which in a number of cases can themselves be identified with known
q
-difference Toda Hamiltonians. Our construction provides explicit expressions for all (higher) Hamiltonians as well. It would be interesting to explore the combinatorial content of these, possibly in the language of cluster integrable systems

[
GK13
]
.

We have constructed time-translation operators
g({\Lambda})
for all
{\mathfrak{g}}
that commute with the corresponding
q
-difference Toda Hamiltonians. More generally, in the spirit of
[
SS18
]
, it would be desirable to construct commuting Baxter
Q
-operators
Q(u;{\Lambda})

that coincide with
g({\Lambda})^{-1}
at
u=1
, but are in general quantum-dilogarithmic generating functions for the Hamiltonians. In all cases but

A_{2N}^{(2)}
, such a construction should exist in terms of mutations of the corresponding quantum cluster algebra.

As another by-product of our proofs, we have unearthed a remarkable structure involving three more
companion theories
B_{N}^{(1)\,^{\prime}}
,
A_{2N-1}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}
and
A_{2N}^{(2)\,^{\prime}}
and their associated quantum
Q-systems. It would be interesting to further investigate their quantum Laurent property, as well as their combinatorial content, in particular the meaning of the associated fermionic sums which in the known cases provide q-multiplicities of decompositions of KR modules onto irreducibles (see
[
DFK14
,
DFK18
]
).

The quantum Q-system relates to the
q
-Whittaker limit of Macdonald-Koornwinder theory, but it is natural to consider the extension of some of our results to finite
t
. The time-shifted Macdonald operators
\mathcal{D}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{a;n}(x;q,t)
by iterated conjugation by
\gamma(x)
were considered in
[
DFK19
]
in type
A_{N-1}^{(1)}
, and are generators of the spherical DAHA or, in the limit
N\to\infty
, the quantum toroidal algebra of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
or the elliptic Hall algebra.
In the Koornwinder we again have elements of the corresponding spherical DAHA but the limit
N\to\infty
is still to be understood.

Moreover, in type
A_{N-1}^{(1)}
a
t
-deformed analogue of the time translation operator
g({\Lambda})
was defined in
[
LNS20
]
(see Proposition 4.2),
and conjectured to act diagonally on the so-called Non-stationary Ruijsenaars function, introduced in
[
Sh19
]
as the (conjectural) universal series solution to the elliptic Ruijsenaars operator eigenvalue equation, also related to the geometry of affine Laumon spaces. We expect this operator to play the same role for the finite
t
macdonald case as
g(\Lambda)
for the q-Whittaker limit
t\to\infty
, i.e. to be the (Macdonald) Fourier transform of the Gaussian for finite
t
.
We believe there should exist a cluster algebra formulation of this operator.
It would also be extremely interesting to investigate this operator for general Koornwinder theory.

Appendix A
Derivation of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators

We combine several constructions
[
Mac01
,
vD95
,
Rai05
]
of commuting difference operators corresponding to the affine algebras in Table
1
. The goal is to construct an appropriate set of
N
commuting operators for each
{\mathfrak{g}}
, with eigenvalues proportional to the symmetric functions in Table
2
, which form a basis for the space spanned by the irreducible fundamental characters of the Lie algebras
R
.
This choice of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald difference operators is designed such that their
q
-Whittaker limits satisfy the type
{\mathfrak{g}}
quantum Q-systems.

A.1.
Macdonald’s operators

For each affine algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
in Table
1
, except for the case of
A_{2n}^{(2)}
, and for each minuscule co-weight of
S
, Macdonald defines a difference operator with eigenvalue which is a fundamental character of
R^{*}

[
Mac01
]
.

Let
\{e_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
be the standard basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
with the standard inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)
. For the set of variables
x=(x_{1},...,x_{N})
, we denote
x^{v}=x_{1}^{v_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{v_{N}}
for any
v=\sum_{i}v_{i}e_{i}
.

\begin{array}[]{|c|l|l|}\hline\cr\hbox{Algebra}&\hbox{Positive roots $R_{+}$}&\hbox{fundamental weights $\omega_{a}$}\\
\hline\cr B_{N}&e_{i},\ i\in[1,N];&\displaystyle{\omega_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{i}e_{k},\ i\in[1,N-1]};\\
&e_{i}\pm e_{j},\ 1\leq i<j\leq N&\displaystyle{\omega_{N}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{N}e_{k}}\\
\hline\cr C_{N}&e_{i}\pm e_{j},\ 1\leq i<j\leq N;\ 2e_{i},\ i\in[1,N]&\displaystyle{\omega_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{i}e_{k},\ i\in[1,N]}\\
\hline\cr D_{N}&e_{i}\pm e_{j},\ 1\leq i<j\leq N&\displaystyle{\omega_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{i}e_{k},\ i\in[1,N-2];}\\
&&\displaystyle{\omega_{N}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{N}e_{k},\ \omega_{N-1}=\omega_{N}-e_{N}}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 4.
Positive roots and fundamental weights of the finite dimensional algebras of types BCD.

There is a surjective map
{}_{*}:{R}\to{S}
,
{\alpha}\mapsto{\alpha}_{*}={\alpha}/u_{\alpha}\in S
, for some real
u_{\alpha}
.
In the case where
R\neq S
,
u_{\alpha}=\frac{({\alpha},{\alpha})}{2}
, so that
u_{\alpha}=1
in all cases but for the short roots of type
B_{N}
or the long roots of type
C_{N}
, in which case it is equal to
\frac{1}{2}
or 2, respectively.

Let
\pi=\sum\pi_{i}e_{i}
, and define
8
8

8

We choose Macdonald’s parameters
t_{\alpha}=t
for all
\alpha
,
independently of the length
{\alpha}
. This allows us to obtain the dual
q
-Whittaker limit by simply taking
t\to\infty
.

(A.1)

\Phi_{\pi}:=\prod_{{\alpha}\in{R}_{+}\atop(\pi,{\alpha}_{*})=1}\frac{1-tx^{\alpha}}{1-x^{\alpha}},\qquad\Gamma_{\pi}=\prod_{i}\Gamma_{i}^{\pi_{i}}.

For each minuscule weight
\pi
of
{S}^{\vee}=\{2\frac{{\alpha}}{({\alpha},{\alpha})},\,{\alpha}\in S\}
, i.e. a weight such that
(\pi,{\alpha}_{*})\in\{0,\pm 1\}

for all
{\alpha}\in{R}
, there is a Macdonald difference operator
{\mathcal{E}}_{\pi}
which acts on functions
f(x)
by the symmetrization

(A.2)

{\mathcal{E}}_{\pi}f=\sum_{w\in W}w\left(\Phi_{\pi}\Gamma_{\pi}f\right).

We list below the explicit formulas for each case treated in
[
Mac01
]
.
The construction refers to the positive roots and fundamental weights for the simple Lie groups of types
BCD
in Table
4
.

A.1.1.
Macdonald operators for
D_{N}^{(1)}

Here,
R=S
is the root system of type
D_{N}
.
There are three minuscule weights:
\omega_{1}=e_{1}
,
\omega_{N-1}

and
\omega_{N}
.
Equation (
A.1
) becomes

\displaystyle\Phi_{\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=2}^{N}\frac{1-tx_{1}x_{j}}{1-x_{1}x_{j}}\frac{tx_{1}-x_{j}}{x_{1}-x_{j}},\ \ \Phi_{\omega_{N-1}}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N-1}\frac{1-tx_{i}x_{j}}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}\frac{tx_{i}-x_{N}}{x_{i}-x_{N}},

\displaystyle\Phi_{\omega_{N}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{1-tx_{i}x_{j}}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}.

The Weyl group of
D_{N}
acts on the set
(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{N})
by permutations of the indices and
inversions of an even number of variables. The three Macdonald operators are

(A.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{1}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}}\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon},

(A.4)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N-1}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon_{1},...,\epsilon_{N}=\pm 1\atop\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}\cdots\epsilon_{N}=-1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma_{i}^{\frac{\epsilon_{i}}{2}},

(A.5)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon_{1},...,\epsilon_{N}=\pm 1\atop\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}\cdots\epsilon_{N}=1}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma_{i}^{\frac{\epsilon_{i}}{2}}.

These will be identified below as
{\mathcal{D}}_{a}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}(x;q,t)={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{a}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}
with
a=1,N-1,N
.

A.1.2.
Macdonald operator for
B_{N}^{(1)}

Here,
R=S
is the root system of
B_{N}

There is a unique minuscule weight of
S^{\vee}=C_{N}
,

\pi=e_{1}=\omega_{1}
, with

\Phi_{\omega_{1}}=\frac{1-tx_{1}}{1-x_{1}}\prod_{j=2}^{N}\frac{1-tx_{1}x_{j}}{1-x_{1}x_{j}}\frac{tx_{1}-x_{j}}{x_{1}-x_{j}}.

The Weyl group
W\simeq S_{N}\ltimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
is generated by all permutations and inversions of the variables in the set
x=(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{N})
, so the corresponding difference operator is

{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{1}}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}=\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon}}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}}\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}.

This will be identified as
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}(x;q,t)={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{1}}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}
.

A.1.3.
Macdonald operator for
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)}

Here,
R=S
is the root system of type
C_{N}
.
There is a unique minuscule weight of
S^{\vee}=B_{N}
,
\pi=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}=\omega_{N}
,
with

\Phi_{\omega_{N}}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{2}}{1-x_{i}^{2}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{1-tx_{i}x_{j}}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}.

The Weyl group is is the same as for type
B_{N}
, resulting in the difference operator

(A.6)

{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(C_{N}^{(1)})}=\sum_{\epsilon_{1},...,\epsilon_{N}=\pm 1}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{2\epsilon_{i}}}{1-x_{i}^{2\epsilon_{i}}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma_{i}^{\frac{\epsilon_{i}}{2}}.

This will be identified as
{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(C_{N}^{(1)})}(x;q,t)={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(C_{N}^{(1)})}
.

A.1.4.
Macdonald operator for
{\mathfrak{g}}=A_{2N-1}^{(2)}

Here,
(R,S)=(C_{N},B_{N})
.
The map
*:R\to S
is given by
(e_{i}\pm e_{j})_{*}=e_{i}\pm e_{j}
, and
(2e_{i})_{*}=e_{i}
.
There is a unique minuscule weight of
S^{\vee}=C_{N}
,
\pi=e_{1}=\omega_{1}
, and

\Phi_{\omega_{1}}=\frac{1-tx_{1}^{2}}{1-x_{1}^{2}}\prod_{j=2}^{N}\frac{1-tx_{1}x_{j}}{1-x_{1}x_{j}}\frac{tx_{1}-x_{j}}{x_{1}-x_{j}}.

Summing over the Weyl group of type
C_{N}
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{1}}^{(A_{2N-1}^{(2)})}=\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{2\epsilon}}{1-x_{i}^{2\epsilon}}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}}\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}.

This will be identified as
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(A_{2N-1}^{(2)})}(x;q,t)={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{1}}^{(A_{2N-1}^{(2)})}
.

Remark A.1
.

The algebra
A_{2N-1}^{(2)}
is obtained from
A_{2N-1}^{(1)}
by a folding procedure using the natural
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
automorphism. Remarkably,
this extends to the difference operators as follows. Consider the specialization
\tau
of
x=(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{2N})
obtained by setting
x_{2N+1-i}=x_{i}^{-1}
,
i=1,2,...,N
,
and accordingly
\Gamma_{2N+1-i}=\Gamma_{i}^{-1}
. We have

\tau\big({\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(A_{2N-1}^{(1)})}(x;q,t)\big)={\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(A_{2N-1}^{(2)})}(x;q,t).

However, the
A_{2N-1}^{(1)}
-Macdonald polynomials specialized via
\tau
have a non-trivial decomposition onto the basis of

A_{2N-1}^{(2)}
-Macdonald polynomials.

A.1.5.
Macdonald operator for
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N+1}^{(2)}

Here,
(R,S)=(B_{N},C_{N})
.
The map
*:R\to S
is given by
(e_{i}\pm e_{j})_{*}=e_{i}\pm e_{j}
, and
(e_{i})_{*}=2e_{i}
. There is a unique minuscule weight
\pi=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}=\omega_{N}
of type
S^{\vee}=B_{N}
, so that

\Phi_{\omega_{N}}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1-tx_{i}}{1-x_{i}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{1-tx_{i}x_{j}}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}.

Summing over the Weyl group of type
C_{N}
gives

(A.7)

{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}=\sum_{\epsilon_{1},...,\epsilon_{N}=\pm 1}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}/2}.

This will be identified as
{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}(x;q,t)={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}
.

A.2.
Higher order Koornwinder-Macdonald difference operators

For generic parameters
(a,b,c,d,q,t)
, Koornwinder defined the first order
q
-difference operator whose eigenfunctions are the Koornwinder polynomials, invariant under the Weyl group of type
C
.
Consequently, van Diejen
[
vD95
]
defined a commuting family of higher order difference operators with the same eigenfunctions. We recall this construction in
A.2.1
.
Using the spectrum of these operators, we construct in
A.2.2

linear combinations of these, such that their eigenvalues are proportional to
elementary symmetric functions
{\hat{e}}_{a}(s)
.
We add to this certain higher order operators due to Rains
[
Rai05
]
.
Upon specialization of the parameters (a,b,c,d), we combine this in Sect.
A.2.4
,
with Macdonald’s construction of Section
A.1
.

A.2.1.
van Diejen’s higher order Koornwinder difference operators

Definition A.2
.

The van Diejen operator of order
m\in[1,N]
is

(A.8)

{\mathcal{V}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}:=\sum_{J\subset[1,N],\ |J|=m\atop\epsilon_{j}=\pm 1\ \forall\ j\in J}\sum_{s=1}^{m}(-1)^{s-1}\sum_{\emptyset\subsetneq J_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq J_{s}=J}\prod_{r=1}^{s}V_{\{x\},\{\epsilon\};J_{r}\setminus J_{r-1};K_{r}}^{(a,b,c,d)}(\prod_{j\in J_{1}}\Gamma_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}-1),

where
J_{0}=\emptyset
,
K_{r}=[1,N]\setminus J_{r}
and

(A.9)

\displaystyle V_{\{x\},\{\epsilon\};J;K}^{(a,b,c,d)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in J}\frac{(1-ax_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}})(1-bx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}})(1-cx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}})(1-dx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}})}{(1-x_{i}^{2\epsilon_{i}})(1-qx_{i}^{2\epsilon_{i}})}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{{i<j}\atop{i,j\in J}}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}\frac{1-qtx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}{1-qx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}}}\prod_{i\in J\atop j\in K}\frac{1-tx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}}{1-x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}x_{j}}\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}-x_{j}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}-x_{j}},

where
J,K\subset[1,N]
are such that
J\cap K=\emptyset
.

When
m=1
, the van Diejen operator is the Koornwinder operators of Equation (
3.2
).

Example A.3
.

Define

(A.10)

A^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=\frac{(1-ax)(1-bx)(1-cx)(1-dx)}{(1-x^{2})(1-qx^{2})}.

Then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}_{2}^{(a,b,c,d)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq N\atop\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}=\pm 1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}\left(A^{(a,b,c,d)}(x_{i_{\ell}}^{\epsilon_{\ell}})\prod_{k\neq i_{1},i_{2}}\frac{1-tx_{i_{\ell}}^{\epsilon_{\ell}}x_{k}}{1-x_{i_{\ell}}^{\epsilon_{\ell}}x_{k}}\frac{tx_{i_{\ell}}^{\epsilon_{\ell}}-x_{k}}{x_{i_{\ell}}^{\epsilon_{\ell}}-x_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle\times\left\{\frac{1-tx_{i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}}{1-x_{i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}}\frac{1-qtx_{i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}}{1-qx_{i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}}(\Gamma_{i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}\Gamma_{i_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}-1)-\frac{1-tx_{i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}x_{i_{2}}}{1-x_{i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}x_{i_{2}}}\frac{tx_{i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}-x_{i_{2}}}{x_{i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}-x_{i_{2}}}(\Gamma_{i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}-1)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-\frac{1-tx_{i_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}x_{i_{1}}}{1-x_{i_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}x_{i_{1}}}\frac{tx_{i_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}-x_{i_{1}}}{x_{i_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}-x_{i_{1}}}(\Gamma_{i_{2}}^{\epsilon_{2}}-1)\right\},

where the sum over
s
in (
A.8
) decomposes into three terms, with
s=1,J_{1}=J=\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
,

s=2,J_{1}=\{i_{1}\},J_{2}=J=\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
and
s=2,J_{1}=\{i_{2}\},J_{2}=J=\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
.

The operators (
A.8
) form a commuting family of difference operators
with common eigenfunctions being the Koornwinder polynomials.
We give a description of their eigenvalues.
Let
\sigma=\sqrt{\frac{abcd}{q}}
.
Recall the elementary and complete symmetric functions
e_{k}(x)=s_{1^{k}}(x)

and
h_{k}(x)=s_{m}(x)
.

Definition A.4
.

For arbitrary
\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{N}
, and
k,m\in[1,N]
, we define the collections of variables

\displaystyle u^{(k)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\sigma t^{k-i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k},

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{s_{i}+s_{i}^{-1}\}_{1\leq i\leq N},

\displaystyle w_{(m)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\sigma t^{N-i}+\sigma^{-1}t^{-(N-i)}\}_{m\leq i\leq N},

with
s_{i}=\sigma t^{N-i}q^{\lambda_{i}}
as usual,
and the functions

(A.11)

\displaystyle d_{m}^{(k)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sigma^{m}\,t^{m(k-\frac{m+1}{2})}\,{\hat{e}}_{m}(u^{(k)}),

(A.12)

\displaystyle d_{\lambda;m}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sigma^{m}\,t^{m(N-\frac{m+1}{2})}\,{\hat{e}}_{m}(s),

(A.13)

\displaystyle f_{\lambda;m}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sigma^{m}\,t^{m(N-\frac{m+1}{2})}\,\sum_{j=0}^{m}(-1)^{j}\,e_{m-j}(v)\,h_{j}(w_{(m)}).

The spectral theorem for van Diejen operators is

Theorem A.5
.

[
vD95
]

The (monic) symmetric Koornwinder polynomials
P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)
satisfy

(A.14)

{\mathcal{V}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=f_{\lambda;m}\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x).

A.2.2.
Koornwinder-Macdonald operators

Using the spectral theorem
A.14
, we can construct linear combinations of van Diejen’s operators with eigenvalues equal to
d_{\lambda;m}
. To do this we prove two combinatorial lemmas about symmetric functions.
Given a set of variables
x=\{x_{1},...,x_{N}\}
, we define associated set

{\tilde{x}}=\{x_{i}+x_{i}^{-1},i\in[1,N]\}
, and if
\beta\leq N
, define
x^{[\beta]}=\{x_{1},...,x_{\beta}\}
and
\tilde{x}^{[\beta]}=\{x_{i}+x_{i}^{-1},i\in[1,\beta]\}
. In particular,
x^{[0]}=\emptyset
.

Lemma A.6
.

For all
r\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
,

(A.15)

\theta_{r,n}(u):=\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}\,(-1)^{\ell}e_{r-\ell}(u^{[n-\ell]})h_{\ell}(u^{[n-\ell+1]})=\delta_{r,0}.

Proof.

When
r=0
the sum is trivially equal to 1. We consider
r>0
.
We define two types of integer configurations. A fermionic configuration on
\{1,...,p\}
with
j
particles, denoted by
F
, is a set of
j
distinct integers in the set
[1,p]
. A bosonic configuration on
\{1,...,p^{\prime}\}
with
j^{\prime}
particles, denoted by
B
, is a sequence of
j^{\prime}
integers in
[1,p^{\prime}]
which are not necessarily distinct. We assign a weight
w_{F}=\prod_{i\in F}u_{i}
to a fermionic configuration, and a weight
w_{B}=\prod_{i\in B}(-u_{i})
to a bosonic configuration. Moreover, to the pair
(F,B)
, we assign a weight
w_{F,B}=w_{F}w_{B}
. The partition function of
j
fermions on
[1,p]
is
e_{j}(u^{[p]})
, and the partition function of
j^{\prime}
bosons on
[1,p^{\prime}]
is
h_{j^{\prime}}(-u^{[p^{\prime}]})=(-1)^{j^{\prime}}h_{j^{\prime}}(u^{[p^{\prime}]})
.

We define the following set of pairs of fermionic and bosonic configurations:

\mathcal{S}_{n,r}=\left\{(F,B)|\,t_{F}:=\max(F)\leq n-|B|,t_{B}:=\max(B)\leq n-|B|+1,|F|+|B|=r\right\}

where
|X|
is the cardinality of the set
X
, and if
|X|=0
we define
\max(X)=0
.
The identity (
A.15
) is an identity for the partition function:

Z_{n,r}:=\sum_{(F,B)\in\mathcal{S}_{n,r}}w_{F,B}=\delta_{r,0}.

To prove this, for any
r\geq 1
we construct a fixed point-free involution
\Phi
on the set
\mathcal{S}_{n,r}
, such that
\Phi(w_{F}w_{B})=-w_{F}w_{B}
.
If such an involution exists, the partition function for any
r>0
vanishes:

\sum_{(F,B)\in\mathcal{S}_{n,r}}w_{(F,B)}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(F,B)\in\mathcal{S}_{n,r}}(w_{(F,B)}+w_{\Phi(F,B)})=0\ .

Figure 2.
An illustration of the involution
\Phi
. Case (a) has
t_{F}=6<t_{B}=7
, hence we move the topmost rightmost bosonic particle to a fermionic particle at position
t_{F}^{\prime}=t_{B}
. Case (b) has
t_{F}=6\geq t_{B}=5
, hence we move the fermionic particle to a bosonic one at position
t_{B^{\prime}}=t_{F}
.

The involution
\Phi
is illustrated in Figure
2
in the language of particles, namely by considering
F,B
as the sets of integer coordinates of particles along the integer line: e.g. in the case of Fig.
2
(a) we have
F=\{2,4,5,6\}
and
B=\{1,1,1,3,4,4,7,7,7\}
. Let
(F,B)\in\mathcal{S}_{n,r}
.
The map
\Phi
acts by moving one particle between
F
and
B
, thus preserving
|F|+|B|=r
and reversing the sign of the weight. It is defined as
\Phi(F,B)=(F^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, where

(a)

If
t_{B}>t_{F}
:

F^{\prime}=F\cup t_{B}
,
B^{\prime}=B\setminus t_{B}
. Since
|B^{\prime}|=|B|-1
,
t_{F^{\prime}}=t_{B}\leq n-|B|+1=n-|B^{\prime}|
and
t_{B^{\prime}}\leq t_{B}\leq n-|B|+1=n-|B^{\prime}|<n-|B^{\prime}|+1
. Therefore,
(F^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{n,r}
, while
w_{F^{\prime},B^{\prime}}=-w_{F,B}
.

(b)

If
t_{B}\leq t_{F}
:

F^{\prime}=F\setminus t_{F}
and
B^{\prime}=B\cup t_{F}
. Then
t_{F^{\prime}}\leq t_{F}-1\leq n-|B|-1=n-|B^{\prime}|
and

t_{B^{\prime}}=t_{F}\leq n-|B|=n-|B^{\prime}|+1
, so that
(F^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{n,r}
, and
w_{F^{\prime},B^{\prime}}=-w_{F,B}
.

The map
\Phi
is clearly an involution. When
r>0
,
\Phi
has no fixed points,
since one can always move a particle. The Lemma follows.
∎

Lemma A.7
.

There is an identity on symmetric functions:

(A.16)

{\hat{e}}_{m}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}{\hat{e}}_{m-j}({y}^{[N-j]})\sum_{k=0}^{j}(-1)^{k}e_{j-k}({\tilde{x}})h_{k}({\tilde{y}}^{[N-j+1]}).

Proof.

We rewrite (
A.16
) using the generating function
{\hat{E}}(z;x)
of (
3.1
) as

(A.17)

{\hat{E}}(z;x)|_{z^{m}}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}{\hat{E}}(z;{y}^{[N-j]})|_{z^{m-j}}F(z;{\tilde{x}},{\tilde{y}}^{[N-j+1]})|_{z^{j}},

where
f(z)|_{z^{m}}
is the coefficient of
z^{m}
in the series expansion of
f(z)
around
0
, and

{\hat{E}}(z;{y}^{[\beta]})=\prod_{i=1}^{\beta}(1+{\tilde{y}}_{i}z+z^{2}),\quad F(z;{\tilde{x}},{\tilde{y}}^{[\beta]})=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{N}1+z\tilde{x}_{i}}{\prod_{i=1}^{\beta}1+z\tilde{y}_{i}}\ .

One can further decompose

\prod_{i=1}^{N-j}(1+z{\tilde{y}}_{i}+z^{2})=(1+z^{2})^{N-j}{\hat{E}}(\frac{z}{1+z^{2}};\tilde{y})=\sum_{k=0}^{N-j}z^{k}\,(1+z^{2})^{N-j-k}\,e_{k}(\tilde{y}^{[N-j]}),

and

\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+z{\tilde{x}}_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{N-j+1}(1+z{\tilde{y}}_{i})}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+z{\tilde{x}}_{i})\sum_{\ell\geq 0}(-1)^{\ell}z^{\ell}h_{\ell}(\tilde{y}^{[N-j+1]}).

The right hand side of (
A.17
) is therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{m}\sum_{k,\ell}(1+z^{2})^{N-j-k}\big|_{z^{m-j-k}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+z{\tilde{x}}_{i})|_{z^{j-\ell}}\,(-1)^{\ell}e_{k}(\tilde{y}^{[N-j]})h_{\ell}({\tilde{y}}^{[N-j+1]})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{m}\sum_{k,\ell\geq 0\atop k+\ell=r}\,\sum_{j=\ell}^{m-k}(1+z^{2})^{N-j-k}\big|_{z^{m-j-k}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+z{\tilde{x}}_{i})|_{z^{j-\ell}}\,(-1)^{\ell}e_{k}(\tilde{y}^{[N-j]})h_{\ell}(\tilde{y}^{[N-j+1]}),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{m-r}(1+z^{2})^{N-j-r}\big|_{z^{m-j-r}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+z{\tilde{x}}_{i})|_{z^{j}}\,\sum_{\ell=0}^{r}\,(-1)^{\ell}e_{r-\ell}(\tilde{y}^{[N-j-\ell]})h_{\ell}(\tilde{y}^{[N-j-\ell+1]}),

where we changed variables
k\mapsto r-\ell
and
j\mapsto j-\ell
.
Finally, using Lemma
A.6
for the collection
u={\tilde{y}}
and
n=N-j
, the expression above drastically simplifies into

\sum_{j=0}^{m}(1+z^{2})^{N-j}\big|_{z^{m-j}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+z{\tilde{x}}_{i})|_{z^{j}}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1+z{\tilde{x}}_{i}+z^{2})\big|_{z^{m}}={\hat{e}}_{m}(x),

which implies (
A.17
).
∎

Definition A.8
.

We define the Koornwinder-Macdonald difference operators
as

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x):=\sum_{j=0}^{m}d_{j}^{(N-m+j)}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{m-j}^{(a,b,c,d)},\qquad\quad(m=1,2,...,N),

in terms of the van Diejen operators of Sect.
A.2.1
, with
d_{j}^{(k)}
as in (
A.11
).

Theorem A.9
.

The Koornwinder-Macdonald polynomials are common eigenfunctions of
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
with eigenvalue
d_{\lambda;m}
defined in (
A.12
):

(A.19)

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=d_{\lambda;m}\,P_{\lambda}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x).

Proof.

Equation (
A.19
) follows from Theorem
A.5
and the relation

(A.20)

d_{\lambda;m}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}d_{j}^{(N-m+j)}\,f_{\lambda;m-j}.

Equation (
A.20
) is obtained by specializing Lemma
A.7
to the variables

x_{i}=\sigma t^{N-i}q^{\lambda_{i}}
and
y_{i}=\sigma t^{N-i}
, and
noting that the prefactors in (
A.12
-
A.13
) amount to an overall factor of
\sigma^{m}t^{m(N-\frac{m+1}{2})}

on both sides of the equation.
∎

A.2.3.
Rains operators

The Rains operator
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)
defined in (
3.7
,
3.9
) has eigenvalues (
3.10
).
This operator is not linearly independent of the K-M operators, as is seen from the following Lemma.

Lemma A.10
.

For generic
(a,b,c,d)
the following relation holds between the Koornwinder-Macdonald operators and the Rains operator:

(A.21)

\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)={\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)+\sum_{m=1}^{N}(-1)^{m}\left(a^{m}b^{m}+\frac{c^{m}d^{m}}{q^{m}}\right)\,t^{m(m-1)/2}\,{\mathcal{D}}_{N-m}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t).

Proof.

This is a consequence of the relation between the eigenvalues:

(A.22)

\hat{d}_{\lambda;N}^{(a,b,c,d)}=d_{\lambda,N}+\sum_{m=1}^{N}(-1)^{m}\left(a^{m}b^{m}+\frac{c^{m}d^{m}}{q^{m}}\right)\,t^{m(m-1)/2}\,d_{\lambda;N-m}.

Using the notation
\sigma=\sqrt{abcd/q}
,
u=ab/\sigma
,
u^{-1}=cd/q\sigma
and

s_{i}=\sigma q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{N-i}
, as well as the expressions (
A.12
) for
d_{\lambda,m}
and (
3.10
) for
\hat{d}_{\lambda;N}
, Equation (
A.22
) is

\displaystyle q^{-|\lambda|}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1-us_{i})(1-u^{-1}s_{i})=\sigma^{N}\,t^{N(N-1)/2}\left\{\hat{e}_{N}(s)+\sum_{m=1}^{N}(-1)^{m}(u^{m}+u^{-m})\hat{e}_{N-m}(s)\right\}

\displaystyle\ \ =\frac{(-1)^{N}\sigma^{N}\,t^{N(N-1)/2}}{u^{N}}\sum_{m=0}^{2N}(-1)^{m}u^{m}\hat{e}_{m}(s)=\frac{(-1)^{N}\sigma^{N}\,t^{N(N-1)/2}}{u^{N}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(1-us_{i})(1-us_{i}^{-1}),

where we have used (
3.1
) in the last line.
The equality follows from
\prod_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}=q^{|\lambda|}\sigma^{N}\,t^{N(N-1)/2}
.
∎

A.2.4.
Koornwinder-Macdonald operators and Macdonald’s operators of Section
A.1

The first Koornwinder-Macdonald operator in Equation of Definition
A.8
is

(A.23)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)+\frac{1-t^{N}}{1-t}\left(1+\frac{abcd}{q}t^{N-1}\right),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)+\frac{1-t^{N}}{1-t}\left(1+\frac{abcd}{q}t^{N-1}\right).

To relate these with some of the operators of Section
A.1
, we have the following Lemma.

Lemma A.11
.

The first order Koornwinder-Macdonald operator can be written as

\qquad{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x;q,t)=\varphi^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\Phi_{i,\epsilon}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon},

where

(A.24)

\displaystyle\varphi^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\frac{\big(1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}a\big)\big(1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}b\big)\big(1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}c\big)\big(1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}d\big)}{2(1-t)(1-q^{-1}t)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1-\frac{\epsilon\,t}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}}{1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}}\,\frac{1-\frac{\epsilon\,t}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}^{-1}}{1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}^{-1}}-t^{N}\right\}.

Proof.

We have:

(A.25)

\varphi^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=\frac{1-t^{N}}{1-t}\left(1+\frac{abcd}{q}t^{N-1}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\Phi_{i,\epsilon}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x).

Using the simple fraction decomposition of the function

(A.26)

\theta(z):=\frac{\big(1-\frac{z}{a}\big)\big(1-\frac{z}{b}\big)\big(1-\frac{z}{c}\big)\big(1-\frac{z}{d}\big)}{\big(1-\frac{z^{2}}{q}\big)\big(1-\frac{z^{2}}{t}\big)}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1-\frac{z}{t}x_{i}}{1-zx_{i}}\,\frac{1-\frac{z}{t}x_{i}^{-1}}{1-zx_{i}^{-1}}.

We find

\theta(z)=\frac{q}{abcd\,t^{2N-1}}+\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{A_{i,\epsilon}}{1-zx_{i}^{\epsilon}}+\frac{B_{\epsilon}}{1-\epsilon\frac{z}{q^{1/2}}}+\frac{C_{\epsilon}}{1-\epsilon\frac{z}{t^{1/2}}}\right\},

where

\displaystyle A_{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-t}{t^{2N}}\,\frac{\big(1-\frac{x_{i}^{-\epsilon}}{a}\big)\big(1-\frac{x_{i}^{-\epsilon}}{b}\big)\big(1-\frac{x_{i}^{-\epsilon}}{c}\big)\big(1-\frac{x_{i}^{-\epsilon}}{d}\big)}{\big(1-\frac{x_{i}^{-2\epsilon}}{q}\big)\big(1-\frac{x_{i}^{-2\epsilon}}{t}\big)}\,\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}-x_{j}}\,\frac{tx_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}-1}{x_{i}^{\epsilon}x_{j}-1},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{q(1-t)}{abcd\,t^{2N-1}}\,\Phi_{i,\epsilon}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x),

and

\displaystyle B_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\big(1-\epsilon\frac{a}{q^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{b}{q^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{c}{q^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{d}{q^{1/2}}\big)}{2\,abcd\,t^{2N-1}\,\big(1-\frac{t}{q}\big)}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1-\frac{\epsilon\,t}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}}{1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}}\,\frac{1-\frac{\epsilon\,t}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}^{-1}}{1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}^{-1}},

\displaystyle C_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q\frac{\big(1-\epsilon\frac{a}{t^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{b}{t^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{c}{t^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{d}{t^{1/2}}\big)}{2\,abcd\,t^{N-1}\,\big(1-\frac{q}{t}\big)}.

By definition (
A.26
),
\theta(0)=1
, therefore

(A.27)

\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Phi_{i,\epsilon}^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)+\frac{abcd\,t^{2N-1}}{q(1-t)}\big(B_{\epsilon}+C_{\epsilon}\big)\right\}=\frac{\frac{abcd}{q}\,t^{2N-1}-1}{1-t}.

Using

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\left\{\frac{t}{2}\big(1-\epsilon\frac{a}{t^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{b}{t^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{c}{t^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{d}{t^{1/2}}\big)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad-\frac{q}{2}\big(1-\epsilon\frac{a}{q^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{b}{q^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{c}{q^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{d}{q^{1/2}}\big)\right\}=\left(1-\frac{q}{t}\right)\left(t-\frac{abcd}{q}\right),

we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\frac{abcd\,t^{2N-1}}{q(1-t)}\,C_{\epsilon}=\frac{t^{N}}{2(1-t)\big(1-\frac{q}{t}\big)}\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\big(1-\epsilon\frac{a}{t^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{b}{t^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{c}{t^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{d}{t^{1/2}}\big),

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{N}-\frac{abcd}{q}t^{N-1}}{1-t}-\frac{t^{N}}{2(1-t)\big(1-\frac{t}{q}\big)}\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\big(1-\epsilon\frac{a}{q^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{b}{q^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{c}{q^{1/2}}\big)\big(1-\epsilon\frac{d}{q^{1/2}}\big).

The Lemma follows by substituting this into (
A.27
), and using the result to reexpress
\varphi^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)

as given by (
A.25
).
∎

When
c=q^{1/2}
and
d=-q^{1/2}
,
\varphi^{(a,b,q^{1/2},-q^{1/2})}(x)=0
. Using Table
1
, we obtain the following.

Corollary A.12
.

In the cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},B_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)}
the first Macdonald operator is equal to the first specialized Koornwinder-Macdonald operator:

(A.28)

{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q,t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\Phi_{i,\epsilon}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}={\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x;q,t).

By direct inspection, the specialized Rains operators of (
3.7
) and (
3.9
) can also be expressed in terms Macdonald’s operators.

Lemma A.13
.

In the cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},C_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}
we have the identifications

(A.29)

\begin{array}[]{ll}D_{N}^{(1)}:&{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}(x;q,t)+{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N-1}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}(x;q,t)={\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(1,-1)}(x;q,t),\\
&\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(1,-1,q^{1/2},-q^{1/2})}=\left({\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(1,-1)}\right)^{2};\\
C_{N}^{(1)}:&{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(C_{N}^{(1)})}(x;q,t)={\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(t^{1/2},-t^{1/2})}(x;q,t),\\
&\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(t^{1/2},-t^{1/2},t^{1/2}q^{1/2},-t^{1/2}q^{1/2})}=\left({\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(t^{1/2},-t^{1/2})}\right)^{2};\\
D_{N+1}^{(2)}:&{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}(x;q,t)={\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(t,-1)}(x;q,t),\\
&\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(t,-1,tq^{1/2},-q^{1/2})}=\left({\mathcal{R}}_{N}^{(t,-1)}\right)^{2}.\end{array}

A.3.
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators and their eigenvalues

For each
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we have chosen a list of
N
linearly independent commuting difference operators (see Def.
3.5
) using the operators in the preceding sections of this Appendix. These have the property that in the
q
-Whittaker limit, they and their time-translates provide solutions of the various quantum Q-systems.
We first explore redundancies between the various definitions to justify Def.
3.5
. Then we describe the eigenvalues of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators, and conclude with a remark on the choice leading to Def.
3.5
.

A.3.1.
Redundancies

Specializing the parameters
(a,b,c,d)
as in Table
1
in the difference operators of Theorem
A.9
gives
a list of
N
commuting difference operators for each
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
As noted in Corollary
A.12
, some of these are Macdonald’s operators listed in Section
A.1
.
In addition, there are non-linear relations
9
9

9

These relations are proved using the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
A.9
, using identities between eigenvalues. Some are a direct consequence of the identifications with Rains operators from Theorem
A.13
.
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N-1}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\alpha}=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}t^{{\alpha}(2{\alpha}+1)}\,{\mathcal{D}}^{(a,b,c,d)}_{N-2{\alpha}-1},

\displaystyle({\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})})^{2}+({\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N-1}}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(a,b,c,d)}+2\sum_{{\alpha}=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor}t^{{\alpha}(2{\alpha}-1)}\,{\mathcal{D}}^{(a,b,c,d)}_{N-2{\alpha}},

for the
D_{N}^{(1)}
specialization.
Similarly, for the
C_{N}^{(1)}
and
D_{N+1}^{(2)}
specializations,

\left({\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{N}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\right)^{2}={\mathcal{D}}_{N}^{(a,b,c,d)}+2\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{N}t^{\frac{{\alpha}({\alpha}+1)}{2}}\,{\mathcal{D}}^{(a,b,c,d)}_{N-{\alpha}}.

In those cases, the Macdonald operators carry more information than the specialization of the Koornwinder-Macdonald operators
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{(a,b,c,d)}
. This justifies the choice in Def.
3.5
.

A.3.2.
The eigenvalues of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators

Definition A.14
.

If
m\leq N_{\mathfrak{g}}
,
let
d_{\lambda,m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
to be the specialization of Equation
A.12
to the parameters corresponding to
{\mathfrak{g}}
in Table
1
:

(A.30)

d_{\lambda,m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}:=d_{\lambda,m}=t^{m(N+\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}-\frac{m+1}{2})}\,\hat{e}_{m}(s),\quad m\in[1,N_{\mathfrak{g}}],

where
s=t^{\rho^{({\mathfrak{g}})}}q^{\lambda}
, i.e.
s_{i}=q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{N-i+\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
Otherwise, define

(A.31)

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)}:&d_{\lambda;\beta}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}=t^{\frac{N(N-1)}{4}}\,{\hat{e}}_{\beta}^{(D_{N})}(s),\qquad\beta=N-1,N,\\
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}:&d_{\lambda;N}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=t^{\frac{N(N+1)}{4}}\,{\hat{e}}_{N}^{(B_{N})}(s),\end{array}

where

\displaystyle{\hat{e}}_{\beta}^{(D_{N})}(x)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon_{1},...,\epsilon_{N}=\pm 1\atop\prod\epsilon_{i}=2(\beta-N)+1}\prod_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}/2}\qquad(\beta=N-1,N),

(A.32)

\displaystyle{\hat{e}}_{N}^{(B_{N})}(x)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1+x_{i}}{\sqrt{x_{i}}}={\hat{e}}_{N-1}^{(D_{N})}(x)+{\hat{e}}_{N}^{(D_{N})}(x).

The functions
\hat{e}_{m}^{(R^{*})}(s)
have the property that their dominant monomial (in powers of
t
) is

s^{\omega_{m}^{*}}
, where
\omega_{m}^{*}
are the fundamental weights of
R^{*}
.

Theorem A.15
.

The
{\mathfrak{g}}
-specialized Koornwinder polynomials are common eigenvectors of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators, satisfying the eigenvalue equation

(A.33)

{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}(x)\,P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=d_{\lambda;m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})},\qquad m\in[1,N],

with

d_{\lambda;m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,\hat{e}_{m}^{(R^{*})}(s)

(
\hat{e}_{m}^{(R^{*})}=\hat{e}_{m}
for
m\leq N_{\mathfrak{g}}
)
and

(A.34)

\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=t^{m(N+\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}-\frac{m+1}{2})},\qquad m\leq N_{\mathfrak{g}},

(A.35)

\begin{array}[]{c}\theta_{\beta}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}=t^{\frac{N(N-1)}{4}},\qquad\beta=N-1,N,\\
\theta_{N}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=t^{\frac{N(N+1)}{4}},\qquad{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}.\end{array}

A.3.3.
Remark about our choice of Macdonald operators

We made the choice of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators for simplicity. A more natural choice is to choose eigenvalues proportional to the fundamental characters of
R^{*}
. This leads to a more complicated choice of the difference operators, but in the
q
-Whittaker limit, they have the same limit as our
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators.

For Macdonald’s operators in Section
A.1
,
the eigenvalue equation is

{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)=\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,s_{\omega_{m}}^{(R^{*})}(s)\,P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),

where the Schur functions
s_{\omega_{m}}^{(R^{*})}
are the fundamental characters of
R^{*}
with highest weight
\omega_{m}^{*}
.
We may choose the set of difference operators
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)
, with
eigenvalues proportional to the fundamental characters of
R^{*}
for all
m
as above.
These are related to
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
via a triangular change of basis, as can be seen from the relation between the fundamental characters and
e_{m}^{(R)}
:

\displaystyle D_{N}:\quad s_{\omega_{m}}^{(D_{N})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{m}^{(D_{N})},\qquad m\in[1,N],

\displaystyle B_{N}:\quad s_{\omega_{m}}^{(C_{N})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{m}-\hat{e}_{m-2},\qquad m\in[1,N],

\displaystyle C_{N}:\quad s_{\omega_{m}}^{(B_{N})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{m}+\hat{e}_{m-1}\qquad m\in[1,N-1],

\displaystyle s_{\omega_{N}}^{(B_{N})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{N}^{(B_{N})}.

Therefore

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
are

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)}:\quad\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}\qquad(m=1,2,...,N),

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},A_{2N}^{(2)}:\quad\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}-(1-\delta_{m,1})\frac{\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}}{\theta_{m-2}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}}\,{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m-2}\quad(m=1,2,...,N),

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}:\quad\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}+\frac{\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}}{\theta_{m-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}}\,{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m-1}\quad(m=1,2,...,N-1),

(A.36)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{N},

with the convention that
{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{0}=1
.
This choice guarantees the following:

Theorem A.16
.

For all
m=1,2,...,N
and all
{\mathfrak{g}}
,

(A.37)

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}\,P_{\lambda}(x)=\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\,s_{\omega_{m}}^{(R^{*})}(s)\,P_{\lambda}(x),\qquad s=q^{\lambda}\,t^{\rho^{({\mathfrak{g}})}}.

A.4.
q
-Whittaker limit of the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald operators

The
q
-Whittaker limit corresponds to sending
t\to\infty
.
The quantity
A^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)
of (
A.10
), which appears in the van Diejen operators (
A.8
), tends to
\sigma=t^{\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}}
under the specialization of Table
1
.
All terms in (
A.8
) have the same leading behavior as that with

s=1
and
J=J_{1}=\{1,2,...,m\}
, namely a sum of three contributions from (
A.9
):

m\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}+m(m-1)+2m(N-m)=m(2N-m-1+\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}).

Therefore,
{\mathcal{V}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\sim(\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{2}\,V_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
, where the difference operator

V_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
is independent of
t
. Similarly
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}\sim(\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{2}\,D_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}

where
D_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
independent of
t
, for
m=1,2,...,N_{\mathfrak{g}}
. By inspection,
we find the same leading behavior for
N_{\mathfrak{g}}<m\leq N
, using (
A.35
). This leads to the definitions:

(A.38)

D_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x):=\lim_{t\to\infty}(\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{-2}\,{\mathcal{D}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x),\ \ V_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}:=\lim_{t\to\infty}(\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{-2}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}

with
\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
as in (
A.34
-
A.35
).

Remark A.17
.

The limit
t\to\infty

of
{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}
is the same as that of
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}
of Sect.
A.3.3
:
Using
{\mathcal{D}}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}\simeq(\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{2}\,{D}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{m}
at leading order in
t
,
the statement follows immediately from (
A.36
) by noting that for
m\leq N_{\mathfrak{g}}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\theta_{m-1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}}{\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\quad(m\geq 1;{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}),

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\theta_{m-2}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}}{\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\quad(m\geq 2;{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},A_{2N}^{(2)}).

For any
{\mathfrak{g}}
, define

\Pi_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-\rho^{*}\cdot\lambda}P_{\lambda}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
.
We refer to these as
q
-Whittaker functions, although they coincide with the usual definition only for the
case of
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)}
and the twisted algebras in Table
1
. The spectral theorem for
D_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
is as follows.

Theorem A.18
.

The functions
\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{\lambda}
are common eigenfunctions of
D_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
, with

D_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}(x)\,\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{\lambda}(x)=\Lambda^{\omega_{m}^{*}}\,\Pi^{({\mathfrak{g}})}_{\lambda}(x).

Proof.

The eigenvalues are the limits

\delta_{\lambda;m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=\lim_{t\to\infty}(\theta_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})})^{-1}{\hat{e}}_{m}^{(R^{*})}(s),\qquad s_{i}=q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{N+\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}-i}.

The limit extracts the leading monomial of the
s
variables in the symmetric function, which is the term involving
s_{1},s_{2},...,s_{m}
with positive powers.
For
m=1,2,...,N_{\mathfrak{g}}
this gives, using (
A.30
) (
A.34
) and (
A.12
):

\delta_{\lambda;m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-m(N+\xi_{g}-\frac{m+1}{2})}{\hat{e}}_{m}(s)=q^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{m}}
.
By inspection, using (
A.31
), (
A.35
) and (
A.14
),

\displaystyle D_{N}^{(1)}:\quad\delta_{\lambda;N}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}=q^{\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{N-1}+\lambda_{N})},\ \delta_{\lambda;N-1}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}=q^{\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{N-1}-\lambda_{N})},

\displaystyle C_{N}^{(1)}:\quad\delta_{\lambda;N}^{(C_{N}^{(1)})}=q^{\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{N-1}+\lambda_{N})},

\displaystyle D_{N+1}^{(2)}:\delta_{\lambda;N}^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}=q^{\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{N-1}+\lambda_{N})}.

The result can be uniformly written as
\Lambda^{\omega_{m}^{*}}=q^{\omega_{m}^{*}\cdot\lambda}
,
\omega_{m}^{*}
the fundamental weights of
R^{*}
.
∎

Remark A.19
.

Using the convention
V_{0}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=1
, Def.
A.8
implies

(A.39)

D_{m}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}V_{j}^{({\mathfrak{g}})},\qquad m\leq N_{\mathfrak{g}}.

Appendix B
Pieri rules

In this section, we list some of the Pieri operators implementing the first Pieri rule for Koornwinder and
{\mathfrak{g}}
-Macdonald polynomials. Their
q
-Whittaker limit gives
q
-difference Toda Hamiltonians for certain root systems.

B.1.
First Pieri rule for Koornwinder polynomials

The first Pieri operator for generic
(a,b,c,d)
is the
q
-difference operator
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s)
of Theorem (
3.3
).

Theorem B.1
.

The first Pieri operator for Koornwinder polynomials is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}(s;q,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{-1}t^{1-N}\varphi^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(s)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\left(\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}\frac{s_{i}-t^{-1}s_{j}}{s_{i}-s_{j}}\frac{qs_{i}-ts_{j}}{qs_{i}-s_{j}}\right)\,T_{i}\right.

\displaystyle+\frac{\prod_{u\in\{a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*}\}}(1-u^{-1}s_{i})(q-us_{i})}{(1-s_{i}^{2})(q-s_{i}^{2})^{2}(q^{2}-s_{i}^{2})}

\displaystyle\times\left.\left(\prod_{j=i+1}^{N}\frac{t^{-1}s_{i}-s_{j}}{s_{i}-s_{j}}\frac{ts_{i}-qs_{j}}{s_{i}-qs_{j}}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{q-ts_{i}s_{j}}{q-s_{i}s_{j}}\frac{1-t^{-1}s_{i}s_{j}}{1-s_{i}s_{j}}\right)T_{i}^{-1}\right],

where

\displaystyle\varphi^{(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\frac{\big(1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}a^{*}\big)\big(1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}b^{*}\big)\big(1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}c^{*}\big)\big(1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}d^{*}\big)}{2(1-t)(1-q^{-1}t)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1-\frac{\epsilon\,t}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}}{1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}}\,\frac{1-\frac{\epsilon\,t}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}^{-1}}{1-\frac{\epsilon}{q^{1/2}}x_{i}^{-1}}-t^{N}\right\},

and
(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})
are the dual Koornwinder parameters (
3.11
).

Proof.

We use the formula (
3.18
) with
(a,b,c,d)\to(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})
. For simplicity, we work with the values
(a,b,c,d)
and interchange them with their duals at the end.
We need the following.

Lemma B.2
.

\displaystyle\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)^{-1}\Gamma_{i}\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{(1-\frac{1}{x_{i}^{2}})(1-\frac{1}{qx_{i}^{2}})}{(1-\frac{1}{ax_{i}})(1-\frac{1}{bx_{i}})(1-\frac{1}{cx_{i}})(1-\frac{1}{dx_{i}})}\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}\frac{1-\frac{qx_{i}}{tx_{j}}}{1-\frac{qx_{i}}{x_{j}}}\prod_{j=i+1}^{N}\frac{1-\frac{x_{j}}{x_{i}}}{1-\frac{x_{j}}{tx_{i}}}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-\frac{1}{x_{i}x_{j}}}{1-\frac{1}{tx_{i}x_{j}}}\,\Gamma_{i},

\displaystyle\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)^{-1}\Gamma_{i}^{-1}\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{(1-\frac{q}{ax_{i}})(1-\frac{q}{bx_{i}})(1-\frac{q}{cx_{i}})(1-\frac{q}{dx_{i}})}{(1-\frac{q}{x_{i}^{2}})(1-\frac{q^{2}}{x_{i}^{2}})}\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}\frac{1-\frac{x_{i}}{x_{j}}}{1-\frac{x_{i}}{tx_{j}}}\prod_{j=i+1}^{N}\frac{1-\frac{qx_{j}}{tx_{i}}}{1-\frac{qx_{j}}{x_{i}}}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-\frac{q}{tx_{i}x_{j}}}{1-\frac{q}{x_{i}x_{j}}}\,\Gamma_{i}^{-1}.

Using Lemma
A.11
, the conjugation of the
{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{(a,b,c,d)}
operator boils down to that of the terms
\Phi_{i,\epsilon}^{(a,b,c,d)}\Gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}
. We have:

\displaystyle\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)^{-1}\Phi_{i,+}^{(a,b,c,d)}\Gamma_{i}\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)=\frac{abcd}{q}t^{2N-2}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}\frac{1-\frac{x_{j}}{tx_{i}}}{1-\frac{x_{j}}{x_{i}}}\frac{1-\frac{qx_{i}}{tx_{j}}}{1-\frac{qx_{i}}{x_{j}}}\right)\Gamma_{i},

\displaystyle\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)^{-1}\Phi_{i,-}^{(a,b,c,d)}\Gamma_{i}^{-1}\Delta^{(a,b,c,d)}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad=t^{2N-2}\frac{(1-\frac{a}{x_{i}})(1-\frac{q}{ax_{i}})(1-\frac{b}{x_{i}})(1-\frac{q}{bx_{i}})(1-\frac{c}{x_{i}})(1-\frac{q}{cx_{i}})(1-\frac{d}{x_{i}})(1-\frac{q}{dx_{i}})}{(1-\frac{1}{x_{i}^{2}})(1-\frac{q}{x_{i}^{2}})^{2}(1-\frac{q^{2}}{x_{i}^{2}})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\times\,\left(\prod_{j=i+1}^{N}\frac{1-\frac{x_{i}}{tx_{j}}}{1-\frac{x_{i}}{x_{j}}}\frac{1-\frac{qx_{j}}{tx_{i}}}{1-\frac{qx_{j}}{x_{i}}}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-\frac{q}{tx_{i}x_{j}}}{1-\frac{q}{x_{i}x_{j}}}\frac{1-\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{t}}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}\right)\Gamma_{i}^{-1}.

We finally use the formula (
3.18
) for
m=1
and substitute the above results with the change

(a,b,c,d)\to(a^{*},b^{*},c^{*},d^{*})
, and the Theorem follows.
∎

B.2.
First Pieri rules for
{\mathfrak{g}}

We may now specialize the result of Theorem
B.1
to
{\mathfrak{g}}
, according to Table
1
. We first treat the cases where
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)}
for which the constant term
\varphi^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}(x)
vanishes
10
10

10

Here we show the explicit dependence on
{\Lambda}_{i}
and
t
(rather than
s_{i}={\Lambda}_{i}\,t^{\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}+N-i}
), as
\xi_{\mathfrak{g}}
varies with
{\mathfrak{g}}
. This makes the limit
t\to\infty
easier to follow.
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(D_{N}^{(1)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\{\prod_{j<i}\frac{t^{i-j-1}{\Lambda}_{j}-{\Lambda}_{i}}{t^{i-j}{\Lambda}_{j}-{\Lambda}_{i}}\frac{t^{i-j+1}{\Lambda}_{j}-q{\Lambda}_{i}}{t^{i-j}{\Lambda}_{j}-q{\Lambda}_{i}}\,T_{i}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\prod_{j>i}\frac{t^{j-i+1}{\Lambda}_{i}-q{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-q{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{t^{j-i-1}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-t^{2N-i-j-1}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}{1-t^{2N-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{t^{2N-i-j+1}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}-q}{t^{2N-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}-q}\,T_{i}^{-1}\right\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(C_{N}^{(1)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\{\prod_{j<i}\frac{t^{j-i+1}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{{\Lambda}_{j}-qt^{j-i-1}{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{j}-qt^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}}\,T_{i}\right.

\displaystyle+\frac{1-t^{N-i}{\Lambda}_{i}}{1-t^{N+1-i}{\Lambda}_{i}}\frac{t^{N+2-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-q}{t^{N+1-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-q}\prod_{j>i}\frac{t^{j-i+1}{\Lambda}_{i}-q{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-q{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{{\Lambda}_{j}-t^{j-i-1}{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{j}-t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left.\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-t^{2N+1-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}{1-t^{2N+2-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{t^{2N+3-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}-q}{t^{2N+2-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}-q}\,T_{i}^{-1}\right\}.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(A_{2N-1}^{(2)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\{\prod_{j<i}\frac{t^{j-i+1}\Lambda_{i}-\Lambda_{j}}{t^{j-i}\Lambda_{i}-\Lambda_{j}}\frac{{\Lambda}_{j}-qt^{j-i-1}{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{j}-qt^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}}\,T_{i}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{t^{2N-2i}\Lambda_{i}^{2}-1}{t^{2N+1-2i}\Lambda_{i}^{2}-1}\frac{t^{2N+2-2i}\Lambda_{i}^{2}-q^{2}}{t^{2N+1-2i}\Lambda_{i}^{2}-q^{2}}\prod_{j>i}\frac{t^{j-i+1}\Lambda_{i}-q\Lambda_{j}}{t^{j-i}\Lambda_{i}-q\Lambda_{j}}\frac{t^{j-i-1}\Lambda_{i}-\Lambda_{j}}{t^{j-i}\Lambda_{i}-\Lambda_{j}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{t^{2N-i-j}\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}-1}{t^{2N+1-i-j}\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}-1}\frac{t^{2N+2-i-j}\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}-q}{t^{2N+1-i-j}\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}-q}\,T_{i}^{-1}\right\}.

We now treat the cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
for which
\varphi^{({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})}
has a non-trivial contribution. We find:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}=G^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}(\Lambda)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\{\prod_{j<i}\frac{t^{j-i+1}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{{\Lambda}_{j}-qt^{j-i-1}{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{j}-qt^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}}\,T_{i}\right.

\displaystyle+\frac{1-t^{2N-2i}{\Lambda}_{i}^{2}}{1-t^{2N+1-2i}{\Lambda}_{i}^{2}}\,\frac{q-t^{2N-2i}{\Lambda}_{i}^{2}}{q-t^{2N+1-2i}{\Lambda}_{i}^{2}}\,\frac{t^{2N+2-2i}{\Lambda}_{i}^{2}-q^{2}}{t^{2N+1-2i}{\Lambda}_{i}^{2}-q^{2}}\,\frac{t^{2N+2-2i}{\Lambda}_{i}^{2}-q}{t^{2N+1-2i}{\Lambda}_{i}^{2}-q}

\displaystyle\times\left.\prod_{j>i}\frac{t^{j-i+1}{\Lambda}_{i}-q{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-q{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{{\Lambda}_{j}-t^{j-i-1}{\Lambda}_{i}}{{\Lambda}_{j}-t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-t^{2N-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}{1-t^{2N+1-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{t^{2N+2-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}-q}{t^{2N+1-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}-q}T_{i}^{-1}\right\},

where

G^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}({\Lambda})=-1+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{t^{N+\frac{3}{2}-i}\Lambda_{i}-\epsilon q^{1/2}}{t^{N+\frac{1}{2}-i}\Lambda_{i}-\epsilon q^{1/2}}\,\frac{t^{N-\frac{1}{2}-i}\Lambda_{i}-\epsilon q^{-1/2}}{t^{N+\frac{1}{2}-i}\Lambda_{i}-\epsilon q^{-1/2}}.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}=G^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}({\Lambda})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\{\prod_{j<i}\frac{t^{j-i+1}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{qt^{j-i-1}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}{qt^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}\,T_{i}\right.

\displaystyle+\frac{1-t^{N-i}\Lambda_{i}}{1-t^{N+1-i}\Lambda_{i}}\frac{q^{1/2}-t^{N-i}\Lambda_{i}}{q^{1/2}-t^{N+1-i}\Lambda_{i}}\frac{q-t^{N+2-i}\Lambda_{i}}{q-t^{N+1-i}\Lambda_{i}}\frac{q^{1/2}-t^{N+2-i}\Lambda_{i}}{q^{1/2}-t^{N+1-i}\Lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle\left.\times\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-t^{2N+1-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}{1-t^{2N+2-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{q-t^{2N+3-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}{q-t^{2N+2-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}\prod_{j>i}\frac{t^{j-i-1}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{t^{j-i+1}{\Lambda}_{i}-q{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-q{\Lambda}_{j}}\,T_{i}^{-1}\right\},

where

G^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}({\Lambda})=\frac{(1-tq^{-1/2})(1+q^{-1/2})}{1-tq^{-1}}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{q^{1/2}-{\Lambda}_{i}t^{N+2-i}}{q^{1/2}-{\Lambda}_{i}t^{N+1-i}}\frac{1-q^{1/2}{\Lambda}_{i}t^{N-i}}{1-q^{1/2}{\Lambda}_{i}t^{N+1-i}}-1\right\},

and finally

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{1}^{(A_{2N}^{(2)})}=G^{(A_{2N}^{(2)})}({\Lambda})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\{\prod_{j<i}\frac{t^{j-i+1}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{qt^{j-i-1}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}{qt^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}\,T_{i}\right.

\displaystyle+\frac{1-t^{N-i}\Lambda_{i}}{1-t^{N+1-i}\Lambda_{i}}\frac{q-t^{2N-2i+1}\Lambda_{i}^{2}}{q-t^{2N+2-2i}\Lambda_{i}^{2}}\frac{q^{2}-t^{2N+3-2i}\Lambda_{i}^{2}}{q^{2}-t^{2N+2-2i}\Lambda_{i}^{2}}\frac{q-t^{N+2-i}\Lambda_{i}}{q-t^{N+1-i}\Lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle\left.\times\prod_{j>i}\frac{t^{j-i-1}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{t^{j-i+1}{\Lambda}_{i}-q{\Lambda}_{j}}{t^{j-i}{\Lambda}_{i}-q{\Lambda}_{j}}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{1-t^{2N+1-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}{1-t^{2N+2-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}\frac{q^{2}-t^{2N+3-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}{q^{2}-t^{2N+2-i-j}{\Lambda}_{i}{\Lambda}_{j}}\,T_{i}^{-1}\right\},

where

\displaystyle G^{(A_{2N}^{(2)})}({\Lambda})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\epsilon=\pm 1}\frac{(1-\epsilon tq^{-1/2})(1+\epsilon q^{-1/2})}{2(1-tq^{-1})}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{q^{1/2}-\epsilon{\Lambda}_{i}t^{N+2-i}}{q^{1/2}-\epsilon{\Lambda}_{i}t^{N+1-i}}\frac{\epsilon-q^{1/2}{\Lambda}_{i}t^{N-i}}{\epsilon-q^{1/2}{\Lambda}_{i}t^{N+1-i}}-1\right\}.

Remark B.3
.

When we consider the
q
-Whittaker limit
t\to\infty
, the constant pieces
G^{({\mathfrak{g}})}({\Lambda})
in the latter three cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},D_{N+1}^{(2)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
provide an extra constant term
in the limiting Hamiltonians
H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
. These read respectively:

\lim_{t\to\infty}G^{(B_{N}^{(1)})}(\Lambda)=\frac{q^{-1}}{{\Lambda}_{N-1}{\Lambda}_{N}}-\frac{1+q^{-1}}{{\Lambda}_{N}^{2}},\ \lim_{t\to\infty}G^{(D_{N+1}^{(2)})}(\Lambda)=-\frac{1+q^{-1/2}}{{\Lambda}_{N}},\ \lim_{t\to\infty}G^{(A_{2N}^{(2)})}(\Lambda)=-\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{N}}.

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
4.10

Here, we provide the proof of Theorem
4.10
, that the time translation operators
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
of Equation (
4.13
)
commute with the limiting first Pieri operator/Toda Hamiltonian
H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
of Equation (
3.46
):

(C.1)

g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}=H_{1}^{({\mathfrak{g}})}g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}.

We divide each Hamiltonian into bulk and boundary pieces, study how
g
commutes with each,
and then sum up the contributions to prove the commutation (
C.1
).

Lemma C.1
.

For all
{\mathfrak{g}}
,

(C.2)

\displaystyle g_{T}\,\left(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-q{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}}\right)T^{-{\alpha}_{a}}g_{T}\quad(a=0,1,...,N-1),

(C.3)

\displaystyle g_{\Lambda}\,\left(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}}\right)T_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-q^{-1}{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a+1}}\right)T_{a+1}\,g_{\Lambda}\quad(a=1,2,...,N-2),

(C.4)

\displaystyle g_{\Lambda}\,\left(1-{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a}}\right)T_{a}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-q^{-1}{\Lambda}^{-{\alpha}_{a-1}}\right)T_{a}^{-1}\,g_{\Lambda}\quad(a=1,2,...,N-1),

with the convention
{\alpha}_{0}=0
.

C.1.
Bulk and boundary terms

Each of the first Pieri operators (
3.46
) is the sum of a bulk and boundary piece, where the bulk piece has the same form for all
{\mathfrak{g}}
:

H_{1}^{[m]}:=\sum_{a=1}^{m}\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{a}}{{\Lambda}_{a-1}}\right)\,T_{a}+\left(1-\frac{{\Lambda}_{a+1}}{{\Lambda}_{a}}\right)\,\frac{1}{T_{a}},

with the convention that
\frac{1}{{\Lambda}_{0}}=0
, for suitable value of
m=m_{\mathfrak{g}}
:
m_{\mathfrak{g}}=N-2
for
{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)},B_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
, and
m_{\mathfrak{g}}=N-1
for all other cases.

The operators
g^{({\mathfrak{g}})}
of (
4.13
) also have some common structures. For

{\mathfrak{g}}=B_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N-1}^{(2)},D_{N+1}^{(2)}
,

g=g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\alpha}g_{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\Lambda}\beta
where
{\alpha},\beta
are functions of
{\Lambda}_{N}
only. For

{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{N}^{(1)}

g=g_{T}g_{\Lambda}\beta
with
\beta
a function of
{\Lambda}_{N-1}{\Lambda}_{N}
only.
The cases
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{N}^{(1)},A_{2N}^{(2)}
are different, and have a
g
operator of the form
g=g_{T}g_{\Lambda}{\alpha}g_{T}g_{\Lambda}\beta
, where
{\alpha},\beta
are functions of
{\Lambda}_{N}
only.

Lemma C.2
.

For all
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
m\leq m_{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
t_{1}=2
for
